\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
Markman's secant sheaves and Schoen's cover constructions provide
algebraic Weil classes in low dimensions and suggest constructions in
higher ones. We prove obstructions to specified finite-cover, quadric,
support and extension constructions on eightfolds. Pure-character
complexes on arbitrary middle-dimensional abelian supports fail
the required invariant second-Ext dimension when their intersections
have codimension at least two. A principal
Gaussian Postnikov network fails the required invariant second-Ext
dimension at every positive integral scale and for every coherent
finite translation--Picard action. Gaussian and Eisenstein reflection
networks have exact positive solutions of all diagonal trace equations;
the full deformation equations remain open. For Mumford fourfolds,
one exceptional detector gives the exceptional plane at cubic degree
cost, and K3-correspondence and sixfold-coniveau criteria isolate
sufficient geometric inputs. For the stated Mumford data, algebraicity
of one specified quadratic Lie block implies the Hodge conjecture for
every power of the fourfold; the half-twist construction does not
provide that block by the stated formal operations.
No positive Gaussian-eightfold algebraicity theorem or uniform
Mumford-family detector construction is claimed. The mathematics and text were
produced by GPT-6 Astra, with separate re-proofs by the same model and
earlier checks by Claude Opus 5.5 at high effort; no human has checked them.
\end{abstract}

\section{Cycle constructions and Mumford's exceptional classes}
\label{sec:introduction-b}
Markman's secant-sheaf construction proves algebraicity of split-sixfold
Weil classes and, with known reductions, the Hodge conjecture for
abelian varieties of dimension at most five
\cite[Theorem~1.2 and Corollary~1.3, pp.~587--588]{MarkmanICM}.
Voisin explains the reductions and their origins in Deligne, Tankeev
and Moonen--Zarhin \cite[Th\'eor\`emes~1.3--1.4,
p.~1248--03; \S2.3]{VoisinB}. The extension to dimension eight
requires additional geometry: a character which remains Hodge is
only one part of a deformation argument.

A Weil $2n$-fold has an imaginary quadratic action of signature $(n,n)$
and a compatible polarization. Its Weil plane is
$W_K=\bigwedge_K^{2n}H^1(A,\Q)$. A detector is an irreducible reduced
subvariety with nonzero projection to the specified exceptional
cohomology. All degrees are intersection numbers with the given
polarization.

\paragraph{Why this matters.}
The uniform-bound criterion of \cite{CompanionA} makes bounded
detectors sufficient for algebraicity throughout a family. This
paper identifies failure mechanisms for specific constructions and
geometric premises that would supply detectors for Mumford's
exceptional classes. A solved trace equation, a character identity
and a Hodge correspondence each require further work before they give
algebraic cycles in a family.

\paragraph{What is new here.}
The explicit obstruction and extraction statements below are the
conclusions developed here. Their inputs include Schoen's cover
construction, van Geemen's quadric mechanism, Markman's
semiregularity strategy, published finite-group classifications,
and K3 structures associated with Mumford varieties.
The companion compares the uniformity criterion with
Cattani--Deligne--Kaplan \cite[p.~483]{CDK},
Bhattacharjee--Bhattacharya
\cite[Lemmas~4.2--4.3 and Theorem~4.5]{BBDense}, and
Br\"unjes--Serp\'e \cite[Theorem~3.4]{BS}.
No priority claim is made.

\subsection{Statements and scope}

The principal hyperbolic Gaussian model in
Section~\ref{sec:principal-graph-postnikov} has sheaves $P,N$
with a nonzero pure Weil character difference. For every integer
$r\geq1$ and every Postnikov class
$e\in\operatorname{Ext}^2(P^{\oplus r},N^{\oplus r})$, the
resulting gluable perfect object has ordinary second self-Ext
dimension at least $374016r^2$. For every coherent finite
translation--Picard action its invariant second self-Ext dimension
is strictly greater than $56$
(Theorems~\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-ordinary} and
\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-all-actions}). The exclusion includes
global translations and degree-zero twists, and the isogeny
pullbacks with the precise compatible descent hypotheses in
Proposition~\ref{prop:principal-postnikov-isogeny-descent}.
It excludes that network rather than arbitrary perfect Weil objects.


Two restricted eightfold constructions admit precise tests. Theorem~\ref{thm:theta-rank} shows that, for $V$ with basis indexed by $\mathbf F_2^4$, every nonzero alternating four-form invariant under translations and character signs induces a pairing on $\bigwedge^2V$ of rank at least $24$, and that $24$ occurs. Corollary~\ref{cor:all-eightfold-quadrics} identifies this $35$-dimensional space with the entire quadratic ideal of the minus projection on a nonempty open subset of the even Gaussian eightfold family. Thus no quadratic relation there can provide the rank-$10$ quadric needed by the direct ruling-class extension of van Geemen's argument. Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-support} proves a different obstruction: a perfect complex supported on translates of abelian subvarieties meeting in codimension at least two cannot deform in a direction with nonzero normal class on a component of nonzero generic virtual multiplicity, even with an infinitesimal gerbe twist. Lemma~\ref{lem:proper-abelian-weil-normal} shows that every
positive-dimensional proper abelian subvariety has a nonzero normal
direction in the full polarized Weil family, without assuming that
the support is stable under the quadratic field.
Theorem~\ref{thm:proper-abelian-support} consequently applies to
arbitrary such abelian supports. For a nonzero full pure Weil
character on middle-dimensional supports,
Corollary~\ref{cor:proper-abelian-pure-weil} gives invariant
second-Ext dimension strictly greater than $56$ on an eightfold,
for every coherent finite translation--Picard action.
This includes arbitrary extensions and thickenings when the
support intersections have codimension at least two.
The general eightfold existence problem remains open.

The fourfold Chern-character test of
\cite[Theorems~B--C and $B_G$]{SecantCharacterTest}
also restricts secant objects. Here is the rank-one form used below.
Let $X$ be an abelian fourfold with a rational ample class $\theta$,
and let $F$ be simple and perfect with no negative self-Ext. Suppose
\[
 \operatorname{ch}(F)=e^B\left(
 1-\frac{\delta\theta^2}{2}+\frac{\delta^2\theta^4}{24}
 +b\left(\theta-\frac{\delta\theta^3}{6}\right)\right),
\]
where $B$ is rational of type $(1,1)$, $\delta>0$ and $b$ are rational.
For every coherent finite translation--tensor action $G$,
Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-one-secant-equivariant} proves
$\dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(F,F)^G\ge\chi(F,F)/|G|+14\ge15$;
strong injectivity would require dimension twelve.
For two such fourfold objects, each generically a line bundle in
degree zero, Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-one-secant-products} gives a
nonzero invariant trace kernel for every finite translation--tensor
action on their external product or any Fourier--Mukai image.
This includes Markman's genus-four ideals and partial-normalization
complexes \cite[Example~8.2.3, pp.~52--54]{MarkmanSecantFourfold}.
The conclusion concerns the full trace, not injectivity only on
the subspace of obstruction classes.


Two other results give exact tests for possible eightfold constructions. Theorem~\compref{thm:pure-weil-ext} applies to a perfect complex $E$ on a balanced Weil $2n$-fold, $n\ge2$, with a rational algebraic $B$-field and an exactly pure nonzero normalized Weil character. For a coherent finite translation--tensor action and an equivariant structure, its ordinary semiregularity trace on invariant obstructions is injective if and only if
\[
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)^G=2n(2n-1).
\]
On an eightfold this number is $56$. Proposition~\ref{prop:sixteen-line-character} constructs an integral sixteen-term character on the principal Gaussian family with precisely that normalized Weil character, but Proposition~\ref{prop:sixteen-line-gluing} proves that every indicated two-level gluing has invariant second self-Ext dimension $448$. Theorem~\ref{thm:all-point-products} excludes every external product of point-ideal characters, for every partition of dimension eight, and all their rational Fourier--Mukai orbits as sources of a nonzero full-family Weil component.


Theorem~\ref{thm:relative-cohesive-effectivity} gives a restricted
relative analytic effectivity result. On a constant-Beltrami family
of compact complex tori, a compatible formal flat superconnection
on one fixed bounded smooth bundle can be replaced, to any finite
jet after a formal gauge, by a convergent analytic perfect object.
It also treats an already specified strict coherent finite action.
Theorem~\ref{thm:formal-perfect-cohesive} constructs the required
fixed formal model from any supplied compatible tower of untwisted
perfect objects, preserving its derived-category transition maps.
Corollary~\ref{cor:formal-perfect-analytic-jet} therefore realizes
any prescribed finite jet of such a tower analytically. Coherent
derived actions, torsion twists, and the production of the compatible
lifts from semiregularity remain separate requirements.

For finite covers of curves, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-cover-rank} says that a faithful irreducible coefficient representation with nonrational character, eight-dimensional parabolic multiplicity of type $(4,4)$, and branch moving ranks at least two needs coefficient rank at least twenty-six to have a sixteen-dimensional period image. Theorem~\ref{thm:gaussian-reflections} excludes dominance when
the complex character field contains $\Q(i)$ and the image contains
a reflection, without a field-of-definition hypothesis.
Corollary~\ref{cor:gaussian-cover-rank} applies this to every
inducing representation: each has rank at least seventy-eight and has
a genus-zero intermediate base. An imprimitive source therefore
has rank at least one hundred fifty-six. The uniform bound is proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:gaussian-rank34} using Zalesski's primitive-group
structure theorem. Proposition~\ref{prop:permuted-tensor100}
proves that a preserved tensor power $(\C^a)^{\otimes t}$, $t\geq2$,
even with permuted factors, requires $a\geq10$ and total rank at
least one hundred. Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-small-factors78} gives total ranks
at least $112,123,140,155$ when an ordered factor has dimension
$2,3,4,5$, respectively, and at least seventy-eight for any
nontrivial ordered tensor decomposition. Proposition~\ref{prop:primitive-ordered100}
strengthens the ordered bound to one hundred for primitive
coefficients over any character field, and hence for all Gaussian
coefficients by the preceding imprimitive bound. Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks} further excludes scalar-standard coefficients in every degree: their period variations factor through rank-one local systems on curves with moduli dimension at most fifteen. These statements restrict construction methods and prove no additional case of algebraicity.


Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-hodge-bundle} gives simple vector bundles on
every CM elliptic product $E^{2n}$, for every imaginary quadratic
field, whose Chern character is a nonzero prescribed rational primitive
middle Hodge class plus an explicit polynomial in the polarization.
For a Weil class and $n\ge3$, the action of its character from
$HH^2(A)$ to $HH_{-2}(A)$ has rank
$7n^2-2n$, equal to $104$ on an eightfold; the general rank formula
is \cite[Theorem~A6]{BBK3Sixfolds}.
The construction uses positive multiplicities and ordinary extensions.
Theorem~\ref{thm:tree-extension-obstruction} proves that its specified
tree of permutation orbits cannot deform in every Weil direction,
even after its filtration is forgotten.  Additional gluing data and
the sufficient second-Ext equality remain to be found.
Theorem~\ref{thm:sign-cube-obstruction-strengthened} extends the obstruction, starting in dimension four, to a
specified family of coordinate-sign networks with many cycles.
Theorem~\ref{thm:gaussian-positive-trace-network} gives a different,
explicit Gaussian reflection network whose positive multiplicities
have a nonzero Weil character and solve every matrix-valued diagonal
trace equation.  The remaining off-diagonal extension equations and
strong semiregularity are not established, so this construction does
not yet give a deforming detector.

For a polarized Hodge-generic Mumford fourfold $A$, the exceptional
codimension-two classes on $A^2$ form a plane identified with the
trace-zero part of its defining totally real cubic field. Theorem
\ref{thm:mumford-cubic} proves
$\delta_{\mathrm{exc}}\leq c_t\leq C_t\delta_{\mathrm{exc}}^3$
for each fixed nonzero class $t$ in that plane, where $c_t$ bounds the positive multiplier and the degrees of both
effective parts of an integral cycle representing that multiple of $t$, and
$\delta_{\mathrm{exc}}$ is the least degree of an irreducible
codimension-two detector for the product polarization. The constants
are uniform in a fixed polarized family with global $t$.
Corollary~\ref{cor:mumford-family} therefore reduces algebraicity
throughout that family to bounded detectors on infinitely many CM
fibres. These exceptional classes are not Weil classes; the theorem
does not construct their first detector.

When $A$ is the Jacobian of one of the genus-four curves of Bouchet--Hanselman--Pieper--Schiavone, Theorem~\ref{thm:mumford-surface} gives a further sufficient condition. Twist the polarization on its rank-nine K3-type Hodge structure by a totally positive $\lambda\in F\setminus\Q^\times F^{\times2}$, and realize it by a projective K3 surface $S_\lambda$. One algebraic surface correspondence from $S_\lambda$ to $C\times C$ with nonzero transcendental action makes the entire exceptional plane algebraic. The extraction uses a nonconstant weighted Casimir tensor. A dominant rational map from $C\times C$ to this chosen $S_\lambda$ cannot provide it. The theorem refines the conditional K3 approach in their Section~5.2; it does not construct the first correspondence.

A different conditional reduction is Theorem~\ref{thm:mumford-coniveau}. Suppose the quaternion algebra over $F$ is split by $F(\sqrt{-3})$ and has split corestriction to $\Q$. On a common finite cover of the Mumford curve there is a split Weil sixfold $B$ and an explicitly algebraic projector onto a rank-sixteen substructure $P\subset H^3(B,\Q)$ with $P(1)\simeq H^1(A,\Q)^{\oplus2}$. At a Hodge-generic point, one nonzero geometrically coniveau-one part of $P$ implies the rational Hodge conjecture for every power of $A$, including the exceptional plane on $A^2$. The known Weil cycles on $B$ give $P$ as a summand of the middle cohomology of supporting threefolds; the additional divisor support remains an open generalized Hodge question.


Theorem~\ref{eisenstein-trace-thm:main} extends the trace construction
to $E_\omega^8$. Its reflection group is
$G(6,6,4)^2$, the weights lie between $1$ and $1251$, and its
character has a nonzero Weil component. The precise transfer acts on
four Laurent modes in the trace columns; it is a rational change of
coordinates, not an embedding of the Gaussian coefficient field into
the Eisenstein one. This solves the same diagonal equations and
leaves the full deformation problem open.

For the Mumford data split by $F(\sqrt{-3})$,
Theorem~\ref{clifford-thm:quadratic-all-powers} proves that an algebraic
quadratic Lie block from a rank-twelve K3 structure implies the rational
Hodge conjecture for every power of $A$, including the entire
exceptional plane on $A^2$. Lemma~\ref{clifford-lem:k3-realization} realizes the required
rank-twelve K3 surface for every stated Mumford datum. Algebraicity
of the specified Clifford map is sufficient, and
Corollary~\ref{clifford-cor:published-input} records the all-powers
consequence of the published canonical statement. Proposition~\ref{clifford-prop:half-twist-obstruction}
shows why the displayed half-twist embedding does not supply that
block by formal endomorphism and commutator operations: its derived
constituents do not contain the Mumford representation. Published
claims of the full Kuga--Satake conjecture and this explicit map
comparison are stated separately in that section.

The scalar-standard construction is excluded in every degree, the
two-level character construction includes its simple members, and the
support theorem includes thickenings and extensions on the indicated
supports. None of these statements classifies all algebraic cycles.
The positive eightfold input is the Eisenstein Hurwitz family
suggested by Severn \cite{Severn}; the companion article proves its
dominance from the explicit reflection data and published degeneration
theorems, and proves the required cycle transfer.  No corresponding
Gaussian family is constructed here.
References prefixed A denote numbered statements of \cite{CompanionA}.

The separate OpenAI manuscript on split eightfolds was withdrawn on
6 October 2026 because its stabilization-trace cancellation has a sign
error; it is not an input here \cite{OpenAIEightWithdrawn}.

\section{Proof overview}\label{sec:overview-b}
For curve covers, Riemann--Hurwitz bounds the branch parameters and
the classification of small representations controls moving ranks.
Induction treats imprimitive representations before primitive
classification is applied: a complex block need not descend to the
character field. A sixteen-dimensional target forces the coefficient
rank threshold. For an ordered factor of dimension two through
five, the branch budget forces a reflection in the other factor
below the stated thresholds. Complex induction and the reflection
classification reduce that factor to a deleted permutation
representation. Seven spectral estimates bound the moving ranks
of the remaining small factor. Compact Shapiro then puts the
multiplicity inside a curve variation with at most fifteen moduli,
fewer than the sixteen required. For primitive ordered coefficients
below rank one hundred, the same reflection reduction leaves eleven
dimension pairs with small factor dimensions six through nine.
A uniform cycle-eigenspace estimate, together with the nonnegative
compact cohomology of the actual small-factor representation,
excludes those pairs. The Gaussian consequence also uses the
independent lower bound for imprimitive coefficients.


For the restricted analytic effectivity theorem, the Dolbeault
Green homotopy compensates for the first-order change of symbol.
A holomorphic Banach implicit-function theorem gives a slice with
a finite-dimensional analytic obstruction map. The Bianchi
identity shows that its zero set is the full flatness locus.
Formal gauge fixing and Artin approximation then provide a
convergent solution, and uniform elliptic estimates give joint
smoothness. The resulting cohesive module is locally perfect
on the total analytic family.
For the formal comparison, a finite nilpotent coordinate change
identifies the varying local Dolbeault algebra. Finite cohesive
replacement gives a model at each Artin level. The curvature and
intertwining defects of a chosen lift form a cocycle in an acyclic
mapping cone. Correcting both together keeps the original smooth
bundle and the specified transition maps at every level.

For theta quadrics, translation and character-sign invariance identify
the relation space with invariant alternating four-forms. A weight
decomposition gives minimum rank $24$. Comparison with the
multiplication map using theta-null nonvanishing and first derivatives near the product locus proves that all relations are
accounted for on a nonempty open subset. That qualification is essential.

The two-level gluing calculation uses the self-Ext spectral sequence,
not just the alternating character. The terms which could remove the
excess diagonal obstruction space vanish. Positive ordinary bundles
lead instead to weighted incidence equations. Trees and the specified
coordinate-sign networks have too few flow directions. The reflection
network supplies an exact rational solution of all diagonal equations;
two-edge products and the upper equation remain independent conditions.

The support theorem localizes the categorical trace at one component.
Intersections in codimension at least two cannot cancel its normal
class; nonzero generic virtual multiplicity makes that trace nonzero.
Every proper abelian subvariety has some such normal direction
in the full polarized Weil family. Indeed, if its real homology
subspace were preserved by every first variation, the noncompact
unitary tangent directions and their brackets would preserve it.
They generate the special unitary Lie algebra, whose standard real
representation is irreducible in the dimensions at issue. This
explains the use of the full family, the restriction on support
intersections, and the remaining possibility of other supports.

For rank-one secants, the self-Euler characteristic is positive,
while the characteristic action has ranks eight and twelve in
degrees one and two. Fourfold Serre duality therefore forces too
large a second self-Ext space. Determinant normalization makes every
finite stabilizer a coherent free translation action, so the same
calculation applies on its quotient. Uniqueness of determinant-one
isomorphisms also supplies compatible factor actions for an arbitrary
finite subgroup acting on an external product.

Mumford extraction uses the three embeddings of the totally real
cubic field to obtain the exceptional plane from one detector through
algebraic contractions. Solving a rational linear system gives the
cubic degree cost. A nonsquare polarization twist on the K3 structure
then yields a nonconstant weighted Casimir tensor. An algebraic
correspondence detecting that tensor would supply the exceptional
plane. In the sixfold criterion an algebraic projector identifies a
rank-sixteen summand; divisor support for it is the missing geometric
premise. Its Hodge-theoretic identification alone does not give support.

For the Eisenstein transfer, an individual trace column has Laurent
exponents $-1,0,1,2$. Evaluation at fourth or sixth roots spans the
same rational coefficient space. A four-by-four rational matrix
transfers the finite certificate; root oddness and the Specht-module
relations reconstruct the flow with the correct edge orientations.
The scalar stabilizer is smaller in the Eisenstein group, so it is
checked separately. The Clifford criterion uses a different mechanism:
the commutator of two specified Clifford maps supplies the Lie action,
and a cubic-field-weighted Casimir gives a nonconstant exceptional
class. A rational Hermitian embedding and density in the unitary group
realize the prescribed rank-twelve K3 structure in the required
order-three family. The remaining hypothesis is algebraicity of the
particular quadratic block. Products of at most three Lie operators span
$\operatorname{End}H^1(A)$; their algebraic correspondences transfer
Hodge cycles from powers of the K3 surface to every power of $A$.
The canonical statement of Bolognesi--Laterveer is a sufficient input,
as recorded in Corollary~\ref{clifford-cor:published-input}.

\section{Degree costs in the CM constructions}
\label{sec:cm-cost}
This section identifies the part of the recent CM arguments that still needs geometric control to imply uniformity.

For fixed Galois CM field and fixed codimension, the finitely many CM types and four-factor relations can be treated at common auxiliary levels. In OpenAI's construction this is explicit in Corollary~6.7 \cite{OpenAICM}. Its Lemma~2.2 and Proposition~3.1, and van Hoften--Sempliner's Lemma~2.3.2 and Theorem~2.3.4 \cite{vHS}, then turn rational Hodge maps into morphisms by clearing lattice denominators and transport surface classes. These are pointwise constructions. The following calculation isolates a cost which remains even when the auxiliary data have been fixed.

\begin{proposition}[The degree of a transported support]\label{prop:transport}
Let $f:(A_0,L_0)\to(A,L)$ be an isogeny of complex polarized abelian varieties with $f^*c_1(L)=q\,c_1(L_0)$, where $q$ is a positive integer. For an integral $r$-dimensional subvariety $Z\subset A_0$,
\[
 \deg_L f(Z)=
 \frac{q^r\deg_{L_0}Z}
 {|\ker f\cap\operatorname{Stab}(Z)|}.
\]
If $\operatorname{Stab}(Z)$ is finite, this is at least
$q^r\deg_{L_0}Z/|\operatorname{Stab}(Z)|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The restriction $Z\to f(Z)$ has generic degree
$e=|\ker f\cap\operatorname{Stab}(Z)|$. Indeed a translate by a kernel element not stabilizing $Z$ meets it in a proper closed subset; remove the finitely many such intersections. Over what remains, the points identified by $f$ are exactly the stabilizer translates. Projection formula gives
$e\deg_L f(Z)=\int_Zf^*c_1(L)^r=q^r\deg_{L_0}Z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A limitation of a fixed collection of cycles]\label{cor:fixed-catalog}
Fix finitely many positive-dimensional integral subvarieties $Z_j\subset A_0$ of the same dimension $r$, each with finite stabilizer. Allow their images under polarized isogenies to a varying target, translations of those images, rational coefficients, and cancellation of equal components. Every nonzero resulting cycle, when written with an integral numerator, has a surviving component of degree at least
\[
 c\,q_{\min}(A)^r,\qquad
 c=\min_j\frac{\deg_{L_0}Z_j}{|\operatorname{Stab}(Z_j)|}>0,
\]
where $q_{\min}(A)$ is the smallest allowed polarization multiplier. Such a construction alone cannot give a uniform numerator-degree bound on a dense orbit in a positive-dimensional polarized family.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Every support has the asserted lower bound by Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}. Cancellation removes supports; it does not create supports of smaller degree. A surviving support has nonzero integer multiplicity in the numerator. Dividing by a rational denominator does not change that support degree.

For bounded $q$, only finitely many polarized targets occur. The degree of the isogeny is bounded by $q^{\dim A_0}$ times the fixed ratio of polarization volumes. There are finitely many subgroups of $A_0$ of bounded finite order, hence finitely many kernels and quotients. For each $q$ and quotient map, the target polarization is determined, because pullback on rational N\'eron--Severi groups is injective. Fixed finite level data add only finitely many choices. A dense orbit in a positive-dimensional family is infinite, so $q_{\min}$ is unbounded on it.
\end{proof}

This is a restriction on the stated method of constructing cycles. It is not a lower bound on the least degree among all representatives of their cohomology classes. The same transport issue is discussed in \cite{BBAlgebraic}. The fourfold constructions show why a different representative can be decisive.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:surface-stabilizer}
If an integral surface $Z$ in a complex abelian variety has a positive-dimensional connected translation stabilizer, its cohomology class belongs to the algebra generated by divisor classes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $B$ be that connected stabilizer and $\pi:A\to A/B$ the quotient. If $\dim B=2$, then $Z$ is a translate of $B$ and is the inverse image of a point; the point class is a rational multiple of a top power of an ample divisor. If $\dim B=1$, then $Z=\pi^{-1}(C)$ for a curve $C$ in the quotient. On a quotient of dimension $d\geq2$, hard Lefschetz identifies $H^2$ with $H^{2d-2}$ by multiplication by an ample class to the power $d-2$. The preimage of $[C]$ has type $(1,1)$, so is a rational divisor class by the Lefschetz theorem. Pullback expresses $[Z]$ in the divisor algebra. If $d=1$, the curve is the whole quotient and the assertion is immediate.
\end{proof}

In particular, a surface detecting a class orthogonal to the divisor algebra in complementary degree must have finite stabilizer. For such surfaces the support-degree cost above cannot be removed by an unbounded stabilizer.

\section{Restrictions on finite-cover eightfold constructions}
\label{sec:finite-cover-eightfolds}
This section bounds the period images available from low-rank finite coefficient systems on curves. It restricts a method for constructing detectors; it gives no lower bound for the degrees of arbitrary cycles.

The reflection classification rests on Cohen's published classification \cite[(2.4)--(2.7), pp.~386--390; (4.16), p.~412; (5.11)--(5.14), pp.~432--435]{CohenReflection}, with its correction \cite[p.~613]{CohenCorrection}, and Malle's character-field tables \cite[Tables~8.1--8.2, pp.~674--675; p.~676]{MalleFields}. A recent unrefereed treatment by lelong\_ridge and cm\_symmetry\_scout \cite[Theorems~A--B and \S\S2,5]{ReflectionSources} classifies the primitive reflection possibilities for eightfold images of dimension at least fifteen and proves a primitive reflection-free coefficient-rank bound of twenty-three for that threshold. That preprint is prior context; its proof is not an input to the classification step below. The two statements below use the sixteen-dimensional threshold for dominance. We give the induction and dimension arguments explicitly, including the case in which a complex block has no descent to the original character field.

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian character fields and reflections]
\label{thm:gaussian-reflections}
Let $V$ be a nontrivial irreducible complex representation of a
finite group, and suppose that its character field contains
$\Q(i)$.  Consider a family of finite covers of smooth complex
curves whose $V$-multiplicity in first cohomology has dimension
eight and Hodge numbers $(4,4)$.  If the faithful coefficient
image contains a complex reflection, the corresponding period
image has complex dimension at most fifteen.
No realization of $V$ over $\Q(i)$ is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
All coefficient groups below are replaced by their faithful
images, and points with identity coefficient inertia are filled
in.  For a nontrivial irreducible finite coefficient system $U$
of rank $r$ on a genus-$\gamma$ curve, write $b$ for its number
of genuine branch points and
$c_j=\operatorname{rank}(g_j-1)\geq1$ for their moving ranks.
The middle-extension Euler formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:curve-euler}
 8=2r(\gamma-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{b}c_j.
\end{equation}
For fixed finite monodromy data, the covers have finite fibers
over the moduli of the pointed base.  Thus, for $r\geq2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:curve-dimension}
 \dim(\text{period image})
 \leq 3\gamma-3+b
 \leq 8+(3-2r)(\gamma-1).
\end{equation}
Positive genus gives at most eight; genus zero gives at most
$2r+5$.  For rank one the same calculation instead gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-one-curve-dimension}
 8=2\gamma-2+b,\qquad
 3\gamma-3+b=\gamma+7\leq12.
\end{equation}
This includes the unramified case.  Finite monodromy contributes
only discrete data over the pointed curve.  These middle-extension
conventions agree with
\cite[Proposition~1.1, Lemma~1.2, Remark~1.3 and
Theorem~2.5]{DettweilerWewers}.

We will use induction over $\C$, without a field-of-definition
assumption.  If $V=\operatorname{Ind}_H^G U$ and $C$ is the
covering curve, Frobenius reciprocity gives
\[
 \operatorname{Hom}_G(V,H^1(C,\C))
 \simeq\operatorname{Hom}_H(U,H^1(C,\C)).
\]
The latter is compact multiplicity cohomology on $C/H$.
It depends only on $C/\ker U\to C/H$.  Finite pushforward and
taking isotypic parts commute with middle extension: the stalks
are the same inertia-invariant spaces.  The isomorphism respects
the Hodge filtration and Gauss--Manin connection, since it is
induced by finite covering correspondences and deck transformations.
Consequently forgetting the other cover data does not increase
the period image.  Moreover, the induced-character formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:induced-character-field-inclusion}
 \Q(\chi_V)\subseteq\Q(\chi_U).
\end{equation}
These statements do not assert that a complex block or its
polarization descends to the original character field.

Average a positive Hermitian form over the original coefficient
group.  Join two reflection root lines when they are not
orthogonal.  The connected components span mutually orthogonal
subspaces whose sum is nonzero and invariant, hence all of $V$.
The group permutes these subspaces transitively.  A block
stabilizer therefore induces $V$ from its irreducible action $U$
on one block.  The reflection subgroup on that block is
irreducible.  By \eqref{eq:induced-character-field-inclusion},
$\Q(\chi_U)$ still contains $\Q(i)$.
It suffices to bound the period variation for $U$.

Let $H$ now denote the faithful block group and let $R$ be the
subgroup generated by all reflections in $H$.  It contains the
irreducible reflection subgroup just constructed, so $R$ is
irreducible and normal in $H$.  Ranks at most five have period
dimension at most fifteen by the preceding estimates.  We may
therefore assume $r\geq6$, a genus-zero base, and a generating
product-one branch tuple in $H$.

The classification of finite irreducible reflection groups gives
the following possibilities in these ranks: the imprimitive
groups $G(m,p,r)$, the real groups $W(A_r)$,
$W(E_6),W(E_7),W(E_8)$, and $G_{34}$ of rank six.
Here the real types $B_r,D_r$ are in the imprimitive list.
For the nonreal primitive list see
\cite[(4.16), p.~412; (5.12), p.~435]{CohenReflection}.
Only the stated reflection classification is used; no
classification of arbitrary finite primitive linear groups is
being asserted.

If $R=G(m,p,r)$, it has a unique system of coordinate lines:
the exceptions in \cite[Lemma~(2.7), pp.~389--390]{CohenReflection}
all have rank at most four.  Every normalizer of $R$, hence $H$,
preserves this system.  Thus $U$ is induced from a finite
rank-one character of a line stabilizer.  Compact Shapiro and
\eqref{eq:rank-one-curve-dimension} bound its period image by
twelve.  No coordinate line is assumed to descend to $\Q(i)$.

Suppose next that $R=W(A_r)$ or one of the three real exceptional
groups.  In a rational root model, every complex normalizer
$h$ has the form $h=cA$ with $A\in\operatorname{GL}_r(\Q)$:
the rational intertwining equations for the automorphism of $R$
induced by $h$ have a one-dimensional solution space by Schur's
lemma.  The matrix $A$ is a rational similarity for the invariant
positive form and permutes root lines.  All roots have the same
length.  It sends one rational root to a rational multiple of
another, so its similarity factor is a rational square.
After rescaling, it is an orthogonal automorphism of the root
system.  A root-system automorphism is a Weyl element followed
by a Dynkin-diagram automorphism.  In types $A_r$ and $E_6$ the
extra diagram involution is $-w_0$; types $E_7,E_8$ have none.
It follows that
\[
 H\subseteq\C^*R.
\]
The scalar factors can be chosen to be roots of unity, since
both $h$ and the root-system automorphism have finite order.

In type $A_r$, the scalar-standard theorem
(Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks}, proved independently
below) bounds the period image by fifteen in every rank and for
every finite scalar character.  Its proof does not use the
present reflection theorem or the coefficient-rank bound.

For $R=W(E_r)$, $r=6,7,8$, write the branch entries as
$g_j=\lambda_j w_j$ with $w_j\in R$ real orthogonal.
If every $\lambda_j$ were real, the generated group would have
real character field, contrary to $\Q(i)\subseteq\Q(\chi_U)$.
The product-one relation implies
$\prod_j\lambda_j\in\{1,-1\}$, since the scalar matrices
in the real root group are contained in $\{I,-I\}$.
Thus at least two of the $\lambda_j$ are nonreal.
For such an entry, the eigenspace of $w_j$ for
$\lambda_j^{-1}$ has dimension at most $\lfloor r/2\rfloor$,
because nonreal eigenvalues of a real matrix occur in conjugate
pairs with equal multiplicities.  Each of these two entries has
moving rank at least $\lceil r/2\rceil$, while every remaining
genuine entry has moving rank at least one.  Therefore
\[
 b\leq2r+10-2\lceil r/2\rceil.
\]
The corresponding bounds on $b-3$ are $13,13,15$.

Finally let $R=G_{34}$.  Its reflection character field is
$E=\Q(\sqrt{-3})$,
\cite[Table~8.2, p.~675; the $G_{34}$ discussion on p.~661]{MalleFields}.
If the period image had dimension at least sixteen, there would
be at least nineteen genuine branches, whereas
\eqref{eq:curve-euler} gives $\sum c_j=20$.
At most one branch entry could then fail to be a reflection.
All the other entries belong to $R$, and the product-one
relation puts the remaining entry in $R$ as well.
The tuple generates $H$, so $H=R$.  This contradicts
$\Q(i)\subseteq\Q(\chi_U)=E$.
This argument needs no classification of the normalizer of
$G_{34}$, and is unaffected by the correction to
\cite[(5.14)]{CohenReflection} in \cite{CohenCorrection}.

All possibilities have period image of dimension at most
fifteen, proving the theorem.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[A coefficient-rank threshold for dominance]\label{thm:finite-cover-rank}
Let $V$ be a faithful irreducible complex representation of a finite group $G$, with nonrational character. Consider a Hurwitz family of $G$-covers whose $V$-multiplicity in first cohomology has complex dimension eight and Hodge numbers $(4,4)$. Suppose that every genuine branch monodromy has moving rank at least two. If its period image has dimension sixteen, then
\[
 \dim_{\C}V\ge26.
\]
The hypothesis concerns the branch monodromies; the group may contain reflections absent from their tuple. No descent of a complex imprimitivity system to a number field is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first justify induction over $\C$, since it is needed even when the original coefficient representation has a smaller field of definition. If $V=\operatorname{Ind}_H^G W$ and $C$ is the covering curve, Frobenius reciprocity gives
\[
 \operatorname{Hom}_G(V,H^1(C,\C))
 \simeq\operatorname{Hom}_H(W,H^1(C,\C)).
\]
This is an isomorphism of variations of complex Hodge structures. To see the extension across the branch points, put $X=C/H$ and take the $W$-multiplicity in the finite pushforward of the constant sheaf from $C$ to $X$. A stalk at a branch point is the corresponding inertia-invariant space. Finite proper pushforward has no higher direct images and taking a finite-group isotypic part is exact, so this is the middle extension of the coefficient local system on the punctured curve. The maps on cohomology commute with the Hodge decomposition, since they come from finite sums of deck transformations.

The inducing representation $W$ is irreducible: otherwise induction of a proper subrepresentation would contradict irreducibility of $V$. It is nontrivial, since induction of the trivial representation contains constants. Its variation depends only on the finite cover $C/\ker(W)\to X$. If $r=\dim W$, $\gamma=g(X)$ and $b$ is its number of genuine coefficient branch points, the Euler formula is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:complex-cover-budget}
 8=2r(\gamma-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{b}c_j,
 \qquad c_j=r-\dim W^{g_j}\ge1.
\end{equation}
For fixed finite monodromy data the covers have finite fibres over the moduli of the marked base curve: its fundamental group has finitely many homomorphisms to the fixed finite group. Consequently the period-image dimension is at most $3\gamma-3+b$. For $r=1$ this is $\gamma+7\le12$. For $r\ge2$ it is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:complex-shapiro-bound}
 2r+5+(3-2r)\gamma\le2r+5.
\end{equation}
There is no unstable unmarked genus-one exception, because such a nontrivial finite system cannot have eight-dimensional cohomology. On a simply connected parameter neighborhood the Shapiro isomorphism is constant on the marked local systems. The filtration at one complex embedding determines the unitary period point, with its conjugate filtration determined by it. Thus forgetting the additional cover data does bound the original period image by \eqref{eq:complex-shapiro-bound}, without asserting number-field descent of $W$ or of a polarization on an intermediate abelian variety.

Assume for a contradiction that $d=\dim V\le24$ and the image has dimension sixteen. Rank one has already been excluded. For $d\ge2$ a positive-genus base gives image dimension at most eight, so the base is the sphere. Erase identity branch monodromies. The resulting product-one generating tuple has $b\ge19$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank25-budget}
 2d+8=\sum_{j=1}^{b}c_j\ge2b\ge38.
\end{equation}
Thus $15\le d\le24$. Some entry has moving rank two, because otherwise the sum would be at least $3b\ge57$, whereas $2d+8\le56$.

Suppose first that $G$ is imprimitive, with $t\ge2$ blocks of common dimension $r$ and $d=rt$. A block stabilizer gives an inducing representation of dimension $r$. Equation~\eqref{eq:complex-shapiro-bound} excludes $r\le5$, so $r\ge6$. Let $\sigma_j\in S_t$ be the permutation of blocks induced by the $j$th branch entry, and put $I_j=t-\#\{\text{cycles of }\sigma_j\}$. An operator cycling $\ell$ blocks has fixed space of dimension at most $r$, since a fixed vector is determined by its entry in one block. Hence $c_j\ge rI_j$. The block monodromy is transitive, and Riemann--Hurwitz for its degree-$t$ cover of the sphere gives
\[
 \sum_j I_j=2(t-1)+2\gamma'\ge2(t-1).
\]
If $J=\{j:I_j>0\}$, then $|J|\le\sum I_j$, while the other branch entries have rank at least two. Therefore
\[
 \sum c_j\ge2(b-|J|)+r\sum I_j
       \ge38+2(r-2)(t-1).
\]
Together with $d=rt$ and \eqref{eq:rank25-budget}, this implies $r+2t\ge17$. But $rt\le24$, $r\ge6$ and $t\ge2$ permit only $t=2,3,4$, for which the respective maxima of $r+2t$ are $16,14,14$. This contradiction excludes imprimitivity.

Next suppose that a tensor decomposition $V=A\otimes B$ is preserved, with $2\le a=\dim A\le s=\dim B$. For a finite-order operator $x\otimes y$, the two factors are diagonalizable up to a scalar: suitable powers of each are scalar. If $x$ is nonscalar, its eigenspaces have dimensions at most $a-1$, so the fixed space of $x\otimes y$ has dimension at most $(a-1)s$. Its moving rank is at least $s$. Since $d\ge15$, we have $s>2$, so a moving-rank-two element must have scalar $x$. Its remaining moving rank is a positive multiple of $a$, forcing $a=2$. Thus $d=2s$, with $s\le12$.

The projective action of $G$ on $A$ is nontrivial, since otherwise every subspace $\ell\otimes B$ would be invariant. A product-one generating tuple must therefore contain at least two entries nonscalar on $A$. They each have moving rank at least $s$; the other entries have rank at least two. It follows that
\[
 4s+8=\sum c_j\ge2s+2(b-2)\ge2s+34,
\]
or $s\ge13$, a contradiction. Here tensor indecomposability means that no such decomposition with both factors preserved exists; permutations of equal tensor factors are included in the convention of the classification theorem used next.

The published Theorem~7.1 and Remark~(3) of Guralnick--Saxl \cite[pp.~548--549]{GS} now apply: a finite irreducible primitive tensor-indecomposable complex group of degree greater than ten containing a moving-rank-two element is, up to scalars, the deleted permutation representation of the alternating or symmetric group. The other natural large-degree cases in that theorem have defining positive characteristic, and its exceptional characteristic-zero degrees are at most ten. Thus every element of $G$ has the form
\[
 g=\lambda\operatorname{Std}(\pi),\qquad\pi\in S_{d+1}.
\]
The scalar is unique, since no nonidentity permutation acts as a scalar on the deleted representation in these degrees. It therefore defines a character of $G$. If this character were trivial, the representation would have rational character. There are consequently at least two branch entries with $\lambda\ne1$.

A nonidentity eigenvalue of a permutation on $d+1$ letters has multiplicity at most $\lfloor(d+1)/2\rfloor$: each contributing cycle has length at least two and contributes one eigenvector. Removing the constant line does not change such an eigenspace. Each of the two nontrivial scalar entries thus has moving rank at least $\lfloor d/2\rfloor$. The branch budget gives
\[
 2d+8\ge2\lfloor d/2\rfloor+2(b-2)
       \ge2\lfloor d/2\rfloor+34.
\]
For even $d$ this forces $d\ge26$, and for odd $d$ it forces $d\ge25$. Both contradict $d\le24$. It remains to exclude $d=25$.

\paragraph{The degree-twenty-five case.}
The base is again the sphere, $b\ge19$, and $\sum c_j=58$. If the
representation is imprimitive, its block rank is either one or five.
The Shapiro bounds above give period-image dimension at most twelve
or fifteen, respectively. Thus $G$ is primitive. We show that a
primitive finite group of degree twenty-five with an element moving
at most four dimensions lies in
$\C^*\operatorname{Std}(S_{26})$.

First consider a preserved tensor structure. Its only nontrivial
factorization is $5\cdot5$. A nonscalar pure tensor has every
 eigenspace of dimension at most $4\cdot5=20$, by the eigenspace
argument above. A factor-interchanging operator has the form
$(A\otimes B)\tau$, with $\tau$ interchanging the factors. It is
conjugate to $(I\otimes C)\tau$, $C=BA$, whose square is
$C\otimes C$. Finite order makes $C$ semisimple. In a diagonal basis
there are five diagonal lines and ten two-dimensional off-diagonal
blocks. Each block contributes at most one vector to any fixed
 eigenspace. Its eigenspaces therefore have dimension at most fifteen.
Neither tensor case contains an element moving at most four dimensions.

The structural reduction in the proof of
\cite[Theorem~7.1, p.~549]{GS} applies to a primitive group with no
such tensor structure. If its Fitting subgroup is noncentral, it is
of extraspecial type, and four conjugates of a nonscalar element
 generate a subgroup containing a nontrivial scalar. Their fixed
spaces have zero intersection, so that element moves at least
$25/4$ dimensions. Otherwise there is a quasisimple normal subgroup
$E$ acting irreducibly on $V$. Indeed a nontrivial multiplicity space
or more than one commuting simple factor would give a tensor
structure. This representation of the matrix group $E$ is faithful.

Hiss--Malle's characteristic-zero classification
\cite[Theorem~1 and Table~2, pp.~22 and 31]{HissMalle}, with its
complete corrected table \cite[pp.~95--96 and 102]{HissMalleCorrection},
gives exactly the following degree-twenty-five possibilities:
\[
 E=A_{26},\quad \operatorname{PSL}_2(25),\quad
 \operatorname{PSL}_2(49),\quad \operatorname{PSp}_4(7).
\]
The alternating representation is deleted permutation. The two
$\operatorname{PSL}_2$ degrees are $q$ and $(q+1)/2$; the only other
ordinary row is $\operatorname{PSp}_4(7)$. The degree-twenty-five row
for ${}^3D_4(2)$ is in characteristic three and does not apply.

Here is the generation estimate, including scalar and outer lifts.
For a nonscalar $g$ with a nonzero fixed space, let $m$ be its
projective order, choose a prime $p\mid m$, and put $x=g^{m/p}$.
Then $x$ has projective order $p$. The scalar $x^p$ fixes every fixed
vector of $g$, so it is the identity. Thus $x$ has prime order and
moves no more dimensions than $g$. If $\alpha$ conjugates of its
induced automorphism generate a group containing $E/Z(E)$, the
intersection of the corresponding fixed spaces on $V$ is zero.
Otherwise their projective group would preserve a line, contradicting
irreducibility of $E$. Consequently
\[
 25\le\alpha\operatorname{rank}(x-I).
\]
For both $\operatorname{PSL}_2$ groups,
\cite[Lemma~3.1, pp.~527--529]{GS} gives $\alpha\le4$, including
field and diagonal automorphisms. For $\operatorname{PSp}_4(7)$,
\cite[Theorem~4.3, p.~534]{GS} gives $\alpha\le5$; its transvection
exception has the smaller value four. These bounds exclude an
 element moving at most four dimensions. The automorphism is
nontrivial, since an element centralizing the irreducible $E$ is
scalar, and a nonidentity scalar has no fixed vectors.

It remains that $E=\operatorname{Std}(A_{26})$. Its automorphism group
is $S_{26}$, and Schur's lemma then gives the full normalizer
$\C^*\operatorname{Std}(S_{26})$. This proves the claimed normalizer
assertion. In the branch tuple some entry moves at most three
 dimensions, since otherwise $58\ge4b\ge76$; hence it applies here.

Write each branch entry as $\lambda_j\operatorname{Std}(\pi_j)$.
A scalar phase outside $\{1,-1\}$ has finite order $k\ge3$, and a
fixed vector requires a cycle of $\pi_j$ whose length is divisible
by $k$. Therefore its moving rank is at least
\[
 25-\lfloor26/k\rfloor\ge17.
\]
If every scalar phase belonged to $\{1,-1\}$, the generated group
would have rational character. At least one phase is outside this
set; the product-one relation forces at least two. The remaining
branches each have moving rank at least two, giving
\[
 58=\sum c_j\ge2\cdot17+2(b-2)\ge68,
\]
a contradiction. This excludes degree twenty-five and proves the
 stated threshold.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The degree of every inducing representation]
\label{cor:gaussian-cover-rank}
Let $V$ be a nontrivial irreducible complex coefficient
representation whose character field contains $\Q(i)$.
Suppose that a finite-cover family has compact $V$-multiplicity
of dimension eight, Hodge numbers $(4,4)$, and period image of
dimension sixteen.  If
\[
 V\simeq\operatorname{Ind}_H^G U,
\]
then $U$ is irreducible, its faithful image contains no complex
reflection, and
\[
 \dim_{\C}U\geq78.
\]
The intermediate base curve $C/H$ has genus zero.
In particular, every block in a complex system of imprimitivity
has dimension at least seventy-eight.  If $V$ is imprimitive, then
\[
 \dim_{\C}V\geq156.
\]
No descent of the blocks or of $U$ to $\Q(i)$ is required.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Inducing a proper nonzero subrepresentation of $U$ would give
a proper nonzero subrepresentation of $V$, so $U$ is irreducible.
It is nontrivial, since induction of the trivial representation
contains constants.  Compact Shapiro identifies its full
multiplicity variation with the original one, and
\eqref{eq:induced-character-field-inclusion} shows that its
character field contains $\Q(i)$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:gaussian-reflections} excludes a reflection in
its faithful image.  Hence every genuine inertia matrix for
$U$ has moving rank at least two.  Its character is nonrational,
so Theorem~\ref{thm:gaussian-rank34} below applies and gives
$\dim U\geq78$. Its proof uses only the earlier rank bound
and does not depend on this corollary.
The positive-genus bound in \eqref{eq:curve-dimension} is at most
eight, proving the genus-zero assertion.  A nontrivial
imprimitivity system has at least two blocks, and the stabilizer
of one block induces $V$ from its action on that block.  This
gives the final inequality.
\end{proof}

The corollary narrows the possible coefficient representations;
it supplies neither a generating branch tuple nor a dominant
Gaussian eightfold family.


The degree-twenty-five profile with eighteen moving ranks three and
one moving rank four satisfies the Euler budget, but the proof shows
that it cannot occur in a dominant nonrational source. The distinction
between containing and being generated by small-moving elements matters:
$\mu_4\operatorname{Std}(S_{26})$ has nonrational character and contains
rank-three involutions, whereas all its rank-three involutions are
ordinary triple transpositions and generate a rational subgroup. A
positive construction in a larger degree still needs a finite group
with the correct character field, a split polarization, Hodge numbers
$(4,4)$ and a period differential of rank sixteen.



\subsection{Scalar-standard coefficients in every degree}

The alternating-group exception can be excluded without a degree
bound: its permutation realization forces the variation to come
from a small pointed curve.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks}
Let a finite-cover source over $\mathbf P^1$ have a nontrivial
irreducible complex coefficient representation $\rho$ of degree
$d\ge1$, compact cohomology multiplicity eight, and image contained
in $\C^*\operatorname{Std}(S_{d+1})$.
Its period image has complex dimension at most fifteen.
No particular character field or lower bound on the branch moving
ranks is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We use genuine coefficient branch points, filling in points with
identity coefficient inertia. If $d=1$, the nontrivial finite
character has ten genuine branch points by the compact Euler formula,
so its period image has dimension at most seven. Assume $d\ge2$.

Put $N=d+1$. No nonidentity permutation acts as a scalar on the
standard representation in these dimensions. Consequently the
scalar and permutation factors are unique and define homomorphisms
\[
 \rho=\kappa\otimes\operatorname{Std}(\pi),\qquad
 \pi:G\longrightarrow S_N,\quad
 \kappa:G\longrightarrow\C^*.
\]
Irreducibility makes the permutation action transitive.
Let $b$ be the number of genuine coefficient branch points and
let $m$ count those with nontrivial scalar phase. A specified
nonidentity eigenvalue of a permutation occurs at most
$\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ times: each contributing cycle has length at
least two and contributes one eigenvector. Removing the constant
line does not change that eigenspace. Each of these $m$ branches
therefore has moving rank at least $\lfloor d/2\rfloor$.
Every other genuine branch has moving rank at least one, so
\[
 2d+8=\sum_j\operatorname{rank}(\rho(g_j)-1)
 \ge b+m(\lfloor d/2\rfloor-1).
\]
If $m\ge5$, this implies
\[
 b\le2d+8-5(\lfloor d/2\rfloor-1)\le14.
\]
Indeed the final expression is $13-e$ for $d=2e$ and $15-e$
for $d=2e+1\ge3$. The branch-configuration dimension is then
$b-3\le11$. It remains to consider $m\le4$.

Let $q:Y\to\mathbf P^1$ be the connected degree-$N$ intermediate
cover defined by $\pi$. Write $\mathcal L$ for the rank-one finite
local system of $\kappa$ and $\mathcal L_Y=q^*\mathcal L$ on the
unramified locus. Compact Shapiro and the decomposition of the
permutation representation give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:standard-shapiro}
 H^1(Y,j_*\mathcal L_Y)
 =H^1(\mathbf P^1,j_*\mathcal L)\oplus H^1_\rho.
\end{equation}
Points with trivial upstairs inertia are filled in. At a branch
point, a permutation cycle of length $e$ contributes an invariant
stalk precisely when its scalar phase to the power $e$ is one;
this is precisely the extension condition for the upstairs
rank-one system. Finite pushforward and the projection formula
therefore identify the middle extensions. The resulting equality
respects Hodge structures and Gauss--Manin connections.

If $\kappa$ is nontrivial, the first summand has dimension $m-2$,
and hence the left side has dimension
\[
 n=m+6\le10.
\]
If $\mathcal L_Y$ is nontrivial, let $h$ be the genus of $Y$ and
$r$ its number of genuine character branch points. Then
$n=2h-2+r$. Finite monodromy contributes only discrete choices
over the moduli of the marked curve. For $h\ge2$ the latter has
dimension
\[
 3h-3+r=n+h-1\le\tfrac32n\le15.
\]
For $h=1$ its dimension is $r=n\le10$, and for $h=0$ it is
$r-3=n-1\le9$. If instead $\mathcal L_Y$ is trivial, then
$2h=n\le10$ and the unpointed curve moduli has dimension at most
twelve. Finally, if $\kappa$ itself is trivial, the first summand
in \eqref{eq:standard-shapiro} is zero and $Y$ has genus four,
giving dimension at most nine.

These bounds also control the selected summand $H^1_\rho$.
On a simply connected marked neighborhood it is a fixed flat
subspace, and its Hodge filtration is the intersection with the
ambient filtration. A tangent vector fixing the pointed curve
and its finite local system therefore fixes this filtration.
Additional motions of the map to $\mathbf P^1$, or of points
with trivial upstairs inertia, cannot increase its period rank.
This proves the theorem.
\end{proof}


\subsection{The branch budget for ordered tensor factors}

A fixed tensor factor imposes a further branch budget in every degree,
independently of the character field.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:ordered-tensor48}
Suppose a finite irreducible coefficient representation
$V=A\otimes B$, with $2\le a=\dim A\le b=\dim B$, preserves this
ordered tensor decomposition.  If a sphere-cover family has compact
cohomology multiplicity eight and period image of dimension sixteen,
then
\[
 2ab+8\ge(a+1)b+18a-a^2,\qquad ab\ge48.
\]
The assertion does not cover elements permuting tensor factors.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $g_1,\ldots,g_N$ be the nonidentity branch matrices.  They generate
the coefficient image, have product one, and $N\ge19$.
Choose a representative $x_i$ of their projective action on $A$,
scaled so that a largest eigenspace has eigenvalue one.  Put
\[
 \ell_i=a-\dim\ker(x_i-1),\qquad L=\sum_i\ell_i.
\]
Finite projective order makes each $x_i$ diagonalizable.  The product
of these representatives is scalar.  The projective action on $A$
is irreducible, since a proper invariant subspace would tensor with
$B$ to a proper invariant subspace of $V$.

Delete the scalar $x_i$.  At least two entries remain: a single
nonscalar matrix cannot have scalar product.  If $L\le a$, the
intersection of the fixed spaces of all but the last remaining
entry has dimension at least the positive moving rank of that last
entry.  It is nonzero and proper.  All preceding entries fix it
pointwise; the scalar product relation makes the last entry scalar
on it.  This contradicts irreducibility.  Thus $L\ge a+1$.

Write $c_i=\operatorname{rank}(g_i-1)$.  If $\ell_i>0$, an eigenspace
of the $B$ component matches at most one eigenspace of $x_i$, of
dimension at most $a-\ell_i$.  Hence $c_i\ge b\ell_i$.
If $\ell_i=0$, the nonidentity matrix $g_i$ moves at least one whole
$A$ factor, so $c_i\ge a$.  With $r$ the number of positive $\ell_i$,
we have $r\le L$, and therefore
\[
 \sum_i c_i\ge bL+a(N-r)
    \ge aN+(b-a)L
    \ge19a+(b-a)(a+1).
\]
The Euler equality $\sum_i c_i=2ab+8$ proves the first inequality.
Rearranging it gives $(a-1)b\ge18a-a^2-8$.
For $a=2,3,4,5,6$, the least possible $b$ is respectively
$24,19,16,15,13$, giving products $48,57,64,75,78$.
For $a\ge7$, the product is already at least $49$.
This proves the degree bound.  Equality at $(a,b)=(2,24)$ is only
a numerical possibility, with no group or cover existence implied.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Small ordered tensor factors}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:ordered-small-factors78}
Let a finite irreducible complex coefficient representation
preserve an ordered decomposition $V=A\otimes B$, where
$2\leq a=\dim A\leq5$ and $r=\dim B\geq2$.
If its compact coefficient multiplicity has dimension eight
and its period image has dimension sixteen, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small-factor-main}
                         (a-1)r\geq2a(18-a)-8.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
 a&2&3&4&5\\ \hline
 r\text{ at least}&56&41&35&31\\
 \dim V\text{ at least}&112&123&140&155
\end{array}
\]
No character-field or primitivity hypothesis is imposed.
Every nontrivial ordered tensor source of this kind, with
arbitrary factor dimensions, has total coefficient degree
at least seventy-eight.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $a\in\{2,3,4,5\}$ and choose a counterexample to
\eqref{eq:small-factor-main} with minimal $r$.
The general curve bound excludes positive genus, so the base
is a sphere. The full inequality of Proposition~\ref{prop:ordered-tensor48}
excludes smaller second factors, including $r<a$ after
interchanging the factors.  Thus the counterexample ranges are
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
 a&2&3&4&5\\ \hline
 r\text{ at least}&24&19&16&15\\
 r\text{ at most}&55&40&34&30.
\end{array}
\]
There are $N\geq19$ genuine branches
and their moving-rank sum is $2ar+8$.

Let $\delta_j$ be the projective moving rank of a branch on
$A$, set $K=\sum\delta_j$, and let $t$ count its positive
terms.  The normalized projective entries have scalar product
and act irreducibly.  Their fixed spaces give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small-factor-projective}
                         K\geq a+\max_j\delta_j.
\end{equation}
Indeed, place a chosen nonscalar entry last by cyclically
reordering the scalar product relation, and normalize every
entry so that a largest eigenspace has eigenvalue one.
If the other fixed spaces had total codimension less than
$a$, their intersection would be nonzero and proper.
The preceding entries fix it pointwise; the scalar product
relation makes the last entry scalar on it.
This contradicts irreducibility.

At a nonscalar-$A$ branch write its coefficient moving rank as
$r\delta_i+f_i$, where $f_i\geq0$, and put $F=\sum f_i$.
At a scalar-$A$ branch absorb its scalar into $B$, writing
$I_A\otimes y_j$, and put
$s_j=\operatorname{rank}(y_j-1)$, $S=\sum s_j$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small-factor-budget}
 F+aS=(2a-K)r+8,\qquad
 F\leq(2a-K)r+8-a(19-t).
\end{equation}
The weaker inequality
$2ar+8\geq aN+(r-a)K$, together with the counterexample
ranges, leaves only
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
 a&K\\ \hline
 2&3\\
 3&4,5\\
 4&5,6\\
 5&6,7,8.
\end{array}
\]

If every scalar-$A$ branch had $s_j\geq2$, then $r>2a$
and $t\leq K$ would give
\[
 2ar+8\geq rK+2a(N-t)
       \geq2aN+(r-2a)K
       \geq(a+1)r+2a(18-a).
\]
This is exactly \eqref{eq:small-factor-main}, a contradiction.
Thus some $y_j$ is a complex reflection.

Take the normal closure of the actual $I_A\otimes y_j$
reflection elements inside the coefficient group.
It has the form $I_A\otimes R$, with $R$ a finite reflection
group on $B$.  Its common fixed part is zero by irreducibility,
and its irreducible reflection components are permuted
transitively.  More than one component induces the complete
multiplicity variation from $A\otimes B_1$ by compact Shapiro.
If $\dim B_1\geq2$, this contradicts minimality of $r$;
if $\dim B_1=1$, the induced coefficient has rank $a$,
whose general period bound is $2a+5\leq15$.
Therefore $R$ is irreducible of rank at least fifteen.

The finite reflection classification leaves a monomial group
or $W(A_r)$.  The monomial normalizer preserves its unique
coordinate-line system in these ranks
\cite[Lemma~(2.7)]{CohenReflection}, and would again induce
from rank $a$.  Consequently $R=W(A_r)$.
The type-$A$ normalizer gives unique actual finite
homomorphisms
\[
 g=u_g\otimes\operatorname{Std}(w_g),\qquad w_g\in S_{r+1}.
\]
Here $u$ is irreducible and the permutation quotient is
transitive.  Projective faithfulness of the deleted permutation
representation ensures that both assignments are homomorphisms.

At a scalar-$A$ branch with $u=\lambda I_A$, $\lambda\ne1$,
the relevant nonidentity permutation eigenspace has dimension
at most $\lfloor(r+1)/2\rfloor$.
Its coefficient moving rank is at least $a\lfloor r/2\rfloor$.
All branches would then give
\[
 2ar+8\geq rK+a(N-t-1)+a\lfloor r/2\rfloor
       \geq(a+1)r+a(17-a)+a\lfloor r/2\rfloor.
\]
The right side minus the left side is at least, respectively,
\[
                  21,\quad(65-r)/2,\quad42-r,
                  \quad(99-3r)/2.
\]
Each is positive in its counterexample range.
Hence every scalar-$A$ branch has actual $u=I_A$.

We next prove that the actual moving ranks
$k_i=\operatorname{rank}(u_i-1)$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small-factor-actual-ranks}
                              \sum k_i\leq10.
\end{equation}
For $a=2$, there are exactly three nonscalar branches, so
the sum is at most six.
For $a\geq3$, set $e_i=k_i-\delta_i\geq0$.
The following elementary spectral calculation supplies the
remaining cases.

Let $m_0=a-k_i$ be the multiplicity of eigenvalue one and
let $p_1\geq p_2\geq\cdots$ be the multiplicities of the
distinct nonidentity eigenvalues.  Put
$T=a-\delta_i=\max(m_0,p_1)$, so $e_i=T-m_0$.
On a permutation cycle of length $q$, the fixed dimension is
at most $m_0$ plus the largest $q-1$ of the $p_j$.
Let $\beta$ be the maximum of this bound divided by $q$.
The full degree-$(r+1)$ permutation tensor has fixed
dimension at most $\beta(r+1)$; deleting its trivial summand
removes $m_0$ fixed dimensions.  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small-factor-spectral}
                         f_i\geq(T-\beta)(r+1)-e_i.
\end{equation}
Using $\delta_i\leq K-a$, the small multiplicity partitions
give the seven uniform bounds $f_i\geq\mu_{a,K}(r)e_i$
displayed below.  The last two columns show the resulting
contradiction if $\sum k_i\geq11$, since then
$\sum e_i\geq11-K$:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c}
 a&K&\mu_{a,K}(r)&F\text{ at least}&F\text{ at most}\\ \hline
 3&4&(r-1)/2&7(r-1)/2&2r-37\\
 3&5&(r-3)/4&6(r-3)/4&r-34\\
 4&5&(r-1)/2&3(r-1)&3r-48\\
 4&6&(r-2)/3&5(r-2)/3&2r-44\\
 5&6&(r-1)/2&5(r-1)/2&4r-57\\
 5&7&(4r-5)/9&4(4r-5)/9&3r-52\\
 5&8&(r-2)/3&r-2&2r-47
\end{array}
\]
The upper bounds use \eqref{eq:small-factor-budget} and
$t\leq K$.  Each lower bound is strictly greater than its
upper bound on the indicated counterexample range.

Here are the partitions verifying every spectral row.
When $\delta_i=1$, the multiplicity partition is $(a-1,1)$.
If $e_i>0$, either $m_0=1$ or $m_0=0$; formula
\eqref{eq:small-factor-spectral} gives
$f_i\geq e_i(r-1)/2$ for $a\geq3$.
For $a=3$, $\delta_i=2$, positive excess requires three
distinct nonidentity eigenvalues; then $\beta\leq3/4$ and
$f_i\geq(r-3)/4$.
For $a=4$, $\delta_i=2$, the remaining positive-excess
partitions are
$(m_0;p)=(1;2,1),(0;2,2),(0;2,1,1)$.
The worst ratio is $(r-2)/3$, obtained from
$(0;2,2)$ with $\beta=4/3$ and $e_i=2$.
For $a=5$, $\delta_i=2$, the possibilities are
$(2;3),(1;3,1),(0;3,2),(0;3,1,1)$.
The worst ratio is $(4r-5)/9$, from $(0;3,2)$ with
$\beta=5/3$, $e_i=3$.
Finally, for $a=5$, $\delta_i=3$, they are
$(1;2,2),(1;2,1,1),(0;2,2,1),(0;2,1,1,1)$.
The worst ratio is $(r-2)/3$: the first and third of these
have $(\beta,e_i)=(5/3,1),(4/3,2)$, respectively.
These lists exhaust the positive-excess possibilities;
for $e_i=0$ use $f_i\geq0$.
Missing specified roots of unity only decrease fixed dimensions.
This proves \eqref{eq:small-factor-actual-ranks}.

Let $\mathcal L_u$ be the actual finite rank-$a$ local
system.  Its irreducibility makes its compact first-cohomology
dimension
\[
                         n_u=\sum k_i-2a\geq0.
\]
Let $q:Y\longrightarrow\mathbf P^1$ be the connected
degree-$(r+1)$ cover defined by $w$.
The permutation decomposition gives, as compact cohomology
variations,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:small-factor-compact-shapiro}
 H^1(Y,j_*q^*\mathcal L_u)
   =H^1(\mathbf P^1,j_*\mathcal L_u)\oplus H^1_V.
\end{equation}
To check the extensions at a branch point, a permutation
cycle of length $e$ has invariant induced sections determined
exactly by $\operatorname{Fix}(u_j^e)$, the upstairs extension
stalk.  Finite proper pushforward then gives
\eqref{eq:small-factor-compact-shapiro}; no commutation of
tensor product with separate middle extensions is assumed.

The left side has dimension
$M=8+n_u=8+\sum k_i-2a\leq18-2a$.
If $Y$ has genus $h$, its pullback local system has $b$
genuine branches, and its invariant subspace has dimension $s$,
then the compact Euler formula is
\[
 M=2a(h-1)+\sum_y\operatorname{rank}(T_y-1)+2s,
 \qquad b\leq M-2a(h-1)-2s.
\]
In genus zero the marked-curve dimension is at most
$M+2a-3=5+\sum k_i\leq15$.
In genus one it is at most $M\leq14$, or one when unmarked.
In higher genus it is at most
$M+(3-2a)(h-1)-2s<16$.
This also covers a reducible upstairs local system.
Finite monodromy supplies only discrete choices, and a fixed
flat summand cannot increase period rank.
Thus the selected multiplicity cannot have period-image
dimension sixteen, completing the proof of
\eqref{eq:small-factor-main}.

For the final assertion, write an ordered factorization with
$a\leq b$.  The cases $a=2,3,4,5$ have the lower degrees
in the theorem's table.
The full ordered inequality gives $b\geq13,12,11,10$ for
$a=6,7,8,9$, yielding degrees $78,84,88,90$.
If $a\geq10$, the degree is at least $100$.
The least remaining numerical possibility is therefore $78$.
\end{proof}

These are necessary restrictions.  No coefficient source at
a boundary degree, integral principal polarization, or
algebraic Weil detector is asserted.


\subsection{Primitive ordered tensor coefficients below one hundred}

Primitivity permits a further uniform spectral estimate. Unlike
Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-small-factors78}, the following result
uses primitivity, but it imposes no character-field condition.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:primitive-ordered100}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, irreducible and primitive,
and suppose that it preserves an ordered decomposition
$V=A\otimes B$ with both factors of dimension at least two.
If a finite-cover family has compact $V$-multiplicity eight
and period image of dimension sixteen, then $\dim V\geq100$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $d=\dim V<100$, and order the dimensions as
$2\leq a=\dim A\leq r=\dim B$. The general positive-genus
bound gives a sphere base. Write $N\geq19$ for the number
of genuine coefficient branches; their moving ranks sum to
$2ar+8$. Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-small-factors78} excludes
$a\leq5$, while $a\geq10$ would give $d\geq100$.
The inequality of Proposition~\ref{prop:ordered-tensor48}
leaves exactly the following ranges:
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
 a&6&7&8&9\\ \hline
 r&13\text{--}16&12\text{--}14&11,12&10,11.
\end{array}
\]
Let $\delta_i$ be the projective moving rank on $A$, and put
\[
 K=\sum_i\delta_i,\qquad D=\max_i\delta_i,
 \qquad t=\#\{i:\delta_i>0\}.
\]
The fixed-space argument of \eqref{eq:small-factor-projective}
gives $K\geq a+D$, and $t\leq K-D+1\leq K$.

A nonscalar-$A$ branch moves at least $r$ dimensions.
If no scalar-$A$ branch is an actual $I_A\otimes y$ with
$y$ a reflection, then every scalar-$A$ branch moves at least
$2a$ dimensions. But in every displayed pair
\[
                 19\min(r,2a)>2ar+8.
\]
Thus there is such a reflection. The normal closure of these
actual elements is $I_A\otimes R$, with $R$ a finite
reflection group on $B$. Its common fixed part is zero,
and its irreducible reflection components are permuted by $G$.
More than one component would give a nontrivial imprimitivity
system $A\otimes B_j$, so $R$ is irreducible.
In ranks $r\geq10$ the reflection classification leaves a
monomial group or $W(A_r)$. In the monomial case its normalizer
preserves the unique coordinate-line system
\cite[Lemma~(2.7), in ranks at least five]{CohenReflection},
again contradicting primitivity. Hence $R=W(A_r)$.
As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-small-factors78},
the type-$A$ normalizer gives actual finite homomorphisms
\[
                    g=u_g\otimes\operatorname{Std}(w_g),
                    \qquad w_g\in S_{r+1},
\]
with $u$ irreducible of degree $a$.

If a scalar-$A$ branch has actual $u=\lambda I_A$ with
$\lambda\ne1$, its moving rank is at least
$a\lfloor r/2\rfloor$. Combining this with the ordinary
ordered budget would give
\[
 2ar+8\geq(a+1)r+a(18-a)+a(\lfloor r/2\rfloor-1).
\]
The right side exceeds the left by at least twenty-one in
the displayed ranges. Thus every scalar-$A$ branch has
actual $u=I_A$.

For a nonscalar branch put
\[
 k_i=\operatorname{rank}(u_i-1),\quad
 e_i=k_i-\delta_i\geq0,\quad T_i=a-\delta_i.
\]
Here $T_i$ is the largest eigenspace multiplicity and the
multiplicity of eigenvalue one is $T_i-e_i$.
Write its coefficient moving rank as $r\delta_i+f_i$, with
$f_i\geq0$. We claim the uniform estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:primitive-tensor-spectral}
                  f_i\geq e_i\frac{T_i r-a}{a+T_i}.
\end{equation}
Suppress the index. A permutation cycle of length $q$
contributes at most
\[
 C_q\leq\min(a,qT-e)
\]
fixed vectors to the full permutation tensor: it sees at most
$q$ eigenvalues of $u$, including eigenvalue one of multiplicity
$T-e$, while each other multiplicity is at most $T$.
Consequently
\[
 \beta:=\max_{q\geq1}\min(a/q,T-e/q)
            \leq\frac{aT}{a+e}.
\]
The final inequality follows by intersecting the decreasing
function $a/q$ and the increasing function $T-e/q$; it also
holds for $e=0$. Removing the constant permutation summand
removes $T-e$ fixed dimensions, so
\[
 f\geq(T-\beta)(r+1)-e
   \geq\frac{eT(r+1)}{a+T}-e
   =e\frac{Tr-a}{a+T},
\]
where $e\leq T$ was used. This proves the claim.

Put $F=\sum f_i$ and $E=\sum e_i$. The remaining genuine
branches, with $u_i=I_A$, cost at least $a$, whence
\[
 F\leq(2a-K)r+8-a(19-t)
   \leq(2a-K)(r-a)+8-a(19-2a).
\]
For $a=6,7,8,9$ the final constant is respectively
$-34,-27,-16,-1$. Since $F\geq0$ and $r\geq a$, we have
$K\leq2a-1$. Set $h=2a-K$, so $1\leq h\leq a-1$.
The inequality $K\geq a+D$ gives $T_i\geq h$.
The actual irreducible rank-$a$ system $u$ has no invariants
or coinvariants; its compact Euler identity gives
\[
 0\leq\dim H^1_{\rm par}(\mathbf P^1,u)
        =\sum_i k_i-2a=E-h.
\]
Thus $E\geq h$. Since $(Tr-a)/(a+T)$ is positive and
increasing in $T$ in these ranges,
\[
 \frac{h(hr-a)}{a+h}\leq F
                \leq hr+8-a(19-2a+h).
\]
The difference of the rightmost and leftmost bounds is
\[
 H(a,r,h)=a\left(\frac{h(r+1-a-h)}{a+h}-(19-2a)\right)+8.
\]
Its maxima over the indicated integer ranges are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 a&r&h&\max H(a,r,h)\\\hline
 6&13\text{--}16&1\text{--}5&-86/5\\
 7&12\text{--}14&1\text{--}6&-33/2\\
 8&11,12&1\text{--}7&-56/5\\
 9&10&1\text{--}8&-1/10\\
 9&11&3\text{--}8&-1.
\end{array}
\]
These values follow by substitution at the largest $r$ in
each row, since $H$ increases with $r$, and at the listed
integer $h$ values. They are all negative.

Only $(a,r,h)=(9,11,1),(9,11,2)$ remain.
Retaining $t\leq K-D+1$ in the budget gives
\[
                          F\leq2h+8-9D.
\]
If $D\geq2$ this is negative, so $D=1$ and every $T_i=8$.
Now \eqref{eq:primitive-tensor-spectral} gives
\[
                          F\geq79E/17\geq79h/17.
\]
For $h=1,2$ this exceeds the respective upper bounds one
and three. All possible pairs have been excluded.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Primitivity was used only in the reflection normal-closure
reduction. For coefficients whose character field contains
$\Q(i)$, Corollary~\ref{cor:gaussian-cover-rank} already
puts every imprimitive source above degree one hundred.
Hence every Gaussian source preserving a nontrivial ordered
tensor decomposition has degree at least one hundred.
The argument does not assert that degree one hundred is
attainable, or supply a dominant cover or a Weil detector.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Permuted tensor powers}
\label{subsec:permuted-tensor-powers}

An ordered tensor decomposition is not needed for the next
restriction.  It treats a tensor power whose factors may be
permuted by the coefficient group.

\begin{lemma}[Moving ranks in a tensor power]
\label{lem:permuted-tensor-moving-rank}
Let $V=(\mathbf C^a)^{\otimes t}$, with $a,t\geq2$ and
$d=a^t$.  Suppose a finite subgroup of $\operatorname{GL}(V)$
preserves this tensor decomposition, allowing permutations of the
$t$ factors.  Every nonidentity element $g$ satisfies
\[
 \operatorname{rank}(g-1)\geq
 \begin{cases}
 d/4,&a=2,\\
 d/a,&a\geq3.
 \end{cases}
\]
More precisely, if its factor permutation is nontrivial, every
eigenspace of $g$ has dimension at most $d(a+1)/(2a)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $g=(A_1\otimes\cdots\otimes A_t)\pi$, where $\pi$
permutes the factors.  First suppose that $\pi=1$.
Some power of $g$ is the identity.  A tensor product of invertible
linear maps can be scalar only when each factor is scalar:
fix nonzero vectors in the other factors and compare the images
of arbitrary vectors in the remaining factor.  Thus a common
positive power of every $A_j$ is scalar.  Each $A_j$ is therefore
semisimple over $\mathbf C$.  If some $A_j$ is nonscalar, its
largest eigenspace has dimension at most $a-1$.  For semisimple
maps $P,Q$, the largest eigenspace of $P\otimes Q$ has dimension
at most the largest eigenspace dimension of $P$ times $\dim Q$;
this follows by summing the dimensions of the $Q$-eigenspaces
paired with each eigenvalue of $P$.  Consequently every eigenspace
of $g$ has dimension at most $(a-1)d/a$.  If instead all the
$A_j$ are scalar, $g$ is a scalar; since $g\ne1$, its moving
rank is $d$.

Now choose a cycle of length $\ell\geq2$ in $\pi$.
Conjugating by a tensor product of changes of basis makes the
operator on these factors a cyclic shift with one return map
$P=A_{j_\ell}\cdots A_{j_1}$.  Since a power of $g$ is the
identity, a power of $P$ is scalar, by the same elementary tensor
observation.  Diagonalize $P$ and use the corresponding tensor
basis on these $\ell$ factors.  The cycle operator permutes the
$a^\ell$ basis words by cyclic rotation and multiplies them by
nonzero scalars.  On each orbit of words it is a weighted cyclic
permutation matrix, whose characteristic polynomial is
$X^q-c$ for some $c\ne0$.  Its eigenvalues are simple.
Thus the multiplicity of any eigenvalue is at most the number
of cyclic-rotation orbits of words.

There are exactly $a$ fixed words, and each remaining orbit has
at least two words.  The number of orbits is therefore at most
\[
 a+\frac{a^\ell-a}{2}=\frac{a^\ell+a}{2}
       \leq\frac{a+1}{2a}\,a^\ell.
\]
Operators associated to distinct cycles form a tensor product.
Each has a scalar positive power, and hence is semisimple.
The eigenspace bound for tensor products used above extends the
last estimate to $d(a+1)/(2a)$ for the whole operator $g$.
Its moving rank is at least $d(a-1)/(2a)$.
Combining the two permutation cases gives
$d\min\{1/a,(a-1)/(2a)\}$, which is the displayed bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A restriction on transitive tensor-power sources]
\label{prop:permuted-tensor100}
Let a finite-cover family have a nontrivial irreducible complex
coefficient representation on $(\mathbf C^a)^{\otimes t}$,
where $a,t\geq2$, and suppose its finite coefficient group
preserves this tensor decomposition up to permutation.
Assume that the compact first-cohomology multiplicity has
complex dimension eight.  If its period image has dimension
sixteen, then
\[
                         a\geq10,\qquad a^t\geq100.
\]
No character-field assumption is needed.  For the tensor powers
of dimensions $27=3^3$ and $32=2^5$, the period-image dimensions
are at most three and six, respectively.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
At positive base genus the Euler-characteristic and
marked-curve bound \eqref{eq:complex-shapiro-bound} is at most
eight, so a sixteen-dimensional image requires a sphere base.
Let $b$ be its number of genuine coefficient branch points.
The compact Euler characteristic and
Lemma~\ref{lem:permuted-tensor-moving-rank} give
\[
 2d+8=\sum_{j=1}^b\operatorname{rank}(g_j-1),\qquad
 b\leq\begin{cases}
 8+32/d,&a=2,\\
 2a+8/a^{t-1},&a\geq3.
 \end{cases}
\]
The period-image dimension is at most $b-3$.
If $a=2$, then $d\geq4$ and $b\leq16$.
If $3\leq a\leq9$, then $t\geq2$ and
$2a+8/a^{t-1}\leq2a+8/a$.
The latter function is increasing on $[3,9]$ and is less than
$19$ at $a=9$.  Hence its integral upper bound for $b$ is at
most $18$, again excluding dimension sixteen.
This proves $a\geq10$, and $t\geq2$ then gives $d\geq100$.
For $a=3,t=3$, the bound is
$b\leq\lfloor62/9\rfloor=6$; for $a=2,t=5$, it is
$b\leq72/8=9$.  Positive-genus bases cannot improve these two
bounds: in genus one the rank estimates give respectively
$b=0$ (incompatible with the Euler characteristic) and
$b\leq1$, while higher genus is impossible because $2d>8$.
The stated period bounds three and six follow.
\end{proof}


\subsection{An imprimitive Gaussian representation of degree ninety-nine}

A larger coefficient rank does not by itself remove the branch-cost
obstruction. The following example isolates the extra cost imposed by
its permutation action on blocks.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:degree99-cover}
Let $G$ be the elementary-abelian extension $2^{10}.M_{11}$ identified
in the Character Table Library by
\texttt{P48/G1/L1/V2/ext2}, and let $\rho$ be either of its conjugate
faithful degree-$99$ characters with character field $\Q(i)$.
A sphere-cover source for $\rho$ with compact cohomology multiplicity
