eight has at most seven nontrivial coefficient branch points. Its
period image therefore has dimension at most four. The assertion
also holds after finite scalar enlargement of the coefficient group.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The group construction and its table are given in
\cite[\S1.2-9]{CTblLib}. Write $N$ for the elementary abelian normal
subgroup of order $1024$. The seven $G$-classes in $N$, in increasing
library order, give the following restriction data:
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrrr}
\text{class size}&1&55&330&396&66&165&11\\
\chi&99&63&27&-9&-9&-45&-81.
\end{array}
\]
The two conjugate characters have the same restriction, since these
values are real. Thus
\[
 \langle\chi|_N,\chi|_N\rangle_N
 =\frac{99^2+55\cdot63^2+330\cdot27^2
       +462\cdot9^2+165\cdot45^2+11\cdot81^2}{1024}
 =891.
\]
Clifford theory expresses this restriction as $m$ times an orbit of
$n$ distinct linear characters of $N$. Since $mn=99$ and
$m^2n=891$, we obtain $m=9$ and $n=11$. The corresponding eigenspaces
are eleven blocks of dimension nine. Their permutation action
factors through $M_{11}$, is transitive, and is faithful because
$M_{11}$ is simple and the action is nontrivial.

The degree-eleven transitive permutation character of $M_{11}$ is
\[
 (11,3,2,3,1,0,1,1,0,0).
\]
For clarity, its identification does not require choosing a block
labeling: the least nontrivial irreducible character degree is ten,
so a degree-eleven transitive permutation character is one plus a
degree-ten irreducible; exactly one such sum has nonnegative integral
values. The class power maps give permutation indices
\[
 (0,4,6,6,8,8,8,8,10,10).
\]
Equivalently, the number of permutation cycles is the multiplicity
of the eigenvalue one, which gives these indices by subtraction
from eleven. In particular every nonzero index is at least four.

Let $i_j$ be the block-permutation index of a coefficient branch
matrix and let $c_j$ be its moving rank. A permutation cycle on
$\ell$ blocks contributes at most nine fixed vectors and hence at
least $9(\ell-1)$ moving vectors. Therefore $c_j\ge9i_j$, with any
scalar phase allowed. If $i_j=0$, the matrix lies in $N$ up to a
scalar and acts as a scalar on each block. A nonidentity matrix
then moves at least one whole block, so $c_j\ge9$. This inequality
also follows from the first one when $i_j>0$. Since a nonzero
$i_j$ is at least four, the two bounds imply
\[
 c_j\ge9+\frac{27}{4}i_j.
\]

The branch permutations generate a transitive degree-eleven group
and have product one. Riemann--Hurwitz for the associated degree-eleven
cover gives $\sum i_j\ge20$. If there are $b$ coefficient branches,
\[
 206=2\cdot99+8=\sum_jc_j
     \ge9b+\frac{27}{4}\sum_ji_j
     \ge9b+135.
\]
Consequently $b\le7$, and the branch-configuration dimension is
$b-3\le4$. All estimates were independent of scalar phases, proving
also the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The numerical restriction data can be verified from the published
permutation-group construction, without accepting an abstract table
as a group-realization certificate. Computing that group's order gives
$8110080$; its solvable radical is elementary abelian of order $1024$;
and its computed ordinary character table has $52$ classes and agrees
with the cited table. The \emph{Gaussian finite-cover checks} in the
\href{\workbenchurl}{workbench} reproduce these exact calculations.
A scan of the $2678$ named tables in version~1.3.11 selects $942$
faithful exact-Gaussian characters of degrees $25$ through $250$.
Only the two characters of this proposition pass the initial necessary
inequality $19\nu\le2d+8$, where $\nu$ is the least nonzero projective
moving rank. This describes a finite search and its coverage; it is
not a classification of finite Gaussian linear groups. In particular,
scalar twists of rational representations need not occur in that list.

\subsection{A degree-one-hundred-ten coefficient over an imaginary quadratic field}

The same exact test over the other imaginary quadratic character
fields leaves one additional pair in that finite catalogue.  Its
branch costs already contradict the compact cohomology dimension.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:degree110-cover}
Let $G$ be the group $2^{10}.M_{11}$ identified in the Character Table
Library by \texttt{P48/G1/L1/V1/ext2}, and let $\rho$ have either
faithful degree-$110$ character with exact character field
$\Q(\sqrt{-2})$.  No sphere-cover source for $\rho$ with compact
cohomology multiplicity eight has a sixteen-dimensional period image.
The assertion permits arbitrary finite scalar enlargement.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The ordinary character table and its power maps give least nonzero
moving rank ten, even after arbitrary scalar phases.  The Euler
budget is $2\cdot110+8=228$.  If there are at least nineteen branch
points, each must have moving rank at most $228-18\cdot10=48$.
Here is the complete list of class and scalar pairs with moving
rank between two and forty-eight; the conjugate characters have the
same list:
\[
\begin{array}{c|r|r}
 \text{class}&\text{scalar}&\text{moving rank}\\\hline
 2a&1&20\\
 2b&1&40\\
 2e&-1&30\\
 2f&-1&10
\end{array}
\]
For each class of order $s$, the power-map traces determine the
multiplicity of every $s$th root of unity by finite Fourier inversion.
A scalar phase can give a fixed vector only when it is the inverse
of one of those eigenvalues; every other phase has moving rank
$110$.  Enumerating these inverse phases gives exactly the four
rows displayed.  Thus all branch costs are divisible by ten, whereas
$228$ is not.  This is impossible before any generation or
polarization condition is imposed.

There is also a quotient check.  The nontrivial degree-ten character
of the table with index two is trivial on the scalar centre and on
all four displayed classes.  A product-one tuple generating the
projective image would have, in that character, total moving rank
at least twenty, by the nonnegativity of its compact first cohomology.
The four classes would give total moving rank zero.  This gives
the same exclusion independently of the divisibility test.
\end{proof}

For context, scanning all $2678$ named ordinary tables in version
1.3.11 selects $1592$ faithful characters of degree $26$ through
$250$ with exact imaginary quadratic character field other than
$\Q(\sqrt{-3})$.  They lie in $371$ named tables and nineteen
imaginary quadratic fields.  Only the two conjugate pairs in
Propositions~\ref{prop:degree99-cover} and
\ref{prop:degree110-cover} satisfy the necessary minimum-cost
inequality, even with arbitrary scalar phases allowed.
The \emph{quadratic finite-cover checks} in the
\href{\workbenchurl}{workbench} give the exact field tests,
power-map computations, branch costs and rational certificates.
These are computations in a specified finite catalogue; they do
not classify all finite groups, assert a Schur index, or construct
an integral coefficient lattice.

\subsection{Rational coefficient characters and arbitrary scalar phases}

There is a separate restriction when the coefficient character before
scalar enlargement is rational.  It permits rank-one moving parts and
all finite scalar phases.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:rational-scalar-budget}
Let $\rho$ be a finite irreducible representation of degree $d>1$
with rational character.  Suppose that a product-one tuple
$M_j=\lambda_j\rho(g_j)$ generates an irreducible group with nonreal
character, has at least nineteen genuine branches, and has parabolic
first cohomology of dimension eight.  Let $\nu$ be the least positive
moving rank among all scalar multiples of elements of $\rho(G)$,
and let $\eta$ be the least moving rank with nonreal scalar phase.
Then
\[
 2\eta+17\nu\le 2d+8,\qquad \eta\ge\lceil d/2\rceil.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A scalar in the rational-character image has rational trace $d\zeta$,
so it is $1$ or $-1$.  The product-one condition consequently makes
$\prod_j\lambda_j$ real.  All real phases would give rational
character on every generated word.  At least one phase is therefore
nonreal, and the real product forces at least two such phases.
The Euler formula gives $\sum_j\operatorname{rank}(M_j-1)=2d+8$.
Two terms cost at least $\eta$, and at least seventeen others cost
at least $\nu$.  Finally the rational character gives equal
multiplicities to conjugate eigenvalues.  A nonreal eigenspace has
dimension at most $\lfloor d/2\rfloor$, which proves the last bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A bounded-degree symmetric-family exclusion]
\label{prop:symmetric-scalar-exclusion}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:rational-scalar-budget},
$\rho$ cannot be a nonstandard irreducible representation of any
$S_s$ of degree $26\le d\le500$, or a scalar enlargement thereof.
Here nonstandard excludes the trivial, sign, standard and
sign-twisted standard representations.  This conclusion is a finite
exact computation together with the degree cutoff proved below.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a cycle class of order $h$, rationality makes the multiplicity
$m_q$ of an individual primitive $q$th root independent of its choice.
Fourier inversion, grouping powers by their order, gives
\[
 m_q=\frac1h\sum_{r\mid h}\chi(g^{h/r})c_r(h/q),\qquad
 c_r(e)=\mu\left(\frac r{(r,e)}\right)
       \frac{\varphi(r)}{\varphi(r/(r,e))},\quad q\mid h.
\]
The second expression is the sum of the primitive $r$th roots to
the $e$th power, obtained by inclusion--exclusion over divisors.
Thus the real-phase costs are $d-m_1,d-m_2$, and the nonreal
minimum is $d-\max_{q>2}m_q$.  A phase whose inverse is absent
from the spectrum costs $d$ and needs no additional enumeration.
Zero cost is omitted from the genuine branches.

The exact Murnaghan--Nakayama calculation in the
\emph{rational scalar checks} of the
\href{\workbenchurl}{workbench} enumerates the $117$ nonstandard
partitions up to transposition with $5\le s\le33$ and degree
between $26$ and $500$.  Transposition tensors with sign, which
preserves all phase-cost minima.  For every cycle partition the
calculation checks nonnegative integral multiplicities and
\[
 \sum_{q\mid h}\varphi(q)m_q=d,\qquad
 \sum_{q\mid h}\mu(q)m_q=\chi(g).
\]
Every selected character has
$2\eta+17\nu-(2d+8)>0$.  The smallest excess is $76$,
at the partition $(7,2)$ of $9$, of degree $27$, with
$\nu=6$ and $\eta=18$.  For comparison, $(31,2)$ of $33$
has degree $495$, $\nu=30$, $\eta=330$ and excess $172$.
The stored rows give the degrees, minima and witnessing cycle
partitions; a separate integer hook-length calculation verifies
the completeness of the partition selection.

It remains to justify the cutoff for all $s$.  A nonstandard
partition of $s$ has first row and first column at most $s-2$.
For $s\ge6$ it has a removable corner leaving a nonstandard
partition of $s-1$.  If both lengths are at most $s-3$, any
corner works.  If the first row has length $s-2$, the remaining
two boxes form $(2)$ or $(1,1)$; removing the last box of the
first row works.  The column case is its transpose, and the two
exceptional bounds cannot hold simultaneously for $s\ge6$.
The restriction branching rule, or equivalently the decomposition
of standard tableaux by the corner containing their largest entry,
makes the degree at least that of this predecessor.
The independent exact hook-length enumeration of all $12310$
partitions of $34$ gives smallest nonstandard degree $527$,
attained only by $(32,2)$ and its transpose.  Repeated removal
therefore excludes degrees at most $500$ for every $s\ge34$.
For $s<5$ all degrees are below $26$.  This proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

The analogous named-table calculation in CTblLib version~1.3.11
reads all $2678$ ordinary table names and selects $12479$ faithful
rational-character rows of degrees $26$ through $500$, occurring
in $1365$ named tables.  Every row violates
Lemma~\ref{lem:rational-scalar-budget}, with smallest strict
excess $28$, even with arbitrary scalar phases and moving rank
one allowed.  Exact cyclic Fourier inversion is used in every
case, with dimension, trace, integrality and conjugation checks.
This last assertion has precisely the scope of that finite
catalogue.  It does not exhaust unlisted finite groups, tensor
products or proper irreducible projective subgroups.
\subsection{A fourth-moment obstruction to finite-cover sources}

A further necessary condition follows from the projective-drop theorem of
Guralnick and Tiep.  For a finite group $G$ and a complex representation $V$,
write
\[
 M_4(G,V):=\dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{End}_G(V\otimes V).
\]
If $\chi_V$ is its character, then
\[
 M_4(G,V)=\frac1{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}|\chi_V(g)|^4
 =\dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{End}_G(\operatorname{End}(V)).
\]
The second equality follows by permuting the factors of
$V^{\otimes2}\otimes(V^*)^{\otimes2}$.  When $V$ is irreducible and
$\dim V\ge2$, the decomposition
\[
 \operatorname{End}(V)=\mathbb C\,\mathrm{id}_V
 \oplus\operatorname{End}^{0}(V)
\]
and complete reducibility show that $M_4(G,V)=2$ if and only if the
trace-free summand $\operatorname{End}^{0}(V)$ is irreducible.
Here $\operatorname{End}^{0}(V)$ is the kernel of the trace map and $G$
acts by conjugation.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:fourth-moment-cover}
Let a Hurwitz family of finite covers of smooth projective curves carry
an irreducible finite coefficient representation $V$ of dimension $d$.
Suppose that its parabolic cohomology multiplicity has complex dimension
eight.  If
\[
 d\ge22\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad M_4(G,V)=2,
\]
then its period image has dimension at most fifteen.  In particular, it
cannot dominate a unitary period domain of signature $(4,4)$.
For base genus zero one has the more explicit bound
\[
 \dim\operatorname{im}(\mathrm{per})
 \le \left\lfloor16+\frac{64}{d}\right\rfloor-3.
\]
The group $G$ in the moment condition may be replaced by its faithful image
on $V$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Pass to the faithful coefficient image.  The coefficient variation is
unchanged, since taking invariants of the kernel in the cohomology of the
cover gives the cohomology of its quotient.  A branch point with identity
coefficient monodromy can therefore be forgotten.  Write $\gamma$ for the
genus of the base and $b$ for the number of remaining branch points.  For
its local monodromies $g_1,\dots,g_b$, put
\[
 k_j:=\operatorname{rank}(g_j-1).
\]
Thus $k_j>0$.  Since $V$ is nontrivial and irreducible, it and its dual have
no global invariant sections.  The Euler characteristic of its middle
extension consequently gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fourth-moment-parabolic-budget}
 8=2d(\gamma-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{b}k_j.
\end{equation}
For fixed finite monodromy, the covering choices have finite fibres over
the moduli of the marked base curve.  Hence the period image has dimension
at most $3\gamma-3+b$ whenever this marked curve is stable.
If $\gamma\ge2$, the first term on the right of
\eqref{eq:fourth-moment-parabolic-budget} exceeds eight, a contradiction.
If $\gamma=1$, the same formula gives $\sum k_j=8$, so $1\le b\le8$;
the moduli dimension is $b\le8$.  The unmarked genus-one case is excluded
by the formula.  It remains to treat $\gamma=0$.

For $g\in G$, let
\[
 \delta_V(g):=d-\max_{\lambda\in\mathbb C}
                      \dim\ker(g-\lambda\,\mathrm{id}_V)
\]
be its projective drop.  The equality $M_4(G,V)=2$ is exactly
$M_4(G,V)=M_4(\mathrm{GL}(V),V)$.  Guralnick--Tiep
\cite[Theorem~1.7, p.~142]{GTtensor} therefore gives
$\delta_V(g)\ge d/8$ for every noncentral $g\in G$.
Its hypothesis is the fourth-moment equality; no assumption that all
primitive finite groups satisfy this bound is used.
For a noncentral branch entry,
$k_j\ge\delta_V(g_j)\ge d/8$.  By Schur's lemma a central entry is a
scalar.  It is not the identity, so $k_j=d$, which satisfies the same
lower bound.  Thus
\[
 \frac{bd}{8}\le\sum_{j=1}^{b}k_j=2d+8,
 \qquad b\le16+\frac{64}{d}.
\]
For $d\ge22$ the last quantity is less than nineteen.  Since $b$ is an
integer, $b\le18$ and the period-image dimension is at most $b-3\le15$.
The displayed sharper bound is the same calculation before discarding
the dependence on $d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:gaussian-reducible-endomorphisms}
A finite-cover source of a sixteen-dimensional Gaussian Weil-type
period image, with faithful irreducible coefficient representation and
exact character field $\mathbb Q(i)$, must satisfy
\[
 \dim V\ge50,\qquad
 M_4(G,V)\ge3.
\]
Equivalently, its trace-free endomorphism representation is reducible.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The coefficient-rank bound is
Theorem~\ref{thm:gaussian-rank34}.  Proposition~\ref{prop:fourth-moment-cover}
excludes $M_4(G,V)=2$ in all remaining ranks.  For an irreducible
representation of dimension at least two, $M_4(G,V)$ is an integer at
least two, and the equivalence with reducibility was established above.
\end{proof}

This is a restriction on the finite coefficient representation of a
curve-cover construction.  It does not classify the representations
with $M_4(G,V)>2$, and it gives no obstruction to algebraic cycles
constructed by other methods.

\subsection{A character-ratio obstruction for a normal quasisimple subgroup}
\label{subsec:quasisimple-character-ratios}

The following obstruction does not assume that the fourth moment of the
coefficient representation is two.  Its hypothesis that the restriction
to the normal quasisimple subgroup is irreducible is essential to the
argument.

\begin{lemma}[Passing a character-ratio bound through a normal subgroup]
\label{lem:normal-quasisimple-support}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, let $d=\dim_{\mathbf C}V$, and
let $L\triangleleft G$ be quasisimple.  Suppose that $V|_L$ is irreducible
and that its character $\chi$ satisfies
\[
 |\chi(h)|\le c d\qquad(h\in L\setminus Z(L)),\qquad 0\le c<1.
\]
Then every $g\in G\setminus\{1\}$ satisfies
\[
 \operatorname{rank}(g-1)\ge \frac{1-c}{4}\,d.
\]
If $G=Z(G)L$, the stronger bound
$\operatorname{rank}(g-1)\ge (1-c)d/2$ holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For any finite-order $a\in\mathrm{GL}(V)$, writing $k=\operatorname{rank}(a-1)$
and diagonalizing $a$ gives
\[
 d-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(a)
   =\sum_{\lambda\ne1}(1-\operatorname{Re}\lambda)\le2k.
\]
Consequently $\operatorname{rank}(h-1)\ge(1-c)d/2$ for
$h\in L\setminus Z(L)$.

If $g$ is not scalar, there is $t\in L$ such that $[g,t]\notin Z(L)$.
Indeed, otherwise $t\mapsto[g,t]$ is a homomorphism from the perfect group
$L$ to its abelian centre.  It is therefore trivial, and Schur's lemma
would make $g$ scalar.  The identity
\[
 [g,t]-1=(gt-tg)g^{-1}t^{-1}
\]
and the expression $gt-tg=(g-1)t-t(g-1)$ show that
\[
 \operatorname{rank}([g,t]-1)\le2\operatorname{rank}(g-1).
\]
Combining these inequalities proves the assertion for nonscalar $g$.
A nonidentity scalar has moving rank $d$ and satisfies the same bound.
This commutator estimate also appears in
\cite[Lemma~3.1]{DuyanHalasiPodoskiRandomBases}.

If $G=Z(G)L$, write $g=zh$ with $z$ scalar and $h\in L$.
For nonscalar $g$, the element $h$ is noncentral and
$|\operatorname{tr}(g)|=|\chi(h)|\le cd$.  The first displayed inequality
then gives the stronger assertion directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Restricted quasisimple finite-cover obstruction]
\label{prop:quasisimple-cover-obstruction}
Consider a family of finite covers of smooth curves and a faithful
irreducible complex coefficient representation $V$ of dimension $d\ge26$.
Suppose the relevant parabolic cohomology has complex dimension eight,
and suppose the resulting period map has image of dimension sixteen.
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be its coefficient monodromy group.
If $G$ has a normal quasisimple subgroup $L$ with $V|_L$ irreducible, then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $L/Z(L)$ cannot be a finite simple group of Lie type over
      $\mathbf F_q$ with $q\ge19$;
\item if $G=Z(G)L$, then $L/Z(L)$ cannot be a group
      of Lie type over $\mathbf F_q$ with $q\ge13$.
\end{enumerate}
Here the assertions involving a defining field use the corresponding
Lie-type presentation of the simple quotient.  No conclusion is asserted
for alternating quotients or for a restriction to $L$ with multiplicity.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Pass first to the quotient cover seen by the coefficient representation
and forget branch points with identity coefficient monodromy.  These
operations do not change the coefficient variation.  Let $\gamma$ be the
genus of the resulting base, let $b$ be the number of its genuine branch
points, and put $k_j=\operatorname{rank}(g_j-1)$ for their inertia images.
The parabolic Euler characteristic formula gives
\[
 8=2d(\gamma-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{b}k_j.
\]
Since $d\ge26$, genus at least two is impossible.  In genus one the formula
gives $b\le8$, and the corresponding pointed-curve moduli space has
dimension at most eight.  Thus a sixteen-dimensional period image forces
$\gamma=0$.  A fixed finite group and branch data give a Hurwitz space
finite over the pointed-curve moduli space, so necessarily
\[
 b\ge19,\qquad \sum_j k_j=2d+8.
\]

For a finite quasisimple group whose simple quotient is not alternating,
\cite[Theorem~4.1]{DuyanHalasiPodoskiRandomBases} gives the following bounds
for every nontrivial irreducible complex character at every noncentral
element of a Lie-type quotient with $q>9$:
\[
 \frac{|\chi(h)|}{\chi(1)}
 \le c(q):=\max\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt q-1},\frac9q\right\}.
\]
For $q\ge19$, one has $c(q)\le9/19$.  The lemma would therefore imply
\[
 2d+8=\sum_j k_j\ge19\,\frac{1-9/19}{4}\,d
    =\frac52d,
\]
whence $d\le16$, a contradiction.

Finally assume $G=Z(G)L$.  For $q\ge13$, $c(q)\le9/13$, and the stronger
part of the lemma gives
\[
 2d+8\ge19\,\frac{1-9/13}{2}\,d=\frac{38}{13}d,
 \qquad d\le\frac{26}{3}.
\]
This contradicts $d\ge26$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the obstruction]
This proposition is a restriction on a finite-cover source for the
sixteen-dimensional Gaussian unitary period domain.  It does not rule out
all finite primitive coefficient groups, prove algebraicity of Weil
classes, or construct a detector at a relative-Weyl CM point.  In
particular, small defining fields, alternating quotients, other normal
subgroup structures, and restrictions with multiplicity require separate
arguments.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A conjugate-generation refinement}

A published bound on the number of conjugates needed to generate a
sporadic almost simple group excludes those groups in every remaining
coefficient degree.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:conjugate-generation-support}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, and suppose that
$L\triangleleft G$ is quasisimple with $V|_L$ irreducible of dimension
$d>1$.  Put $S=L/Z(L)$.  Suppose that, for every nonidentity automorphism
$x\in\operatorname{Aut}(S)$ induced by $G$, at most $a$ conjugates of $x$
in $\langle S,x\rangle$ generate a subgroup containing $S$.
Then every nonidentity $g\in G$ has
\[
 \operatorname{rank}(g-1)\ge d/a.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The kernel of the conjugation map $G\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(S)$
consists of scalars.  Indeed, an element in this kernel has central
commutators with $L$; perfectness makes those commutators trivial, and
Schur's lemma then applies.  Thus $G/Z(G)$ identifies with an almost
simple subgroup of $\operatorname{Aut}(S)$ containing $S$.

Let $g$ be nonscalar and choose at most $a$ conjugates $g_1,\ldots,g_t$
whose images generate a subgroup containing $S$.  If
$H=\langle g_1,\ldots,g_t\rangle$, then $HZ(G)$ contains $L$.
Since $L$ is perfect and $Z(G)$ is central,
\[
 L=[L,L]\subset[HZ(G),HZ(G)]=[H,H]\subset H.
\]
Consequently $V^H=0$.  The intersection of the $t$ fixed spaces of the
$g_i$ is therefore zero, and the codimension inequality gives
\[
 d\le\sum_{i=1}^t\operatorname{rank}(g_i-1)
   =t\operatorname{rank}(g-1)\le a\operatorname{rank}(g-1).
\]
A nonidentity scalar has moving rank $d$, so it also satisfies the
assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:sporadic-cover-obstruction}
In the setting of Proposition~\ref{prop:quasisimple-cover-obstruction},
the simple quotient $L/Z(L)$ cannot be sporadic.  In fact, the same
conclusion holds for coefficient degree $d\ge22$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Guralnick--Saxl give $a\le8$ for all sporadic simple groups, including
outer automorphisms, in Table~1 on p.~554 of \cite{GS}, in the proof of
Lemma~7.6.  Their table bounds prime-order automorphisms, which is
sufficient: if $y$ is a nonidentity prime-order power of $x$, then any
conjugates of $y$ generating a subgroup containing $S$ are powers of
the corresponding conjugates of $x$.  Thus the same upper bound holds
for $x$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support} gives $k_j\ge d/8$ for
every genuine branch entry.  For a sphere cover this yields
\[
 b\le16+\frac{64}{d}<19\qquad(d\ge22).
\]
The period image has dimension at most $b-3\le15$.  The genus-one
and higher-genus cases are disposed of by the same Euler characteristic
argument as before.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Nonreal phases above a real normal representation]
\label{lem:real-model-phase}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, and let
$L\triangleleft G$ be quasisimple such that $V|_L$ is irreducible
and admits a real model.  Put $d=\dim_{\mathbf C}V$, assume
$\Q(i)\subseteq\Q(\chi_V)$, and suppose that the
conjugate-generation parameter for every nonidentity automorphism
of $L/Z(L)$ induced by $G$ is at most $a$.
For a sphere-cover source with compact multiplicity eight and
$b$ genuine coefficient branches,
\[
 2d+8\geq2\lceil d/2\rceil+(b-2)d/a
       \geq d+(b-2)d/a.
\]
In particular, $a\leq10$ and $d\geq34$ exclude a
sixteen-dimensional period image.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Work in a real model of $L$ and average a real positive form
$Q$ over $L$.  The positive Hermitian form obtained by averaging
over $G$ is a positive scalar multiple of $Q$, by irreducibility.
Thus $G$ preserves $Q$ as a Hermitian form.  For $g\in G$,
the real intertwining equations for its induced automorphism of
$L$ have a one-dimensional complex solution space, spanned by
$g$.  They therefore have a nonzero real solution $A$, which
is invertible, and $g=cA$.  The identity $g^*Qg=Q$ gives
$A^{\mathsf T}QA=|c|^{-2}Q$.  Hence
\[
 g=\lambda R,\qquad |\lambda|=1,
 \qquad R\in\mathrm O(Q,\mathbf R).
\]
The scalar class in $\mathbf C^*/\mathbf R^*$ is well defined
and multiplicative.  It is nontrivial on $G$, since otherwise
all character values would be real, contradicting the Gaussian
subfield.  A generating product-one branch tuple therefore has
at least two entries with nonreal scalar phase.

For such an entry $g=\lambda R$, its fixed space is the
$\lambda^{-1}$-eigenspace of the real matrix $R$.  Complex
conjugation supplies a distinct eigenspace of the same dimension,
so its moving rank is at least $\lceil d/2\rceil$.
Every other genuine entry has moving rank at least $d/a$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support}; its proof includes
all scalar and central-cover possibilities.  Summing and using
the compact Euler identity proves the inequalities.
If the period image has dimension sixteen, then $b\geq19$;
for $a\leq10$ the inequalities imply $d\leq80/7$, a
contradiction to $d\geq34$.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Degrees twenty-six through thirty-three in the quasisimple case}

The small interval immediately above the general coefficient-rank bound
can be excluded under an explicit normal-subgroup hypothesis.  No
character-field restriction is needed for this refinement.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:quasisimple-degrees26-33}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, with $V$ faithful and irreducible
of complex dimension $26\le d\le33$.  Suppose that $G$ has a normal
quasisimple subgroup $L$ such that $V|_L$ is irreducible.
A Hurwitz family with coefficient representation $V$ and parabolic
cohomology multiplicity eight has period-image dimension at most fifteen.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
As above, a sixteen-dimensional period image would require a sphere
base with genuine branch entries $g_1,\ldots,g_b$ satisfying
\[
 b\ge19,\qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^b\operatorname{rank}(g_j-1)=2d+8.
\]
If every nonidentity element moves at least $d/8$ dimensions, this gives
$b\le16+64/d<19$, a contradiction.

We use the complete low-degree classification of
\cite[Theorem~1, p.~22; Table~2, p.~31]{HissMalle}, with its
\emph{corrected} main table
\cite[Table~2, pp.~102--103]{HissMalleCorrection}.
Only characteristic-zero rows are retained.  Besides the generic
deleted-permutation representations of $A_{d+1}$ and the generic
$\mathrm{PSL}_2(q)$ families, the possible simple quotients $L/Z(L)$ are
\[
\begin{gathered}
 A_8,A_9,A_{12},A_{13},\quad
 \mathrm{PSL}_3(3),\mathrm{PSL}_3(4),\mathrm{PSL}_3(5),
 \mathrm{PSL}_4(3),\mathrm{PSL}_5(2),\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_3(3),\mathrm{PSU}_3(5),\mathrm{PSU}_4(2),
 \mathrm{Sp}_6(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_7(3),
 \mathrm{P}\Omega^+_8(2),\\
 G_2(3),{}^3D_4(2),{}^2F_4(2)',\quad \mathrm{Ru},M_{12}.
\end{gathered}
\]
The $A_{12}$ and $A_{13}$ cases occur only as faithful degree-thirty-two
representations of their double covers.  The corrected
$\mathrm{PSL}_3(3)$ rows in degrees twenty-six and twenty-seven are
included here.  The degree-twenty-six $A_9,A_{10}$ rows and the
degree-thirty-three $\mathrm{Sp}_4(4),\mathrm{P}\Omega^-_8(2)$ rows are
modular only and do not enter this list.

For all listed non-spin cases other than deleted permutation, the
following published bounds for the conjugate-generation parameter
$\alpha(x)$ suffice:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|l}
 \text{simple quotient}&\alpha(x)\le&\text{source in \cite{GS}}\\ \hline
 \mathrm{PSL}_2(q)&5&\text{Lemma 3.1}\\
 \mathrm{PSL}_3(3),\mathrm{PSL}_3(4),\mathrm{PSL}_3(5)&4&\text{Lemma 3.2}\\
 \mathrm{PSL}_4(3),\mathrm{PSL}_5(2)&6&\text{Theorems 4.1--4.2}\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_3(3),\mathrm{PSU}_3(5),\mathrm{PSU}_4(2)&6&\text{Lemmas 3.3--3.4}\\
 \mathrm{Sp}_6(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_7(3),\mathrm{P}\Omega^+_8(2)&8&\text{Theorems 4.2--4.4}\\
 G_2(3),{}^3D_4(2),{}^2F_4(2)'&7&\text{Theorem 5.1, Propositions 5.5--5.7}\\
 \mathrm{Ru},M_{12}&8&\text{Table 1, p. 554}\\
 A_8,A_9&8&\text{Lemma 6.1}.
\end{array}
\]
These bounds include induced outer automorphisms.  Passing to a
nonidentity prime-order power extends a prime-order bound to every
nonidentity automorphism.  Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support}
therefore gives moving rank at least $d/8$ for all these cases,
including their scalar and finite central-cover extensions.

For the generic deleted-permutation case, every automorphism of
$A_{d+1}$ is induced by $S_{d+1}$, since $d+1\ge27$.
The standard representation extends to $S_{d+1}$; Schur's lemma then
puts $G$ in $\mathbf C^*\operatorname{Std}(S_{d+1})$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks} excludes a
sixteen-dimensional period image in this case.

It remains to handle $L=2A_n$ with $n=12$ or $13$ and $d=32$.
If the automorphism induced by a nonscalar $g\in G$ is not a
transposition, \cite[Lemma~6.1]{GS} gives
$\alpha(g)\le n/2<7$.  The lifting argument of
Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support} gives
$\operatorname{rank}(g-1)\ge d/7$.

If $g$ induces a transposition, use the standard Schur double cover
of $S_n$ \cite[Section~4.1]{FayersMorotti}.
Let $z$ be its central involution and let $t_1,t_3$ lift
the disjoint transpositions $(12),(34)$; they satisfy
$t_1t_3=z t_3t_1$.  The preimage of $A_n$ is $2A_n$, and
$u=t_1t_3$ belongs to it.  Direct multiplication gives
\[
 t_1ut_1^{-1}=zu.
\]
An automorphism of the perfect group $2A_n$ inducing the identity
on $A_n$ is trivial: its central discrepancy is a homomorphism to
$Z(2A_n)$.  Thus, after conjugating the transposition if necessary,
the automorphism of $L$ induced by $g$ agrees with that induced by
$t_1$.  It follows that $gug^{-1}=zu$.
Faithfulness makes $z$ act as $-I$, so $u^{-1}gu=-g$ and
$\operatorname{tr}(g)=0$.  Since $g$ has finite order,
\[
 d-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(g)
 \le2\operatorname{rank}(g-1),
\]
and hence its moving rank is at least $d/2$.
This argument allows every scalar multiple of the lift and does
not assume that $V$ extends to a chosen representation of $2S_n$.

Nonidentity scalars have moving rank $d$.  Thus in both double-cover
cases every genuine branch moves at least $d/7$ dimensions.  But
\[
 72=2d+8\ge\frac{bd}{7}\ge\frac{19\cdot32}{7}>72,
\]
a final contradiction.
\end{proof}

The restriction hypothesis in this proposition is essential to its
proof. The structure theorem used below combines it with the other
coefficient restrictions to give an unrestricted Gaussian rank bound.

\subsection{The next interval of quasisimple degrees}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:quasisimple-degrees34-47}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, with $V$ faithful and
irreducible of complex dimension $34\leq d\leq47$, and assume
$\Q(i)\subseteq\Q(\chi_V)$.  Suppose that
$L\triangleleft G$ is quasisimple and $V|_L$ is irreducible.
A finite-cover family with coefficient representation $V$ and
compact cohomology multiplicity eight has period-image dimension
at most fifteen.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
As before, dimension sixteen would require a sphere base with
$b\geq19$ genuine branches and total moving rank $2d+8$.
We inspect the complete low-degree classification of
\cite[Theorem~1 and Table~2]{HissMalle}, using the corrected
main table \cite[Table~2, pp.~103--106]{HissMalleCorrection}.
The columns specifying characteristic are essential: only rows
containing characteristic zero are retained.

Besides the generic deleted-permutation representations of
$A_{d+1}$ and the generic $\mathrm{PSL}_2(q)$ families, the
possible simple quotients $S=L/Z(L)$ are
\[
\begin{gathered}
 A_7,A_8,A_9,A_{10},A_{11},\quad
 \mathrm{PSL}_3(3),\mathrm{PSL}_3(4),\mathrm{PSL}_4(3),\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_3(4),\mathrm{PSU}_3(7),
 \mathrm{PSU}_4(2),\mathrm{PSU}_4(3),
 \mathrm{PSU}_5(2),\mathrm{PSU}_7(2),\\
 \mathrm{Sp}_4(4),\mathrm{PSp}_4(5),\mathrm{PSp}_4(9),
 \mathrm{Sp}_6(2),\mathrm{Sp}_8(2),\mathrm{PSp}_8(3),
 \mathrm{P}\Omega_8^+(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_8^-(2),\ {}^2B_2(8),\\
 J_2,M_{11},M_{12},M_{22},M_{23},M_{24}.
\end{gathered}
\]
This list includes the finite central covers appearing in the
table through their simple quotients.  In particular the
degree-thirty-nine $\mathrm{PSL}_3(3)$ row added in the
corrigendum is included.  The $A_{12}$ rows in this interval,
and the degree-forty-seven $A_9$ row, are modular only.

The following bounds from \cite{GS} cover all cases except
$A_{10},A_{11},\mathrm{Sp}_8(2)$ and the generic deleted
permutation case:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|l}
 S&\alpha(x)\leq&\text{source in \cite{GS}}\\ \hline
 \mathrm{PSL}_2(q)&5&\text{Lemma 3.1}\\
 \mathrm{PSL}_3(3),\mathrm{PSL}_3(4)&4&\text{Lemma 3.2}\\
 \mathrm{PSL}_4(3)&6&\text{Theorem 4.1}\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_3(4),\mathrm{PSU}_3(7)&3&\text{Lemma 3.3}\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_4(2),\mathrm{PSU}_4(3)&6&\text{Lemma 3.4}\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_5(2),\mathrm{PSU}_7(2)&7&\text{Theorem 4.2}\\
 \mathrm{PSp}_4(4),\mathrm{PSp}_4(5),\mathrm{PSp}_4(9)&5&\text{Theorem 4.3}\\
 \mathrm{Sp}_6(2),\mathrm{PSp}_8(3)&8&\text{Theorems 4.2--4.3}\\
 \mathrm{P}\Omega_8^+(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_8^-(2)&8&\text{Theorem 4.4}\\
 {}^2B_2(8)&5&\text{Theorem 5.1}\\
 A_7,A_8,A_9&8&\text{Lemma 6.1}\\
 \text{the listed sporadic groups}&8&\text{Table 1, p. 554}.
\end{array}
\]
The bounds include induced outer automorphisms; a bound stated
for prime-order automorphisms extends to all nonidentity ones
by taking a nonidentity prime-order power.
Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support} gives moving rank
at least $d/8$.  Thus
$b\leq16+64/d<19$, a contradiction.
The generic deleted-permutation case is excluded by
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks}, as in the preceding
interval.

For the remaining three simple quotients, the
characteristic-zero rows and their Frobenius--Schur indicators
are as follows:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 S&d&\text{indicator}&\alpha(x)\leq\\ \hline
 A_{10}&35,36,42&+1&9\\
 A_{11}&44,45&+1&10\\
 \mathrm{Sp}_8(2)&35&+1&9.
\end{array}
\]
The indicator entries are in
\cite[Table~2, pp.~103--105]{HissMalleCorrection}; in each of
these rows $L$ is the simple group itself.  Indicator $+1$
supplies a real model.  The bounds in the last column follow
from \cite[Lemma~6.1 and Theorem~4.2]{GS}.
Lemma~\ref{lem:real-model-phase} applies with $a\leq10$
and gives the final contradiction.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}\label{prop:quasisimple-degree48}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, with $V$ faithful and
irreducible of complex dimension forty-eight.  Suppose that
$L\triangleleft G$ is quasisimple and $V|_L$ is irreducible.
A finite-cover family with compact coefficient multiplicity eight
has period-image dimension at most fifteen.
No character-field restriction is needed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The characteristic-zero degree-forty-eight rows of the corrected
classification \cite[Table~2, p.~106]{HissMalleCorrection},
apart from the generic families in
\cite[Table~2, p.~31]{HissMalle}, have quasisimple groups
\[
 2.A_8,\ A_9,\ 2.A_9,\ 2.A_{10},\
 12_1.\mathrm{PSL}_3(4),\
 12_2.\mathrm{PSL}_3(4),\
 3.\mathrm{PSU}_3(5),\
 2.\mathrm{Sp}_6(2).
\]
The subscripts distinguish the central covers in the table.
For their simple quotients, the respective uniform
conjugate-generation bounds are at most
$7,8,8,9,4,4,3,7$, by
\cite[Lemmas~3.2--3.3 and 6.1; Theorem~4.2]{GS}.
The generic $\mathrm{PSL}_2(q)$ families have bound five by
\cite[Lemma~3.1]{GS}.  Thus
Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support}, together with
integrality of moving ranks, gives
\[
 \operatorname{rank}(g-1)\geq\left\lceil\frac{48}{9}\right\rceil=6
\]
for every genuine inertia matrix in all these cases.
A sphere source with period dimension sixteen would require
at least nineteen such entries, whereas
$2\cdot48+8=104<19\cdot6$.

The remaining generic case is the deleted-permutation
representation of $A_{49}$, handled by
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks}.
Positive-genus bases are excluded by the general Euler and
pointed-curve bound.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}\label{prop:quasisimple-degrees50-77}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, with $V$ faithful and
irreducible of complex dimension $50\leq d\leq77$, and assume
$\Q(i)\subseteq\Q(\chi_V)$. Suppose that $L\triangleleft G$
is quasisimple and $V|_L$ is irreducible. A finite-cover family
with coefficient representation $V$ and compact multiplicity
eight has period-image dimension at most fifteen.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
As above, a sixteen-dimensional period image would require a
sphere base with $b\geq19$ genuine coefficient branches and
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^b\operatorname{rank}(g_j-1)=2d+8.
\]
We use \cite[Theorem~1, p.~22; Table~2, p.~31]{HissMalle}
with the complete corrected table
\cite[Table~2, pp.~106--110]{HissMalleCorrection}.
Only characteristic-zero rows are retained. Besides the generic
$\mathrm{PSL}_2(q)$ families and deleted-permutation modules,
the following simple quotients occur:
\[
\begin{gathered}
 A_8,A_9,A_{10},A_{12},A_{13},A_{14},A_{15},\\
 \mathrm{PSL}_3(4),\mathrm{PSL}_3(7),\mathrm{PSL}_3(8),
 \mathrm{PSL}_4(3),\mathrm{PSL}_6(2),\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_3(4),\mathrm{PSU}_3(8),\mathrm{PSU}_3(9),\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_4(2),\mathrm{PSU}_4(3),\mathrm{PSU}_4(4),\\
 \mathrm{PSU}_5(2),\mathrm{PSU}_5(3),\mathrm{PSU}_6(2),\\
 \mathrm{Sp}_4(4),\mathrm{PSp}_4(5),\mathrm{PSp}_4(11),\\
 \mathrm{Sp}_6(2),\mathrm{PSp}_6(5),\mathrm{Sp}_8(2),
 \mathrm{P}\Omega_8^+(2),\mathrm{P}\Omega_8^-(2),\\
 {}^2B_2(8),G_2(3),G_2(4),{}^3D_4(2),F_4(2),\\
 M_{11},M_{12},M_{22},J_1,J_2,\mathrm{HS},\mathrm{He},\mathrm{Suz}.
\end{gathered}
\]
Finite central covers in the table are included through their
simple quotients. In particular the ordinary degree-fifty-two
row $2.F_4(2)$ is included; it is not the Tits-group row, whose
degree-seventy-seven entry is modular only.

Except for the alternating quotients $A_n$ with $n\geq10$
and $\mathrm{Sp}_8(2)$, all these groups have
conjugate-generation bound at most eight, including induced
outer automorphisms. This follows from
\cite[Lemmas~3.1--3.4; Theorems~4.1--4.4 and 5.1;
Proposition~5.7; Lemma~6.1; Table~1, p.~554]{GS}.
The convenient bounds for the classical families are four for
$\mathrm{PSL}_3(q)$, six for $\mathrm{PSL}_4(3)$ and
$\mathrm{PSL}_6(2)$, three for the listed $\mathrm{PSU}_3(q)$,
six for $\mathrm{PSU}_4(q)$, at most six for the listed
$\mathrm{PSU}_5(q)$ and $\mathrm{PSU}_6(2)$, five for
$\mathrm{PSp}_4(q)$, seven for $\mathrm{Sp}_6(2)$, six for
$\mathrm{PSp}_6(5)$, and eight for the two orthogonal groups.
The exceptional groups have bound at most eight, including the
involution exception for $F_4(2)$ in Theorem~5.1 of that source.
The sporadic groups are also covered by
Corollary~\ref{cor:sporadic-cover-obstruction}.

Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support} would give
$2d+8\geq19d/8$, which is impossible for $d\geq50$.
The generic $\mathrm{PSL}_2(q)$ families have bound five and
are excluded in the same way. The generic deleted-permutation
case is excluded by Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks}.

The remaining ordinary rows are exactly
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 L&d&\text{Frobenius--Schur indicator}&\alpha(x)\leq\\\hline
 \mathrm{Sp}_8(2)&51&+1&9\\
 2.A_{10}&64&+1&9\\
 A_{10}&75&+1&9\\
 A_{12}&54,55&+1&11\\
 A_{13}&65,66&+1&12\\
 A_{14}&77&+1&13\\
 2.A_{14}&64&-1&13\\
 2.A_{15}&64&0&14.
\end{array}
\]
The group and indicator columns are from the corrected table;
the last column follows from Theorem~4.2 and Lemma~6.1 of
\cite{GS}. In the first three rows, integrality of the moving
rank gives respectively
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 19\left\lceil\frac{51}{9}\right\rceil&=114>110,\\
 19\left\lceil\frac{64}{9}\right\rceil&=152>136,\\
 19\left\lceil\frac{75}{9}\right\rceil&=171>158.
 \end{aligned}
\]

For the three simple alternating groups $A_{12},A_{13},A_{14}$,
the positive indicator supplies a genuine real model of $V|_L$.
The full inequality in Lemma~\ref{lem:real-model-phase}, with
$a\leq13$, gives
\[
 2d+8\geq d+17d/13=30d/13,
\]
which would force $d\leq26$, a contradiction.
This does not assume an extension of the real model to $G$:
the real-intertwiner argument in that lemma treats the whole
normalizer and all scalar phases.

Finally let $L=2.A_n$, where $n=14$ or $15$, and $d=64$.
The last two indicators are not used to infer a real model.
If the induced nonidentity automorphism of $A_n$ is not a
transposition, \cite[Lemma~6.1]{GS} gives
$\alpha(x)\leq n/2\leq15/2<8$; the weaker bound eight suffices.
The lifting argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugate-generation-support}
then gives moving rank at least $d/8$.

For a transposition, take lifts $t_1,t_3$ of $(12),(34)$ in
a Schur double cover of $S_n$, with central involution $z$
\cite[Section~4.1]{FayersMorotti}. They satisfy $t_1t_3=zt_3t_1$. The element $u=t_1t_3$ belongs
to the preimage $2.A_n$, and
\[
 t_1ut_1^{-1}=zu.
\]
The automorphism of $L$ induced by the actual inertia matrix
$g$ agrees, after conjugating the transposition, with that induced
by $t_1$. Indeed, their quotient induces the identity on $A_n$;
its central discrepancy is a homomorphism from the perfect
$L$ to $Z(L)$, and is trivial. Faithfulness gives $z=-I$.
Consequently $u^{-1}gu=-g$, so $\operatorname{tr}(g)=0$ even
for an arbitrary scalar multiple of the lift. Finite order gives
\[
 d-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(g)
 \leq2\operatorname{rank}(g-1),
\]
hence moving rank at least $d/2$. No representation of a
prescribed double cover of $S_n$ is assumed to extend $V|_L$.
A nonidentity scalar moves all of $V$. Thus every genuine branch
in the two remaining cases moves at least eight dimensions,
whereas $2d+8=136<19\cdot8=152$. This completes the list.
\end{proof}


\subsection{A normal subgroup of class two}

The commutator estimate applies to more than extraspecial groups.
It also covers the cyclic-center groups of symplectic type that
occur in primitive linear-group structure theorems.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:extraspecial-cover-obstruction}
Let $G\subset\mathrm{GL}(V)$ be finite, let
$d=\dim_{\mathbf C}V\geq26$, and suppose that $G$ has a normal
subgroup $E$ such that $[E,E]\subseteq Z(E)$ and $V|_E$ is
irreducible.  A finite-cover family with coefficient
representation $V$ and compact cohomology multiplicity eight
has period-image dimension at most six.  For a sphere base the
bound is
\[
 \left\lfloor8+\frac{32}{d}\right\rfloor-3\leq6.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Irreducibility and faithfulness make every nonidentity element of
$Z(E)$ a nonidentity scalar.  If $e\notin Z(E)$, choose $t\in E$
with $[t,e]=z\ne1$.  The class-two hypothesis makes $z$ central,
so it acts by a scalar $\zeta\ne1$.  Consequently
\[
 \operatorname{tr}(e)=\operatorname{tr}(tet^{-1})
   =\zeta\operatorname{tr}(e),\qquad
 \operatorname{tr}(e)=0.
\]
Since $e$ has finite order, its eigenvalues have modulus one, and
\[
 d=d-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(e)
       \leq2\operatorname{rank}(e-1).
\]
A nonidentity central element moves all $d$ dimensions.
Thus every nonidentity element of $E$ has moving rank at least
$d/2$.

For a nonscalar $g\in G$, Schur's lemma gives $t\in E$ with
$[g,t]\ne1$.  Normality puts this commutator in $E$.  The rank
inequality
\[
 \frac d2\leq\operatorname{rank}([g,t]-1)
       \leq2\operatorname{rank}(g-1)
\]
therefore gives moving rank at least $d/4$ for $g$.
Nonidentity scalars satisfy the same lower bound.
This is the normal-subgroup support argument of
\cite[Lemma~3.1]{DuyanHalasiPodoskiRandomBases}, with its
elementary proof included here.

At base genus $\gamma$, with $b$ genuine coefficient branches,
the Euler formula is
\[
 8=2d(\gamma-1)+\sum_{j=1}^b c_j,
 \qquad c_j\geq d/4.
\]
Genus at least two is impossible because $2d>8$.
At genus one, $b\leq\lfloor32/d\rfloor\leq1$, and the
pointed-curve dimension is at most one, including an unmarked
elliptic base.  At genus zero,
$bd/4\leq2d+8$, and the dimension bound $b-3$ gives the result.
\end{proof}

The irreducibility of $V|_E$ remains essential to this argument.
A normal subgroup acting with multiplicity need not have scalar
centralizer.  No assertion about such a restriction is made in
this proposition.


\subsection{A uniform Gaussian coefficient-rank bound}

The preceding restrictions can now be assembled using a published
structure theorem for primitive finite linear groups.  This step
does not assume that a chosen normal subgroup acts irreducibly;
that condition is supplied by the structure theorem after the
ordered tensor case has been excluded.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:gaussian-rank34}
Let $V$ be a nontrivial irreducible complex coefficient
representation of a finite group, with
$\Q(i)\subseteq\Q(\chi_V)$.  Suppose that a finite-cover
family has compact $V$-multiplicity of dimension eight, Hodge
numbers $(4,4)$, and period image of dimension sixteen.  Then
\[
                         \dim_{\mathbf C}V\geq78.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose that a source contradicts the conclusion, and choose one
of least coefficient dimension $d<78$ among all sources satisfying
the hypotheses. Pass to the faithful coefficient image $G$ and
fill in points with identity coefficient inertia. The reflection
theorem and Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-cover-rank} give $d\geq26$.
If $V$ were imprimitive, write it as $\operatorname{Ind}_H^G U$
from a block stabilizer. The block representation $U$ is irreducible;
it is nontrivial because induction of the trivial representation
contains constants. Compact Shapiro identifies the full multiplicity
Hodge variation, retaining dimension eight, Hodge numbers $(4,4)$
and period-image dimension sixteen. Equation
\eqref{eq:induced-character-field-inclusion} puts $\Q(i)$ in
$\Q(\chi_U)$. Passing to the faithful image of $U$ therefore
gives another source satisfying the theorem's hypotheses, of
strictly smaller coefficient dimension. This contradicts the
choice of $d$. Thus $G$ is primitive.
The positive-genus bound shows that the coefficient base is a
sphere. Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-small-factors78} excludes every
ordered tensor decomposition, since such a source would have
degree at least seventy-eight.

We use \cite[Lemma~6.3, p.~375]{ZalesskiEigenvalues}.
For a finite irreducible primitive complex group with no ordered
tensor decomposition, that lemma supplies one of the following:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item an irreducibly acting normal subgroup $N$ that is a
 central product of quasisimple groups permuted transitively
 by $G$;
 \item an irreducibly acting normal $p$-subgroup $E$ such that
 $EZ(G)/Z(G)$ is elementary abelian.
\end{enumerate}
The convention is the ordered tensor convention: the paragraph
following Lemma~6.8 on p.~376 of the same source defines tensor
decomposability by containment in
$\mathrm{GL}(V_1)\otimes\mathrm{GL}(V_2)$.
In particular the cited lemma includes the possibility of
permuted tensor factors.

In the second case,
$[E,E]\subseteq E\cap Z(G)\subseteq Z(E)$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:extraspecial-cover-obstruction}, in its
class-two form, applies and contradicts dimension sixteen.
This does not require a chosen extraspecial subgroup of $E$ to
be normal in $G$.

In the first case write $N=L_1\cdots L_t$ as the indicated
central product.  Pulling the irreducible representation back to
$L_1\times\cdots\times L_t$ gives
\[
 V\simeq U_1\otimes\cdots\otimes U_t,
\]
where $U_j$ is irreducible for $L_j$.  Every $U_j$ has dimension
at least two: the coefficient image is faithful and a quasisimple
group has no nontrivial one-dimensional representation.
Transitivity on the factors gives a common dimension $a$.
Moreover, $G$ preserves this tensor structure up to permutation.
Indeed, after permuting the factors according to conjugation,
the action on each factor can be implemented by an intertwiner;
the quotient of the resulting tensor map and the given element
centralizes the irreducibly acting $N$, and is scalar by Schur's
lemma.

If $t\geq2$, Proposition~\ref{prop:permuted-tensor100}
would require $d\geq100$, a contradiction. If $t=1$, $N$ is
a normal quasisimple group acting irreducibly. Degrees twenty-six
through thirty-three are excluded by
Proposition~\ref{prop:quasisimple-degrees26-33}; degrees thirty-four
through forty-seven by Proposition~\ref{prop:quasisimple-degrees34-47};
and degree forty-eight by Proposition~\ref{prop:quasisimple-degree48}.

In degree forty-nine every nongeneric row of
\cite[Table~2, p.~106]{HissMalleCorrection} is modular only:
the listed characteristics are $17,3,3,11,3$ for
$\mathrm{Sp}_4(4),\mathrm{Sp}_6(2),M_{22},J_1,\mathrm{HS}$,
respectively. The characteristic-zero possibilities are the
generic deleted-permutation representation of $A_{50}$ and the
generic $\mathrm{PSL}_2(q)$ families in
\cite[Table~2, p.~31]{HissMalle}. The former is excluded by
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-standard-all-ranks}; the latter have
conjugate-generation bound five \cite[Lemma~3.1]{GS}, giving
moving rank at least $\lceil49/5\rceil=10$ and fewer than
nineteen genuine branches. This excludes degree forty-nine.
Proposition~\ref{prop:quasisimple-degrees50-77} excludes every
remaining degree from fifty through seventy-seven. No possible
$26\leq d<78$ remains, contradicting the choice of the source.
Thus $d\geq78$.

\end{proof}

The theorem is a necessary condition on finite-cover
constructions.  It neither gives a generating branch tuple nor
constructs a dominant Gaussian family or an algebraic Weil
detector.


\subsection{Induction preserves an existing period variation}

\begin{proposition}[Induction through a rational cover]
\label{prop:wreath-inflation}
Suppose a family of finite Galois covers of $X=\mathbf P^1$
has group $F$ and a faithful nontrivial irreducible complex
coefficient representation $W$ of degree $r$.  For every integer
$t\geq2$, after restricting to a nonempty open set and making
a finite base change if necessary, there is a family of finite
Galois covers of another projective line with coefficient group
$F\wr S_t$ in its natural irreducible block representation $V$
of degree $rt$, such that
\[
 \Q(\chi_V)=\Q(\chi_W),\qquad
 H^1_{\rm par}(\mathbf P^1,V)
       \simeq H^1_{\rm par}(\mathbf P^1,W)
\]
as complex Hodge variations.  A field of definition for $W$ is
also a field of definition for $V$.  In particular the period
image has the same dimension.  If $W$ has reflection-free image
and $r\geq2$, then $V$ also has reflection-free image.

This is a conditional construction from a given source; it does
not assert the existence of a dominant Gaussian source.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $a_1,\ldots,a_b\in X$ be the genuine coefficient branch
points.  Choose a degree-$t$ rational map $q:X\to Y=\mathbf P^1$
with simple ramification and distinct critical values, such that
the values $q(a_j)$ are distinct and avoid the critical values.
These are nonempty open conditions on a rational map in
characteristic zero.  There are $2t-2$ simple critical points.
After removing the critical values and the $q(a_j)$ from $Y$,
the pushforward of the coefficient local system is a rank-$rt$
local system $V$, with monodromy in $F\wr S_t$.

We check that its monodromy is the full wreath product.  The
permutation quotient is the monodromy of $q$.  It is transitive
and generated by transpositions, so it is $S_t$.  Choose a point
$x_0$ over the base point of $Y$ and paths from $x_0$ to the
$a_j$ giving an inertia generating tuple for the original
$F$-cover.  The paths can avoid the finite set of preimages of
all other removed values.  Push these paths down under $q$.
A resulting conjugate of a small loop around $q(a_j)$ acts by
the original inertia generator in the $x_0$ block and by the
identity in every other block.  Indeed, its other lifted small
loops surround points where the original coefficient system is
unramified; transport along a path and its reverse cancels.
Thus the monodromy contains $F$ acting in one block.  Conjugation
by elements of the transitive permutation quotient supplies
$F$ independently in each block.  The kernel therefore contains
$F^t$, proving that the full monodromy is $F\wr S_t$.

The natural block representation is irreducible.  Its restriction
to $F^t$ is the direct sum of $t$ pairwise inequivalent
irreducible modules, each supported on one factor.  The quotient
$S_t$ permutes these modules transitively.  Complete reducibility
and this transitivity exclude a proper invariant subspace.

The trace of any wreath-product matrix is the sum of the
$W$-traces attached to its fixed blocks, so
$\Q(\chi_V)\subseteq\Q(\chi_W)$.  Conversely,
$\operatorname{diag}(f,I,\ldots,I)$ has trace
$\chi_W(f)+(t-1)r$.  This proves equality of the character
fields.  The block matrices also show that every field of
definition of $W$ defines $V$.

Finite pushforward commutes with middle extension in this
situation: at a removed point, both stalks are the corresponding
inertia-invariant spaces.  The finite map has no higher direct
images, so
\[
 H^1(Y,j_*V)=H^1(X,j_*W).
\]
The identification is induced by the finite covering
correspondence.  It respects the Hodge filtration and
Gauss--Manin connection, and hence gives the asserted isomorphism
of variations.  It proves equality of their period-image
dimensions; it does not assert that a prescribed principal
polarization or integral marking is unchanged.

At $q(a_j)$ the moving rank is the original moving rank $c_j$.
At a simple critical value of $q$, inertia interchanges two
blocks with identity return map, so its moving rank is $r$.
For example, if the original compact multiplicity is eight,
the new moving-rank sum is
\[
 \sum_j c_j+(2t-2)r=(2r+8)+(2t-2)r=2rt+8,
\]
as required.  If $W$ is reflection-free, every nonidentity
block-diagonal matrix moves at least two dimensions.  A
nontrivial block permutation moves at least $r$ dimensions:
on a cycle of length $\ell\geq2$, its fixed space has dimension
at most $r$ in a space of dimension $r\ell$.  Thus $V$ is
reflection-free when $r\geq2$.

Taking the Galois closure of the composite original cover with
$q$ realizes this local system by a finite Galois cover of $Y$.
For families, choose $q$ at one point of the parameter space;
the separation and simple-ramification conditions persist on a
nonempty open set.  The Galois closures form a family after
finite base change, equivalently by passage to the corresponding
Hurwitz cover.  The pushforward isomorphism holds throughout
that family.
\end{proof}

Thus a hypothetical dominant primitive Gaussian source would
yield induced sources of all coefficient degrees $tr$ with the
same period variation and character field.  A cover tower alone
cannot exclude every imprimitive source.  The inducing-rank
restriction remains useful because it reduces the question to
primitive complex coefficient systems of sufficiently large
degree.


\section{The quadratic relations of a general Gaussian eightfold}
\label{sec:theta-eightfolds}
This section tests the most direct extension of van Geemen's fourfold construction and identifies exactly where that extension stops.

For an even principal Gaussian abelian variety of dimension $2n$,
with the theta structure used in \cite{vG96}, van Geemen's
Corollary~2.4 gives
\[
 \dim H^0(A,2\Theta)_-=2^{n-1}(2^n-1).
\]
Here the subscript denotes the minus eigenspace of the induced
involution on second-order theta sections.
His Lemma~3.3 identifies the base locus of the minus projection on an indecomposable member as $2^{2n}$ two-torsion points \cite[pp.~621--622]{vG96}. For $n=2$, Proposition~3.6 produces a smooth quadric fourfold in the resulting $\mathbf P^5$; Theorem~3.7 then proves that the pullback of a ruling plane detects the Weil classes. For $n=4$, the same general statements give $120$ sections, a map to $\mathbf P^{119}$, and $256$ base points. They do not give a quadric eightfold in $\mathbf P^9$.

We examine the precise space of quadratic relations obtained by applying the same symmetry to the theta identities. Put $G=\mathbf F_2^4$ and let $V$ have basis $(e_u)_{u\in G}$. The second-order theta coordinates form a matrix $(X_{uv})_{u,v\in G}$. The involution transposes this matrix, so its minus eigenspace identifies with $\bigwedge^2V$. The vanishing theta constants in \cite[Lemma~2.3 and \S3.5]{vG96} give the $120$ quadratic forms
\begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-forced}
 Q_{a,b}(X)=\sum_{u,v\in G}(-1)^{(u+v)\cdot b}
 X_{uv}X_{u+a,v+a},\qquad a\cdot b=1.
\end{equation}
A linear combination descends through the minus projection when it vanishes on the plus space of symmetric matrices. There are no mixed terms, since each form is invariant under transposition.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:theta-rank}
The combinations of \eqref{eq:theta-forced} that descend to the minus eigenspace form a vector space of dimension $35$. Every nonzero form in this space has rank at least $24$, and rank $24$ occurs. In particular this space contains no rank-$10$ quadric.
\end{theorem}

We first prove this linear-algebra assertion. We then show that these
are all the quadratic relations of the minus projection on a nonempty
Zariski-open subset of the even principal Gaussian family.

\begin{proof}
For $a\cdot b=1$ define the skew matrix
\[
 (P_{a,b})_{u,w}=(-1)^{u\cdot b}\,\mathbf1_{w=u+a}.
\]
For fixed nonzero $a$, the eight permitted characters form a basis of the skew matrices supported on displacement $a$. Different displacements have disjoint support. Consequently these $120$ matrices form a basis of $\bigwedge^2V^*$.

Associate to $Q=\sum c_{a,b}Q_{a,b}$ the symmetric tensor
$T=\sum c_{a,b}P_{a,b}\otimes P_{a,b}$ on $\bigwedge^2V$. Substitution gives
\[
 Q(vv^{\mathsf t}+ww^{\mathsf t})=2T(v\wedge w,v\wedge w).
\]
Since every complex symmetric matrix is a sum of rank-one symmetric matrices, $Q$ vanishes on the symmetric matrices if and only if $T(v\wedge w,v\wedge w)=0$ for every $v,w$. Polarizing first in $w$ and then in $v$ gives
\[
 T(x\wedge y,x\wedge z)=0,\qquad
 T(x\wedge y,w\wedge z)=-T(w\wedge y,x\wedge z).
\]
Together with the two wedge antisymmetries, these identities say that $T$ is induced by an alternating four-form $\xi\in\bigwedge^4V^*$:
\[
 T_\xi(x\wedge y,z\wedge w)=\xi(x,y,z,w).
\]
Exchanging the middle two indices shows that the restriction of $Q$ to the skew matrices is a nonzero scalar multiple of this form. Thus their ranks agree.

Let $\tau_s e_u=e_{u+s}$ and $\epsilon_t e_u=(-1)^{t\cdot u}e_u$. On the skew matrices $P_{a,b}$ these operations have characters $(-1)^{b\cdot s}$ and $(-1)^{a\cdot t}$. The pairs of characters are distinct. Hence their invariant symmetric tensors are exactly the diagonal tensors $\sum c_{a,b}P_{a,b}^2$. It follows that the descending forms identify with the four-forms invariant under every $\tau_s$ and $\epsilon_t$.

Sign invariance forces $\xi(e_u,e_v,e_w,e_z)=0$ unless $u+v+w+z=0$. For distinct indices this means that they form an affine two-plane. Translation identifies the coefficients on the four cosets of a fixed two-dimensional subspace $P\subset G$. Translation inside $P$ is a double transposition on its four vertices and preserves its wedge orientation. There is therefore one free coefficient $c_P$ for each such subspace. Their number is
\[
 \frac{(16-1)(16-2)}{(4-1)(4-2)}=35.
\]

It remains to prove the sharp rank bound. Call $P$ active when $c_P\ne0$, let $U$ be the union of the nonzero vectors in the active planes, and put $m=|U|$. For $a\in U$, let $k_a$ count the active planes through $a$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:plane-incidence}
 1\le k_a\le\left\lfloor\frac{m-1}{2}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad \sum_{a\in U}k_a=3\#\{P:c_P\ne0\}.
\end{equation}
The first bound holds because the other two nonzero vectors in different planes through $a$ are disjoint pairs.

There is an orthogonal decomposition
\[
 \bigwedge^2V=\bigoplus_{a\ne0}E_a,
 \qquad E_a=\langle e_x\wedge e_{x+a}:x\in G\rangle,
 \quad \dim E_a=8.
\]
Indeed the sign characters distinguish these summands. Choose a complement $H$ to $\langle a\rangle$ in $G$. In the basis $f_x=e_x\wedge e_{x+a}$, $x\in H$, translation invariance gives the convolution matrix
\[
 T_\xi(f_x,f_y)=b_a(y-x),\qquad
 b_a(z)=\xi(e_0,e_a,e_z,e_{z+a}).
\]
Here $H\cong\mathbf F_2^3$, $b_a(0)=0$, and $b_a$ has precisely $k_a$ nonzero values. Its eight character vectors diagonalize the matrix. If $r_a$ is its rank, then $r_a$ is the size of the Fourier support of $b_a$, and the total rank is $\sum_{a\in U}r_a$.

We record the required elementary Fourier facts. If a nonzero function $b$ on $\mathbf F_2^3$ satisfies $b(0)=0$, write $k=|\operatorname{supp}b|$ and $r=|\operatorname{supp}\widehat b|$. Then
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
\text{condition}&k=1&k=2&k=3&\text{any }k&r=2&r=3\\
\hline
\text{conclusion}&r=8&r\ge4&r\ge6&r\ge2&k=4&k=6.
\end{array}
\]
For two terms, the Fourier values have the forms $c_1+c_2$ and $c_1-c_2$, each four times. For three terms, factor out one character. The remaining two character differences are independent, so the four values are $c_1\pm c_2\pm c_3$, each twice. At most one vanishes, since two vanishing values would force a coefficient to be zero. A Fourier support of size one contradicts $b(0)=0$. A Fourier support of size two gives $b=c(\chi_1-\chi_2)$, supported on four points. For a Fourier support of size three apply the three-term argument to Fourier inversion: the value at zero vanishes, so exactly one of the four patterns vanishes, giving support size six. In particular a block with $k\le5$ has rank at least four unless $k=4$, when rank two is possible.

We now bound the sum of the ranks by considering $m$. If $m=3$, one plane is active and its three blocks have rank eight, giving $24$. The value $m=4$ is impossible. If $m=5$, exactly two planes are active and meet in one nonzero vector. No third plane can be contained in their union: a vector space cannot be covered by two proper subspaces. Four blocks have $k=1$, and the remaining block has $k=2$, giving rank at least $36$.

If $6\le m\le8$, every $k_a\le3$, so every active block has rank at least four and the total is at least $24$. If $m=9$ and $k_a=4$, the four planes through $a$ pair the other eight elements of $U$ under translation by $a$. Thus $U\cup\{0\}$, of size ten, is translation invariant under $a$. Its translation stabilizer has order a power of two dividing ten, and therefore has order at most two. At most one $a$ can have $k_a=4$. The rank is at least $8\cdot4+2=34$.

For $m=10$, the baseline rank is $20$. A total below $24$ would exclude $k=1$ and $k=3$ blocks, whose excesses above two are at least six and four. It would permit at most one $k=2$ block. All other $k$'s would be four, so $\sum k_a$ would be $40$ or $38$, contrary to divisibility by three in \eqref{eq:plane-incidence}. For $m=11$, $k_a\le5$, so rank three is impossible. A total below $24$ would make every block rank two and every $k_a=4$, giving the impossible sum $44$. Finally, if $m\ge12$, the rank is at least $2m\ge24$.

To attain $24$, take the invariant orbit sum for a single two-plane $P$. Its four terms are volume forms on the four disjoint coordinate four-spaces indexed by its affine cosets. Each volume form induces a nondegenerate pairing of rank six on the exterior square of its four-space. Mixed wedges are in the radical. The resulting rank is $4\cdot6=24$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:generic-gaussian-theta-nulls}
Let $n\ge2$ and consider the even principal Gaussian period domain
\[
 \mathcal D_n=
 \left\{\tau=
 \begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\-Y&X\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak H_{2n}:
 X^{\mathsf t}=X,\ Y^{\mathsf t}=-Y\right\}.
\]
On a nonempty open subset, the only vanishing even theta constants
are those whose two genus-$n$ characteristics are equal and odd.
Their number is $2^{n-1}(2^n-1)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write a characteristic as a pair $(m_1,m_2)$, where
$m_j=(a_j,b_j)\in\mathbf F_2^n\times\mathbf F_2^n$ and its parity is
$a_j\cdot b_j$. An even characteristic of genus $2n$ has the same
parity in the two factors. Changing summation variables
$(u,v)\mapsto(-v,u)$ in the theta series shows
\[
 \vartheta_{m_1,m_2}(\tau,0)
   =(-1)^{a_2\cdot b_2}\vartheta_{m_2,m_1}(\tau,0).
\]
Thus the equal odd pairs vanish identically, as in
\cite[Lemma~2.3]{vG96}.

At $Y=0$ the constant factors as
$\vartheta_{m_1}(X,0)\vartheta_{m_2}(X,0)$.
Every even theta constant is a nonzero holomorphic function on
$\mathfrak H_n$: symplectic change of marking reduces it to the
zero characteristic, nonzero at a product of elliptic periods.
The product of two nonzero holomorphic functions on this connected
domain is nonzero. Therefore a pair of even characteristics does
not vanish identically on $\mathcal D_n$.

Suppose $m_1,m_2$ are distinct odd characteristics, and put
$g_m(X)=\nabla_z\vartheta_m(X,0)$.
Differentiating the convergent theta series in a skew matrix
direction $B$ gives, at $Y=0$,
\[
 d_Y\vartheta_{m_1,m_2}(X,0)[B]
   =\frac{1}{2\pi i}\,g_{m_1}(X)^{\mathsf t}B\,g_{m_2}(X).
\]
It suffices to find one $X$ where these two gradients are not
proportional.

The integral symplectic group is transitive on ordered pairs of
distinct odd characteristics. Here is the finite-dimensional reason.
Characteristics are quadratic refinements of the symplectic form
on $\mathbf F_2^{2n}$, and parity is their Arf invariant.
Two odd refinements differ by pairing with a unique nonzero vector
$v$ isotropic for the first refinement. The orthogonal group of
that odd refinement is transitive on its nonzero isotropic vectors:
complete $v$ to a hyperbolic pair, and identify the orthogonal
complements using the standard normal form of a nonsingular
quadratic form over $\mathbf F_2$. The finite symplectic changes
lift to integral ones, since symplectic transvections generate
the group and lift integrally.

We may consequently reduce to two odd characteristics with their
only odd elliptic component in the first and second coordinates,
respectively. At a product of elliptic periods their gradients
are nonzero multiples of the first and second coordinate vectors.
Indeed the odd elliptic theta function has a simple zero at the
origin and each even elliptic theta constant is nonzero.
The theta transformation formula takes an odd gradient to a
nonzero scalar times one common invertible linear transformation
of that gradient; its other differentiated term is zero because
the odd theta constant is zero. Independence of the two gradients
therefore persists after undoing the change of marking.
The displayed derivative is nonzero for a suitable skew $B$.

Every even theta constant other than an equal odd pair is thus
nonzero somewhere on the connected domain $\mathcal D_n$.
There are only finitely many such constants, so the complement
of their zero loci is nonempty and open. The algebraic form of
the conclusion also follows below from the rank of the
multiplication map.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:all-eightfold-quadrics}
On a nonempty Zariski-open subset of each marked even principal
Gaussian eightfold component, the entire quadratic ideal of the
minus-projected image in $\mathbf P^{119}$ is the
$35$-dimensional space in Theorem~\ref{thm:theta-rank}.
Every nonzero quadric in that ideal has rank at least $24$.
In particular no ten sections of this minus eigenspace can map
the general fibre dominantly to a smooth quadric eightfold.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $g=8$ and let $X_\sigma$, $\sigma\in\mathbf F_2^g$, be the
second-order theta coordinates. The quadratic forms
\[
 Q_{a,b}=\sum_\sigma(-1)^{\sigma\cdot b}
                 X_\sigma X_{\sigma+a},
 \qquad a\cdot b=0,
\]
form a basis of all quadratic forms: for a fixed displacement $a$,
the permitted characters form the Fourier basis on the unordered
pairs $\{\sigma,\sigma+a\}$; when $a=0$ they form the basis on
the squares. The addition formula
\cite[\S3.5, pp.~622--623]{vG96} says
\[
 Q_{a,b}\bigl(\vartheta[\sigma;0](2\tau,2z)\bigr)
 =\vartheta[a;b](\tau,0)\,\vartheta[a;b](\tau,2z).
\]
The sections $\vartheta[a;b](\tau,2z)$, as $(a,b)$ ranges over the
even characteristics, form a basis of the even sections of $4\Theta$.
It follows that the full Kummer quadratic ideal is precisely the
span of the quadrics for vanishing even theta constants.

Lemma~\ref{lem:generic-gaussian-theta-nulls} makes this kernel,
on a nonempty open set, the span of the $120$ forms
\eqref{eq:theta-forced}. A quadric on the minus projection
pulls back to a polynomial involving only minus coordinates.
Its vanishing is equivalent to membership in the full kernel.
The intersection of that kernel with these polynomials is
exactly the $35$-dimensional space already computed.
Conversely every such polynomial vanishes on the projected image.

After a finite level cover the spaces of sections and their
multiplication are algebraic vector bundles and an algebraic
bundle map. The minimal kernel dimension is attained on a
Zariski-open set. Thus the nonempty analytic open just found
gives the asserted nonempty Zariski-open set.
Ten independent sections defining a map to a smooth $Q^8$
would pull its equation back to a nonzero rank-$10$ quadratic
form on the minus space, contradicting the rank bound.
\end{proof}

The smallest nonzero relation on this open set has a smooth
quotient quadric of dimension $22$. Its degree-eight cohomology
is below the middle and is generated by a hyperplane power.
Pulling back that class gives a multiple of $\theta^4$, whose
Weil projection is zero. Thus the direct ruling-class argument
cannot use any quadric of the general minus projection.
Special fibres may have additional relations. Cycles involving
singular loci, sections outside the minus space, and different
target varieties require other arguments.

Write $\delta_W(A,L)$ for the least degree of a reduced irreducible
Weil detector. Write $m_W(A,L)$ for the least positive integer $m$
such that four divisors in $|mL|$ meet properly and their intersection
has a detecting component; either minimum is infinite if no such
cycle exists. These are the conventions of
\cite[Proposition~\compref{prop:complete-intersections}]{CompanionA}.

\begin{proposition}[What a quadric eightfold would give]\label{prop:quadric-gate}
Let $(A,L)$ be a Weil eightfold and $\Delta=\chi(L)$. Suppose ten sections of $2L$ define a dominant rational map to a smooth quadric $Q^8\subset\mathbf P^9$, with base locus of dimension at most three. Suppose that, for a general member $P\cong\mathbf P^4$ of one ruling, the closure of its inverse image has nonzero Weil projection. Then
\[
 m_W(A,L)\le2,\qquad \delta_W(A,L)\le8\cdot8!\Delta=322560\Delta.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A general $\mathbf P^5$ containing a ruling $P$ cuts $Q^8$ in $P\cup P'$, where $P'$ is a member of the other ruling. The $\mathbf P^5$ is cut out by four hyperplanes in $\mathbf P^9$. By transitivity of the orthogonal group and the dimension theorem for a general translate, the inverse images of a general such pair of ruling planes have dimension four away from the base locus. The base locus has dimension at most three. Thus the four corresponding members of $|2L|$ have proper intersection. Their intersection contains the closure of the inverse image of $P$, with positive multiplicities. Its nonzero Weil projection implies that one irreducible component detects. Proposition~\compref{prop:complete-intersections} gives
$\delta_W\le(8!\Delta/2)2^4=8\cdot8!\Delta$.
\end{proof}

The geometric hypotheses of this proposition remain unproved in
the Gaussian family. Corollary~\ref{cor:all-eightfold-quadrics}
excludes obtaining its rank-$10$ quadric from any ten sections
of the general minus eigenspace.
\section{A Weil character whose perfect representative is obstructed}
\label{sec:equivariant-eightfolds}
This section separates the construction of a Weil class in $K$-theory from the deformation of a perfect complex representing it.

Perry's Theorem~1.2 \cite{Perry}, an unrefereed preprint, extends the semiregularity method used by Markman. In a family of abelian varieties it permits a perfect complex $E$ with $\operatorname{Ext}^{<0}(E,E)=0$, a finite group $G\subset A\times A^\vee$ acting by translations and degree-zero line bundles, and weak $G$-semiregularity. If the prescribed twisted Chern character remains Hodge, the theorem deforms $E$ as a twisted perfect complex and propagates algebraicity. An isolated CM automorphism is not automatically one of the permitted group actions. More fundamentally, a virtual class with the right Chern character need not have a semiregular representative.

We give an explicit calculation at the split Gaussian eightfold
\[
 A_0=X\times Y,
 \qquad X=Y=E_i^4,
 \qquad a(x,y)=(ax,\bar a y),
\]
with product principal polarization. Its Hermitian lattice is $I_{4,4}$. Let $\Delta\subset X\times Y$ be the diagonal. If $a=2+i$, write $S=a^*+\bar a^*$ for the sum of pullback operations on coherent $K$-theory, and put
\[
 P(s)=(s+1170)(s+350)(s-750)(s-1250).
\]

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:graph-obstruction}
The integral class $v=P(S)[\mathcal O_\Delta]$ has a perfect representative $E_v$ satisfying
\[
 \operatorname{ch}(E_v)=-339429556224\,e_W[\Delta]\ne0,
 \qquad \operatorname{Ext}^{<0}(E_v,E_v)=0.
\]
Its whole Chern character remains Hodge along the signature-$(4,4)$ unitary domain. Nevertheless its first-order obstruction map on that domain has rank six. No infinitesimal gerbe twist removes those six normal obstruction directions. For every finite translation and degree-zero-twist action under which $E_v$ is equivariant, they form a six-dimensional subspace of the kernel of the invariant semiregularity map.
\end{proposition}

The representative in the proposition is the direct sum of the graph structure sheaves in the signed expansion of $v$, with a shift by one on the negative summands. Gluability is asserted for this representative. The normal-obstruction calculation also applies to its translates, degree-zero twists and finite equivariant inductions. It does not give a nonexistence assertion for every perfect complex with a Weil component.

\begin{proof}
Choose coordinates $x_1,\ldots,x_4$ and $y_1,\ldots,y_4$ on the two tangent spaces. The polarized $K$-linear deformation tangent space consists of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unitary-tangent}
 \kappa_B=\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\B^{\mathsf t}&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad B\in M_4(\C).
\end{equation}
Indeed commuting with the complex multiplication action kills the two diagonal Beltrami blocks; compatibility with the polarization makes the full matrix symmetric. The resulting space has dimension $16$.

For a graph with tangent equation $y=Tx$, the normal quotient is $(v_x,v_y)\mapsto v_y-Tv_x$. Restricting \eqref{eq:unitary-tangent} to its antiholomorphic tangent and taking this quotient gives the normal obstruction
\[
 \nu_T(B)=B^{\mathsf t}-TB\bar T
 \in H^1(\Gamma_T,N_{\Gamma_T/A_0}).
\]
In particular, for the diagonal and for every scalar graph of norm-one slope,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:graph-normal-obstruction}
 \nu_{\lambda I}(B)=B^{\mathsf t}-B,
 \qquad \lambda\bar\lambda=1.
\end{equation}
Its kernel is the ten-dimensional space of symmetric matrices and its rank is six. The matrix $B=E_{12}$ gives the nonzero example $E_{21}-E_{12}$.

We check that this is an obstruction for the structure sheaf itself. The automorphism $(x,y)\mapsto(x,y-x)$ sends the diagonal to $X\times\{0\}$. K\"unneth and the Koszul resolution of the point of $Y$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-ext}
 \operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal O_\Delta,\mathcal O_\Delta)
 =H^2(X,\mathcal O_X)
 \oplus\bigl(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\otimes T_{0,Y}\bigr)
 \oplus\bigwedge^2T_{0,Y}.
\end{equation}
The dimensions are $6,16,6$. Huybrechts--Thomas \cite[Corollary~3.4, manuscript p.~14]{HT} identify the obstruction of a perfect complex as the product of its Atiyah class with the Kodaira--Spencer class. In normal coordinates $u=y-x$, the Koszul Atiyah class has normal symbol $\sum_jdu_j\otimes e_j$. Its contraction is
\[
 \operatorname{ob}_{\kappa_B}(\mathcal O_\Delta)
 =\sum_{j,p}(B^{\mathsf t}-B)_{jp}\,
 d\bar x_p\otimes e_j,
\]
in the middle summand of \eqref{eq:diagonal-ext}. These constant harmonic representatives are independent. An infinitesimal gerbe class $\beta\in H^2(A_0,\mathcal O_{A_0})$ acts as $\beta|_\Delta\operatorname{id}$ in the first summand. It therefore cannot cancel a nonzero normal component.

For a rational norm-one slope, Hilbert~90 writes $\lambda=\bar c/c$ after clearing denominators. The $K$-endomorphism $(x,y)\mapsto(cx,\bar c y)$ takes the diagonal isogenously to that graph and extends over the unitary family. Pullback to the corresponding finite \'etale cover reduces the obstruction to the calculation above. Translations and tensoring by degree-zero line bundles preserve it.

The diagonal detects the Weil plane: a generator of one complex Weil line is
\[
 dx_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_4
 \wedge d\bar y_1\wedge\cdots\wedge d\bar y_4,
\]
whose restriction to $\Delta$ is a nonzero volume form. Its tangent and normal bundles are trivial, so Grothendieck--Riemann--Roch gives $\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal O_\Delta)=[\Delta]$, with no other components. On the five paired $K$-weights in degree eight, $S$ has eigenvalues
\[
 5^j\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\Q}(a^{8-2j}),\quad0\le j\le4,
 \qquad (-1054,-1170,-350,750,1250).
\]
The first is the Weil weight. Hence $P(S)$ kills the other weights and acts there by
$P(-1054)=-339429556224$.

Every isogeny pullback of $\mathcal O_\Delta$ is the structure sheaf of a reduced disjoint union of torsion translates of scalar norm-one graphs. Expand $v=\sum_Zm_Z[\mathcal O_Z]$, combining identical supports, and define
\[
 E_v=\bigoplus_{m_Z>0}\mathcal O_Z^{\oplus m_Z}
 \ \oplus\!
 \bigoplus_{m_Z<0}\mathcal O_Z[1]^{\oplus(-m_Z)}.
\]
This proves the Chern-character assertion. For distinct integral supports of the same dimension, $\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal O_Z,\mathcal O_{Z'})=0$: a nonzero image inside the torsion-free rank-one sheaf $\mathcal O_{Z'}$ would have generic support $Z'$, forcing $Z'=Z$.
