Fixed lattices and sufficiently fine level realize the two rational
representations as abelian schemes over a common finite cover of the
Mumford curve; the compact central Hodge data are fixed throughout.

\subsection{An algebraic projector onto the rank-sixteen part}

Put $H=H^1(B,\mathbf Q)$.  For one embedding $\sigma:K\to\mathbf C$,
write
\[
 H_\sigma=M_1\oplus M_2\oplus M_3,\qquad \dim M_i=2.
\]
The three Hodge-circle weight lists are $(1,-1),(1,1),(-1,-1)$.
The multidegree $(1,1,1)$ part of $\Lambda_K^3H$, together with its
conjugate, is
\begin{equation}
 P_{\mathbf C}=(M_1\otimes M_2\otimes M_3)
       \oplus(\overline M_1\otimes\overline M_2\otimes\overline M_3).
 \label{eq:mumford-rank16}
\end{equation}
It is Galois stable.  Its Hodge types are $(2,1)$ and $(1,2)$, each
with dimension eight.  The connected central torus acts trivially on
it: the Galois conjugates of the central cocharacter $(0,1,-1)$ span
the trace-zero plane, whereas the tensor in each summand has the
product character.  The derived representation is $(2,2,2)$.
The split corestriction gives its rational eight-dimensional form
$V$, with endomorphism field $\mathbf Q$.  The Hodge circle just
computed therefore identifies $P(1)$ with two copies of $V$.
These are statements at a Hodge-generic point of the common family;
they do not use the enlarged endomorphism algebra of a CM fibre.

We now give a projector without assuming that a general Hodge
projector is algebraic.  Algebraic K\"unneth projectors on an abelian
variety are polynomials in a multiplication map.  On $H^3(B)$ first use
\[
 e_K=\frac{1+[\omega]^*+[\omega^2]^*}{3}.
\]
The eigenvalue of $[\omega]^*$ on a wedge with $r$ factors from
$H_\sigma$ is $\omega^{2r-3}$.  Consequently $e_K$ selects exactly
$\Lambda^3H_\sigma\oplus\Lambda^3H_{\bar\sigma}$.

Choose a primitive element $a\in F$ with conjugates $a_1,a_2,a_3$.
Let $D_a$ apply $a$ in one of the three exterior factors and the
identity in the others, summed over the three choices.  It is an
algebraic cohomological operator, since
\begin{equation}
 D_a=\frac{[a+2]^*-2[a+1]^*+[a]^*}{2}-3\operatorname{id}
       \quad\hbox{on }H^3(B).
 \label{eq:mumford-derivation}
\end{equation}
Indeed, the second finite difference of $\Lambda^3(a+t)$ is
$6\operatorname{id}+2D_a$.  A rational endomorphism is interpreted
using a rational graph correspondence, or after replacing the lattice
by an isogenous one on which it is integral.

On \eqref{eq:mumford-rank16} the eigenvalue is
$c=\operatorname{Tr}_{F/\mathbf Q}(a)$.
All the other multidegrees are $(2,1,0)$, with eigenvalues
$2a_i+a_j$, $i\ne j$.  Set
\[
 Q_a(T)=\prod_{i\ne j}(T-2a_i-a_j)\in\mathbf Q[T].
\]
Then
\[
 Q_a(c)=-\prod_{i<j}(a_i-a_j)^2\ne0,
 \qquad e_P=e_K\frac{Q_a(D_a)}{Q_a(c)}
\]
is the required algebraic projector.  Repeated unwanted eigenvalues
cause no problem.  This also constructs the projector relatively,
using fixed endomorphisms and rational coefficients.

\subsection{A divisor support gives the weight-one correspondence}

We explain precisely the support assertion used in
\eqref{eq:mumford-coniveau-input}.  The coniveau-one part of $H^3(B)$
is the sum of Gysin images
\begin{equation}
 H^1(\widetilde D,\mathbf Q)(-1)\longrightarrow H^3(B,\mathbf Q)
 \label{eq:mumford-divisor-gysin}
\end{equation}
from resolutions of divisors.  Equivalently it is the image of a
correspondence from the first cohomology of a smooth projective curve
\cite[Proposition~1.3, p.~9]{ACMV}.  For degree three this can also be seen
directly: remove the singular loci and intersections of the supporting
divisors.  They have codimension at least two and contribute no
cohomology with supports in degree three.  Purity for the remaining
smooth divisors gives their $H^1(-1)$.  Only its weight-one part can
map to the pure group $H^3(B)$, and this part comes from a smooth
projective resolution.  A finite sum suffices for any given class.

Apply $e_P$ to these maps.  Under
\eqref{eq:mumford-coniveau-input} at least one resulting map is
nonzero.  Polarizable rational Hodge structures are semisimple,
and $P(1)=V^{\oplus2}$ with $V$ simple.  Therefore the Albanese
variety of a product of the resolutions has an isogeny factor
isogenous to $A$.  Hodge morphisms between the first cohomology of
abelian varieties are induced by homomorphisms up to isogeny.
Composing such a map with \eqref{eq:mumford-divisor-gysin} and $e_P$
produces a nonzero algebraic correspondence
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma:V\longrightarrow P(1).
 \label{eq:mumford-coniveau-bridge}
\end{equation}

In the convention of a map from $A$ to $B$, this correspondence has
codimension five in $A\times B$: its degree shift is $3-1=2$.
Conversely, a codimension-five cycle on this ten-dimensional product
has dimension five.  Its image in the sixfold $B$ is contained in a
divisor, so its cohomological image is in $N^1H^3(B)$.
Degree-zero algebraic correspondences preserve coniveau, by moving
intersections properly and the support dimension inequality; thus
applying $e_P$ preserves this conclusion.

For comparison with a Hodge tensor, the relevant component of the
codimension-five cycle is in $H^7(A)\otimes H^3(B)$.
The inverse of $L_A^3:H^1(A)\to H^7(A)$ is algebraic.  In the notation
of Section~\ref{sec:mumford}, it is $\Lambda_A^3/36$, since
$\Lambda_A^3L_A^3=36$ on $H^1(A)$.
Applying it in the first factor gives a codimension-two cycle with
component in $H^1(A)\otimes H^3(B)$.  Multiplication by $\theta_A^3$
reverses this operation.  Neither description asserts that the bridge
is a divisor on $A\times B$.

\subsection{Divisor invariants transfer to every power}

Let $U$ be the rational algebraic group of $E$-linear automorphisms of
$H^1(B)$ preserving its alternating polarization.  It contains the
Hodge circle of $B$, and
\[
 U_{\mathbf C}\simeq\mathrm{GL}_2^3,
 \qquad H_{\mathbf C}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^3(M_i\oplus M_i^\vee).
\]
The indicated dualities include the polarization Tate twist.
The invariants of $U$ in the cohomology of powers of $B$ are generated
by divisors.  Indeed the first fundamental theorem for the general
linear group expresses every invariant tensor as a linear combination
of contractions between $M_i$ and $M_i^\vee$.  These contractions are
the polarization pairings with the $E$-action inserted.  They are
divisor classes on $B^2$; permutations, exterior products and diagonal
pullbacks give their images in all exterior powers.  The rational
descent and the divisor formulation are
\cite[Proposition~3.6(c) and Remark~3.7, pp.~17--18]{MilneLefschetz}.
This assertion concerns the larger group $U$.  It does not say that
all Hodge classes on $B$ are generated by divisors.

The space $P$ is primitive in $H^3(B)$: the lowering operator pairs
opposite $K$ eigenspaces, whereas each summand in
\eqref{eq:mumford-rank16} uses just one eigenspace.
Consequently the polarization adjoint of $\Gamma$ is algebraic.
More explicitly, on the relevant cohomological degrees it is
\[
 \beta=(L_A^3)^{-1}\,\Gamma^t\,L_B^3 e_P:P(1)\longrightarrow V,
\]
up to the fixed sign in the polarization convention.  Include the
algebraic degree-one projector on $A$.  The resulting correspondence
lies in $\mathrm{CH}^5(B\times A)_{\mathbf Q}$.
It is nonzero, since it is the adjoint of a nonzero map between
polarized Hodge structures.

Put $P_+=M_1\otimes M_2\otimes M_3$ and
$P_-=M_1^\vee\otimes M_2^\vee\otimes M_3^\vee$ over $\mathbf C$.
For $G_{\mathbf C}=\mathrm{SL}_2^3$, both are irreducible of the
same type as $V_{\mathbf C}$.  Each restriction $\beta|_{P_\pm}$
is an isomorphism of $G_{\mathbf C}$-modules: it is either zero or
an isomorphism, and rationality interchanges the two restrictions.
They cannot both be zero.

For an integer $m\geq1$ consider
\[
 I_m=(P^{\otimes 2m})^U.
\]
After complexification its summand with $m$ factors $P_+$ and $m$
factors $P_-$ contains all the $G_{\mathbf C}$-invariants of that
balanced tensor product.  To check this, regroup its factors as
\[
 \bigotimes_{i=1}^3
       \bigl(M_i^{\otimes m}\otimes(M_i^\vee)^{\otimes m}\bigr).
\]
The scalar centre of each $\mathrm{GL}_2$ acts trivially here.
Thus its $\mathrm{GL}_2$-invariants and
$\mathrm{SL}_2$-invariants coincide.
The isomorphisms $\beta|_{P_\pm}$ now show that
\begin{equation}
 \beta^{\otimes2m}:I_m\longrightarrow(V^{\otimes2m})^G
       \quad\hbox{is surjective}.
 \label{eq:mumford-balanced-invariants}
\end{equation}
Surjectivity over $\mathbf C$ implies surjectivity over $\mathbf Q$.

Every element of $I_m$ is a divisor-product class of codimension $3m$
on $B^{2m}$.  Applying $\beta^{\boxtimes2m}$ produces a cycle of
codimension $m$ on $A^{2m}$: the correspondence has codimension $10m$
and the source has dimension $12m$.  This proves algebraicity of
every tensor in the target of \eqref{eq:mumford-balanced-invariants}.
Finally, $H^*(A^r)=\Lambda^*(V^{\oplus r})$.  Exterior multiplication
is equivariant, and invariants are exact for the reductive group $G$.
Every invariant in degree $2m$ is therefore a sum of images of
invariant $2m$-fold tensors.  Assigning each tensor factor to one of
the $r$ coordinates is the pullback along a map
$A^r\to A^{2m}$ whose coordinates repeat the corresponding
projections.  Such pullbacks preserve algebraicity.
This proves the rational Hodge conjecture on every $A^r$, and hence
Theorem~\ref{thm:mumford-coniveau}.

\subsection{Transporting the Lie action and its field weights}

We give a direct description in degree four of the exceptional classes
obtained from the same bridge.

Inside $\operatorname{End}_{\mathbf Q}(H)$ consider
\[
 \mathfrak g=\{X\in\operatorname{End}_E(H):
                    X^*=-X,\ \operatorname{Tr}_E X=0\},
\]
where $*$ is the polarization adjoint.  It is the rank-nine
representation $\operatorname{Res}_{F/\mathbf Q}\mathfrak{sl}_1(D)$.
Its projector is algebraic for an explicit reason.  The separability
idempotent of $E/\mathbf Q$, expressed in trace-dual bases, projects
to $\operatorname{End}_E(H)$.  The operation $(1-*)/2$ takes its
skew-adjoint part.  Subtracting the scalar skew-adjoint $E$ part by
the rational trace pairing takes the trace-zero part.
All $E$ actions are endomorphisms of $B$; the adjoint, pairings and
their inverses use the polarization and the algebraic Lefschetz
operators.  Thus these operations are algebraic on powers of $B$.
Multiplication by any $a\in F$ on $\mathfrak g$ is algebraic too.

The derivation action on $\Lambda^3H$, followed by $e_P$, gives an
algebraic map $\mathfrak g\to\operatorname{End}(P(1))$.
At the common Hodge-generic point, \eqref{eq:mumford-coniveau-bridge}
intertwines this action with the Mumford action.  Its polarization
adjoint is algebraic and
\[
 \Gamma^\dagger\Gamma=r\operatorname{id}_V,
 \qquad 0<r\in\mathbf Q,
\]
because $\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{HS}}(V)=\mathbf Q$.
Consequently
\[
 X\longmapsto r^{-1}\Gamma^\dagger\rho_P(X)\Gamma
\]
is the usual $\mathfrak g$ action on $V$, expressed by algebraic
correspondences.

Fix the trace form $B_{\mathfrak g}(X,Y)=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathbf Q}(XY)$
in the $E^2$ representation.  It is nondegenerate and has the same
rational index on the three complex $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ factors.
Its inverse tensor is algebraic, using the preceding projector and
the ambient trace coevaluation.  After fixing this inverse, apply
multiplication by a nonrational $a\in F$ in one factor and transport
both factors to $\operatorname{End}(V)$.  The resulting tensor is,
up to a common nonzero rational constant, the weighted Casimir
$\sum_i a_i C_i$.  The separate multiplication by $a$ is essential:
it is not a change of metric followed by an inverse that cancels it.

Regroup its four $V$ factors into two exterior pairs.  The calculation
\eqref{eq:mumford-casimir-eigen} gives the three eigenvalues
\[
 \frac14\operatorname{Tr}_{F/\mathbf Q}(a)-a_j.
\]
For clarity, the cross Casimir is $1/4$ on the symmetric square of
the two-dimensional standard representation and $-3/4$ on its
exterior square.  In the $j$th primitive summand of $\Lambda^2V$,
the exterior choice occurs at $j$ and the symmetric choices at the
other two indices.  This gives exactly the displayed expression.
Algebraic primitive projection and trace subtraction leave
\[
 -a+\frac13\operatorname{Tr}_{F/\mathbf Q}(a)\in F^0\setminus\{0\}.
\]
The cubic-field composition argument of
Theorem~\ref{thm:mumford-cubic} now gives the entire exceptional plane.
This describes the exceptional plane explicitly.

\subsection{What the algebraic Weil classes do give}

The ordinary Weil classes on $B$ are algebraic by the split-sixfold
results, with the input status in Section~\compref{sec:effective-sixfolds}.
They provide the following intermediate conclusion, which does not
settle the support condition above.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:weil-support-middle}
If a nonzero rational pure Weil class $w\in H^6(B,\mathbf Q)$ is
represented by a rational combination of integral threefolds $Y_i$,
then $P$ is an algebraic direct summand of
$\bigoplus_i H^3(\widetilde Y_i,\mathbf Q)$, where the tildes are
smooth projective resolutions.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $i:\widetilde Y_i\to B$, the cup action of its cycle class is
$i_*i^*:H^3(B)\to H^9(B)$.
The inverse of $L_B^3:H^3(B)\to H^9(B)$ is algebraic, by the
Lefschetz lowering operator and its finite primitive decomposition.
Consider on $P$ the operator
\[
 T=e_P(L_B^3)^{-1}(w\smile -)e_P.
\]
It factors algebraically through the displayed sum of middle
cohomology groups.  We show it is invertible.

Over $\mathbf C$ write $H=H_\sigma\oplus H_{\bar\sigma}$.
The polarization pairs these spaces perfectly and respects their
three $F$ blocks.  The two components of $w$ are nonzero volume
forms on these six-dimensional spaces: rationality and $w\ne0$
make both coefficients nonzero.  For
$u\in M_1\otimes M_2\otimes M_3\subset\Lambda^3H_\sigma$,
only the conjugate volume contributes to $w\smile u$.
The restriction of $L_B^3$ from the conjugate triple wedge to this
bidegree is an isomorphism, given by complementary exterior-power
pairing.  In multidegree $(1,1,1)$ it uses one Lefschetz pair from
each $F$ block.  Thus $T$ exchanges the two summands of
\eqref{eq:mumford-rank16} by invertible maps.  Here the two volume
forms refer to $K$ eigenspaces; their Hodge type is $(3,3)$.

The inverse of $T$ on $P$ is a polynomial in $T$ with rational
coefficients, by its characteristic polynomial and nonzero
determinant.  Composing this inverse with the factorization gives
an algebraic splitting, as asserted.
\end{proof}

The proposition reaches $H^3$ of threefolds.  A coniveau-one class
on one such $\widetilde Y_i$ whose image under
$e_P(L_B^3)^{-1}i_*$ is nonzero would suffice: its Gysin
pushforward raises coniveau by three, and the inverse $L_B^3$
lowers it by at most three, leaving the additional step of
coniveau one on $B$.  Ordinary algebraicity of the degree-six
Weil class does not prove this generalized Hodge assertion.

There is also a direct limitation on translations.  At the generic
point, every $H^1$ of a power of $B$ has derived constituents
$(2,1,1),(1,2,1),(1,1,2)$; a CM parameter variety adds only
derived-trivial constituents.  Hence
\[
 \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{HS}}
   (H^1(B^m\times C_{\mathrm{CM}}),P(1))=0.
\]
Any construction using these parameter first-cohomology groups,
addition, translations and Lefschetz correspondences therefore
has zero component in the required space.  This does not exclude
a singular divisor whose resolution has a new Albanese factor
isogenous to $A$; finding such a factor is the unresolved geometry.

Abdulali's type-IV theorem \cite[Theorem~13]{AbdulaliIV} requires
$|p_\alpha-q_\alpha|=1$ at every real place.  Here those differences
are $0,2,2$.  His other criterion requires every real simple factor
to be $\mathrm{SU}(p,1)$ with $p>1$
\cite[Theorems~6.5--6.6]{AbdulaliSurvey}; the compact factors here
do not satisfy it.  Appendix~B.5 of that survey adds the former
signature hypothesis to the earlier type-IV statements.
Its Section~8.2 records Schoen's generalized Hodge result for the
rank-two determinant structure of a signature-$(5,1)$ Eisenstein
sixfold.  That is a CM structure in degree six, not the rank-sixteen
part of degree three used here.  None of these cited statements
establishes \eqref{eq:mumford-coniveau-input}.

Finally, the family conclusion needs a family input.  A relative
nonzero correspondence \eqref{eq:mumford-coniveau-bridge} over a
nonempty open subset gives a uniform cycle bound after spreading
out, and proper bounded-cycle spaces extend it to the whole base.
The same follows if \eqref{eq:mumford-coniveau-input} holds at every
Hodge-generic point: countably many closed bounded-class loci cover
those points, so one dominates the complex curve.  Uniformly
bounded bridges on a dense set also suffice by first taking a
dominating Chow component and then arguing generically.
An isolated generic bridge is a pointwise input.  An isolated CM
bridge does not justify the generic endomorphism and Lie-action
identifications used in the theorem.
\section{Tate classes at an ordinary Mumford reduction}
\label{sec:mumford-ordinary}

This section counts the classes that a finite-field construction would have
to control.  The count uses the Frobenius torus, rather than the Hodge group.
The two invariant spaces have different dimensions.

Let $A$ be an abelian fourfold over a number field whose $\ell$-adic
representations are of Mumford's type: over an algebraic closure the derived
group is $\mathrm{SL}_2^3$ and its representation on $H^1$ is
$\mathbf2\otimes\mathbf2\otimes\mathbf2$.  This hypothesis holds for
the non-CM fibres over number fields of Mumford's families
\cite[\S1.2, p.~4]{NootMumfordGalois}.
Let $A_v$ be a good ordinary reduction, defined over $\mathbf F_q$.
For $r\geq0$, write
\[
 T^{2r}(A_v^2)=
 \bigcup_{m\geq1}
 H^{2r}_{\mathrm{\acute et}}
 ((A_v^2)_{\overline{\mathbf F}_q},\mathbf Q_\ell(r))^{\mathrm{Frob}_q^m=1}.
\]
This is the space of geometric Tate classes; its definition does not assert
that every such class is algebraic.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:mumford-ordinary-count}
For every such ordinary reduction,
\[
 \dim T^2(A_v^2)=16,\qquad \dim T^4(A_v^2)=132.
\]
The subspace of $T^4(A_v^2)$ generated by products of divisor classes
has dimension $100$.  Thus its quotient in $T^4(A_v^2)$ has dimension
$32$.  The five K\"unneth summands in degree four have Tate dimensions
\[
 8,\quad32,\quad52,\quad32,\quad8.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first identify the torus whose invariants are being counted.
Noot's Lemma~1.3 supplies a prime $\ell$ different from the residue
characteristic for which the derived $\ell$-adic group is
$\mathbf Q_\ell$-simple.  His proof of Proposition~4.1
\cite[pp.~12--14]{NootMumfordGalois} permits a finite residue-field
extension, a lift through the central isogeny, and removal of the scalar
$\sqrt q$.  The weights of a maximal derived torus are then the cube
\[
 \Omega=\{(\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2,\epsilon_3):\epsilon_i\in\{1,-1\}\}.
\]
In its rational character space, the kernel of evaluation at normalized
Frobenius is stable under a signed cyclic permutation of the three
coordinates.  One may take a power and change coordinate signs so that
this is the usual three-cycle.

Here is the ordinary specialization of that argument.  Write the
$p$-adic valuations of the normalized eigenvalues as
$\epsilon_1b_1+\epsilon_2b_2+\epsilon_3b_3$, with $v(q)=1$.
Ordinarity says that all eight numbers are $1/2$ or $-1/2$.
Their squares are constant on the cube.  Comparing the three
nonconstant Fourier coefficients of those squares gives
$b_1b_2=b_1b_3=b_2b_3=0$.  Their constant coefficient gives
$b_1^2+b_2^2+b_3^2=1/4$.  Hence exactly one $b_i$ is nonzero.
Every multiplicative relation has zero valuation.  Applying the
three-cycle to that relation makes each of its three coordinates
zero.  The evaluation kernel is therefore zero.  The connected
normalized Frobenius torus is the full three-dimensional torus.
Taking a finite residue-field extension does not change geometric
Tate classes.

We may now compute over $\overline{\mathbf Q}_\ell$.
Let $V$ have one basis vector of each weight in $\Omega$.
Then $H^1(A_v^2)$ is $V\oplus V$.  All degree-two invariants pair
opposite weights.  There are four opposite pairs, and each weight
has multiplicity two, so their dimension is $4\cdot2\cdot2=16$.

For the degree-four K\"unneth pieces, first observe that a four-element
subset of $\Omega$ has sum zero in precisely eight cases: the six
unions of two opposite pairs and the two tetrahedra consisting of the
vertices whose coordinate products are respectively $1$ and $-1$.
Thus $\dim(\bigwedge^4V)^T=8$.
For a fixed vertex $\epsilon$, there are four triples of distinct
vertices with sum $-\epsilon$: three consist of $-\epsilon$ and one
of the other opposite pairs, and the fourth completes the tetrahedron
containing $\epsilon$.  Consequently
$\dim(V\otimes\bigwedge^3V)^T=8\cdot4=32$.
The weights of $\bigwedge^2V$ are zero with multiplicity four,
six weights with one nonzero coordinate with multiplicity two each,
and twelve weights with two nonzero coordinates with multiplicity one
each.  Their nonzero coordinates are $2$ or $-2$.  Therefore
\[
 \dim(\bigwedge^2V\otimes\bigwedge^2V)^T
 =4^2+6\cdot2^2+12=52.
\]
The other two K\"unneth pieces are symmetric to the first two, giving
$8+32+52+32+8=132$.

The Tate theorem for divisors on abelian varieties over finite fields
\cite[Main Theorem, p.~134]{TateFiniteFields} identifies all sixteen degree-two
invariants with divisor classes, after a finite extension.
Products involving two different opposite pairs span
$\binom42\cdot4^2=96$ independent exterior basis monomials.
Products from one opposite pair span one monomial for each of the four
pairs, because both two-dimensional weight spaces must be exhausted.
These $100$ monomials are independent.  No other monomial is a product
of two weight-zero pairs.  The remaining monomials use the four
different weights of one of the two tetrahedra; each tetrahedron gives
$2^4=16$ choices of multiplicity labels.  This also directly gives
the complementary dimension $32$.
\end{proof}

These geometric Tate dimensions are independent of the auxiliary
prime $\ell$. Frobenius is semisimple on the cohomology of an
abelian variety over a finite field, and the relevant products of
its characteristic roots being roots of unity are independent of
$\ell$.

The calculation supplies no bound for the degrees of representatives of
the thirty-two remaining Tate directions.  The exceptional Hodge plane
before reduction is a different invariant space, cut out by the entire
Mumford group.  Its dimension two must not be substituted for the
finite-field quotient dimension thirty-two.

\subsection{What the Kuga--Satake theorem in finite characteristic supplies}

The available K3 theorem gives an exact, but more limited, bridge.
In odd characteristic, Madapusi Pera's Theorem~4.17(3)--(4)
\cite[pp.~22--23]{MadapusiPeraK3} constructs compatible primitive
cohomology embeddings and identifies primitive divisor classes with
special endomorphisms of the Kuga--Satake abelian variety.
It does not assert algebraicity of the full correspondence on
$S\times\operatorname{KS}(S)\times\operatorname{KS}(S)$.
Its proof lifts a special endomorphism and its corresponding divisor
one at a time.  Consequently it does not give a uniform degree bound
for correspondences inducing the complete Clifford map.
To obtain the required uniform exceptional detectors by this route,
one still needs algebraic correspondences inducing the required
quadratic block, together with a degree bound independent of $v$.

For the characteristic-zero order-three K3 family,
Bolognesi--Laterveer do state the full Kuga--Satake Hodge conjecture:
their Conjecture~4.2 specifies the Kuga--Satake embedding, their
Theorem~4.4 proves its algebraicity for the indicated complete
intersections, and Proposition~6.2 extends that statement to
$\mathcal M_{0,1}$ \cite[pp.~14--16]{clifford-BL}.
This published statement and the formal limitation of the half-twist
map in Proposition~\ref{clifford-prop:half-twist-obstruction} are
distinct assertions.  The latter is not a refutation of the former.

\section{Direct sums on abelian subvarieties}
\label{sec:subtorus-direct-sums}

We return to the Gaussian support construction of
Proposition~\ref{prop:graph-obstruction}. This section allows
arbitrary middle-dimensional abelian supports and arbitrary
intersections between them.  The conclusion concerns the strong
semiregularity condition of Theorem~\compref{thm:pure-weil-ext}.
The following section then treats extensions in the principal
Postnikov network.

We first record the elementary linear algebra that distinguishes a
rational Weil class from a sum of two rational decomposable classes.
The complex uniqueness statement is a special case of rank-two
Grassmannian identifiability; see \cite[Theorem~3.2 and Example~2.5]
{GalganoSecants}.  The following proof gives precisely the form needed
here.

\begin{lemma}[Complementary decomposable summands]
\label{lem:complementary-secants}
Let $V=U\oplus W$ be a complex vector space with
$\dim U=\dim W=m\geq3$.  Suppose
\[
 z=u+w,\qquad
 0\ne u\in\bigwedge^mU,\quad
 0\ne w\in\bigwedge^mW.
\]
Then the unordered pair of decomposable summand lines
$\{\mathbb C u,\mathbb C w\}$ is uniquely determined by $z$ among
expressions of $z$ as a sum of two nonzero decomposable $m$-vectors.

Consequently, if $V=V_{\mathbb Q}\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb C$ and
complex conjugation exchanges $U$ and $W$, a nonzero rational vector
of this form is not a sum of at most two rational decomposable
$m$-vectors.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Multiplication by $z$ from $V$ to $\bigwedge^{m+1}V$ is injective:
the contributions from $U$ and $W$ have respective bidegrees
$(1,m)$ and $(m,1)$ and each is injective.  If
$z=z_1+z_2$ is another decomposable expression, with supporting
$m$-planes $V_1,V_2$, every vector of $V_1\cap V_2$ annihilates
$z$ by multiplication.  Thus $V_1\cap V_2=0$, and these two planes
are complementary.

Consider the quadratic annihilator
\[
 K_z=\ker\left(\bigwedge^2 V\xrightarrow{\,\alpha\mapsto
                    \alpha\wedge z\,}\bigwedge^{m+2}V\right).
\]
With respect to $U\oplus W$ it is exactly $U\wedge W$.
Indeed the mixed terms annihilate both volume forms, whereas the
pure terms map injectively to bidegrees $(2,m)$ and $(m,2)$;
these bidegrees are distinct because $m\geq3$.

For $0\ne v\in V$ put
\[
 K_z(v)=\{a\in V:v\wedge a\in K_z\}.
\]
If $v\in U$ then $K_z(v)=\mathbb C v\oplus W$, and similarly
with $U,W$ exchanged.  If $v=v_U+v_W$ with both components
nonzero, the conditions on the pure exterior components give
\[
 K_z(v)=\mathbb C v_U\oplus\mathbb C v_W.
\]
It follows that
\[
 \{0\}\cup\{v\ne0:\dim K_z(v)>2\}=U\cup W.
\]
The two maximal linear subspaces of this locus recover the unordered
pair $\{U,W\}$.  Applying the same argument to $V_1\oplus V_2$
proves uniqueness.  The two summand lines then follow by taking
top exterior powers.

In the rational situation, each supporting plane of a nonzero
rational decomposable vector is defined over $\mathbb Q$, hence
is fixed by complex conjugation.  Uniqueness would make these planes
$U$ and $W$, which are distinct and exchanged by conjugation.
This is impossible.  A one-term expression is already excluded by
the injectivity of multiplication by $z$ on $V$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Self-Ext on an abelian support]
\label{lem:flat-subtorus-ext}
Let $A$ be a complex abelian variety of dimension $g$, let
$Z=a+B$ be a translate of a connected abelian subvariety of dimension
$d$, and let $i:Z\hookrightarrow A$.  For
$L\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(Z)$ set $S=i_*L$.  Then
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^*(S,S)\simeq
 \bigwedge^*\left(H^1(B,\mathcal O_B)\oplus
                  (T_0A/T_0B)\right)
\]
as graded algebras, with a choice of splitting.  In particular,
$\dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(S,S)=\binom g2$.

Every translation--tensor functor
$\Phi=t_x^*(-)\otimes P$, $P\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(A)$,
which carries $S$ to an isomorphic sheaf acts trivially on this
self-Ext algebra, after any isomorphism $\Phi(S)\simeq S$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a complementary abelian subvariety $C$ so that addition gives
a finite \emph{\'etale} isogeny
$f:B\times C\longrightarrow A$.  The inverse image of $Z$ is a
disjoint union of varieties $B\times\{c\}$, and each such component
maps isomorphically to $Z$ by a translation.  Hence $f^*S$ is the
direct sum of sheaves $L_c\boxtimes k(c)$ on these components, with
$L_c\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(B)$.  Self-Ext between different
components is zero.  On each component the K\"unneth formula and
the Koszul resolution of $k(c)$ give
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^*(L_c\boxtimes k(c),L_c\boxtimes k(c))
 =H^*(B,\mathcal O_B)\otimes\bigwedge^*T_cC.
\]
The first factor is the exterior algebra on $H^1(B,\mathcal O_B)$.

The kernel of $f$ acts simply transitively on the components and
acts on their self-Ext algebras by the canonical translation
identifications.  Finite \emph{\'etale} descent in characteristic
zero identifies $\operatorname{Ext}^*(S,S)$ with the invariants of
this direct sum.  This proves the asserted exterior algebra;
$T_0C$ identifies with $T_0A/T_0B$.

Suppose now that $\Phi(S)\simeq S$.  Equality of supports forces
$x\in B$.  Since $L$ is algebraically trivial,
$t_x^*L\simeq L$; hence the restriction of $P$ to $B$ is trivial.
On $B\times C$ the functor consequently has the form
\[
 t_{(x,0)}^*(-)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_C^*P_C,
 \qquad P_C\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(C).
\]
Translation acts trivially on $H^*(B,\mathcal O_B)$ and on the
normal tangent space.  Tensoring $k(c)$ with $P_C$ acts trivially
on its self-Ext algebra: a local trivialization of $P_C$ identifies
the functor with the identity near $c$, and the scalar ambiguity
of the isomorphism on $k(c)$ disappears under conjugation.
The same is true of the isomorphism on $L_c$, since its endomorphisms
are scalars.  The action is therefore trivial on each component
algebra, and hence on the descended algebra.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[An invariant contribution from each direction]
\label{prop:subtorus-orbit-ext}
Let $A$ have dimension $g$ and let
\[
 E=\bigoplus_{i=1}^N (i_i)_*L_i[k_i],
 \qquad Z_i=a_i+B_i,\quad L_i\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(Z_i),
\]
where all the connected abelian subvarieties $B_i$ have the same
dimension.  Let a finite group $G$ act coherently by translations
and tensor products with algebraically trivial line bundles, and
suppose that $E$ has a $G$-equivariant structure.
If $r$ is the number of distinct connected subvarieties among the
$B_i$, then
\[
 \dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)^G\geq r\binom g2.
\]
More precisely, one obtains a contribution $\binom g2$ from each
$G$-orbit of isomorphism classes of the shifted summands, after
combining repeated copies of an isomorphic summand.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $S_i=(i_i)_*L_i$.  Two nonisomorphic sheaves of the indicated
form have no degree-zero morphisms between them.  If the supports
differ, a morphism has image supported on their proper intersection,
which is impossible in a line bundle on an integral support.  If
the supports agree, the assertion follows from the absence of
sections of a nontrivial algebraically trivial line bundle.
Also $\operatorname{End}(S_i)=\mathbb C$.

One must allow the equivariant isomorphisms to have off-diagonal
terms between unequal shifts.  On
\[
 M=\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)
   =\bigoplus_{i,j}\operatorname{Ext}^{2+k_j-k_i}(S_i,S_j)
\]
use the finite descending filtration
\[
 F^aM=\bigoplus_{k_j-k_i\geq a}
             \operatorname{Ext}^{2+k_j-k_i}(S_i,S_j).
\]
Every degree-zero morphism between sums of shifted sheaves has
entries only when the target shift is at least the source shift,
because negative Ext between sheaves vanishes.  This applies both
to each equivariant isomorphism and to its inverse.  Conjugation
therefore preserves this filtration.  The translation--tensor
functors are exact on coherent sheaves, so they preserve it as
well.

On $\operatorname{gr}^0_F M$ only the equal-shift parts of the
equivariant isomorphisms act.  The degree-zero vanishing just proved
makes these parts block diagonal, up to permutation of isomorphic
types.  Accordingly, the direct sum of the diagonal isotypical
blocks
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^2(S,S)\otimes\operatorname{End}(V_S)
\]
is a $G$-stable subspace of $\operatorname{gr}^0_F M$; here $V_S$
is the multiplicity space at the specified shift.
For the stabilizer of a type, Lemma~\ref{lem:flat-subtorus-ext}
makes the action on $\operatorname{Ext}^2(S,S)$ trivial.  The
action on $\operatorname{End}(V_S)$ is by conjugation and fixes
its identity, even when the chosen action on $V_S$ is projective.
Thus each orbit of types contributes at least $\binom g2$
invariants to this diagonal subspace: transport an arbitrary
self-Ext class tensored with the identity around the orbit.

Taking invariants of finite-dimensional complex representations
of a finite group is exact.  Hence the invariant dimension of
$M$ is the sum of the invariant dimensions of its associated
graded pieces, and is at least the contribution just found in
degree zero.  Finally, translations preserve the direction $B_i$
and tensor products do not change support, so different directions
cannot belong to the same orbit.  This proves the inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The obstruction for direct sums on middle-dimensional
abelian supports]\label{thm:subtorus-direct-sums}
Let $A$ be a balanced Weil abelian variety of dimension $g=2n\geq4$,
and let $W_K\subset H^{2n}(A,\mathbb Q)$ be its Weil plane.
Suppose
\[
 E=\bigoplus_{i=1}^N(i_i)_*L_i[k_i],
 \qquad Z_i=a_i+B_i,\quad \dim B_i=n,
 \quad L_i\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(Z_i),
\]
has $\operatorname{ch}(E)\in W_K\setminus\{0\}$.
For every coherent finite translation--tensor action and every
equivariant structure on $E$,
\[
 \dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)^G
       \geq3\binom{2n}{2}>2\binom{2n}{2}.
\]
Consequently $E$ is not strongly $G$-semiregular.
For a Weil eightfold the lower bound is $84$, whereas strong
semiregularity requires $56$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The tangent bundles of $A$ and of each $Z_i$ are trivial, and
$c_1(L_i)=0$.  Grothendieck--Riemann--Roch therefore gives the
\emph{entire} Chern character
\[
 \operatorname{ch}((i_i)_*L_i)=[B_i]\in H^{2n}(A,\mathbb Q).
\]
For $q_i:A\longrightarrow A/B_i$ one has
$[B_i]=q_i^*[\mathrm{pt}]$.  This is a rational decomposable
$2n$-vector in $\bigwedge^{2n}H^1(A,\mathbb Q)$.
Combining equal directions gives
\[
 \operatorname{ch}(E)=\sum_B c_B[B],\qquad
 c_B=\sum_{i:B_i=B}(-1)^{k_i}\in\mathbb Z.
\]

Over $\mathbb C$, a nonzero rational Weil class is a sum of
nonzero volume forms on the complementary $2n$-dimensional
$K$-eigenspaces $H^1_\sigma$ and $H^1_{\bar\sigma}$.  These
eigenspaces are exchanged by complex conjugation.
Lemma~\ref{lem:complementary-secants} shows that at least three
coefficients $c_B$ must be nonzero.  In particular $E$ has at
least three support directions.  The preceding proposition gives
the lower bound, and Theorem~\compref{thm:pure-weil-ext} excludes
strong $G$-semiregularity.
\end{proof}

The same conclusion holds if a rational algebraic $B$-field
normalizes the character to a nonzero pure Weil class: the
character of the displayed direct sum is already concentrated in
degree $2n$, so its degree-$2n$ component is unchanged by the
normalization.  The assumption $L_i\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(Z_i)$
means algebraic triviality, not just zero degree for one chosen
polarization.

This obstruction requires a direct-sum presentation.  It does not
settle arbitrary extensions, cones, thickenings, or perfect
complexes.  In particular it is complementary to
Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-support}, which permits such operations
on its specified supports but requires a normal obstruction and
an intersection hypothesis.  Nor does failure of the strong
condition alone establish failure of the weak equivariant
semiregularity condition.
\subsection{Arbitrary line bundles and cancellation of Chern terms}
\label{subsec:subtorus-generalization}

The direct-sum obstruction persists for arbitrary line bundles on
translated abelian subvarieties of arbitrary dimensions.  In this
generality the relevant count is the number of orbits of sheaf
types, rather than the number of support directions.

First, Lemma~\ref{lem:flat-subtorus-ext} holds for every line bundle
$L$ on $Z$, with no algebraic-triviality assumption.  The proof of
the self-Ext computation is unchanged.  For a stabilizing functor,
one now has
\[
 x\in B,\qquad t_x^*L\otimes P|_Z\simeq L.
\]
On the product isogeny the functor is
$t_{(x,0)}^*(-)\otimes(P_B\boxtimes P_C)$.  The first factor acts
trivially on
$\operatorname{Ext}^*_B(L_c,L_c)=H^*(B,\mathcal O_B)$:
tensoring cancels in the endomorphism sheaf, conjugation by the
line-bundle isomorphism is the identity on $\mathcal O_B$, and
translation acts trivially on its cohomology.  The skyscraper
factor is treated as before.  This proves triviality on the full
self-Ext algebra for arbitrary $L$.

\begin{proposition}[Arbitrary dimensions and line bundles]
\label{prop:arbitrary-subtorus-orbit-ext}
Let $A$ be a complex abelian variety of dimension $g$ and let
\[
 E=\bigoplus_{i=1}^N (i_i)_*L_i[k_i],
 \qquad i_i:Z_i=a_i+B_i\hookrightarrow A,
\]
where the $B_i$ are connected abelian subvarieties of any dimensions
and the $L_i$ are arbitrary line bundles.  For a coherent finite
translation--tensor action and an equivariant structure on $E$,
let $q$ be the number of $G$-orbits of shifted sheaf isomorphism
types among its summands.  Then
\[
 \dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)^G\geq q\binom g2.
\]
Repeated copies of one shifted sheaf are counted as one type.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $S_i=(i_i)_*L_i$ and $d_i=\dim Z_i$.  Fix an ample integral
class $h$ on $A$ and set
\[
 s_i=\int_{Z_i} c_1(L_i)\,(h|_{Z_i})^{d_i-1}
 \quad(d_i>0),\qquad s_i=0\quad(d_i=0).
\]
Both $d_i$ and $s_i$ are preserved by translations and tensor
products with algebraically trivial line bundles.
Each shifted sheaf has scalar endomorphisms and is indecomposable.
Uniqueness of finite decompositions in the Hom-finite derived
category therefore makes $G$ permute these isomorphism types;
exactness on coherent sheaves preserves their shifts.

A nonzero degree-zero morphism $S_i\to S_j$ requires
$d_i\geq d_j$, since $S_j$ is pure of dimension $d_j$.  If the
dimensions are equal, the supports must agree.  On this common
support a nonzero morphism of the two line bundles is a section
of $L_j\otimes L_i^{-1}$.  Its zero divisor is either empty,
in which case the line bundles are isomorphic, or is an effective
nonzero divisor, in which case ampleness gives $s_j>s_i$.

Let $R=\max_i s_i-\min_i s_i$.  Choose positive integers
$M_2>R$ and $M_1>gM_2+R$, and put
\[
 \lambda_i=M_1k_i-M_2d_i+s_i.
\]
Every nonzero degree-zero morphism between summands has
$\lambda_j\geq\lambda_i$.  Indeed a strictly larger target
shift dominates all dimension and degree terms; at equal shift
a smaller target dimension dominates the degree term; at equal
shift and dimension the preceding paragraph applies.  Equality
can occur only between isomorphic sheaves at the same shift.

Filter $\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)$ by the differences
$\lambda_j-\lambda_i$, exactly as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:subtorus-orbit-ext}.  Equivariant
isomorphisms and their inverses preserve this filtration.
The zero associated-graded part has a stable sum of diagonal
isotypical blocks.  The strengthened version of
Lemma~\ref{lem:flat-subtorus-ext} gives a trivial
$\binom g2$-dimensional self-Ext factor for each stabilizer,
while the identity in each multiplicity endomorphism space is
fixed.  Each orbit therefore supplies $\binom g2$ invariant
classes.  Exactness of finite-group invariants gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two characters cannot produce the Weil leading term]
\label{lem:two-subtorus-characters}
Let $A$ be balanced of Weil type, of dimension $2n\geq4$.
A rational linear combination of at most two characters
$\operatorname{ch}(i_*L)$ of the preceding form cannot have all
components below degree $2n$ zero and its degree-$2n$ component
in $W_K\setminus\{0\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For a support $Z=a+B$ of codimension $c$, the character has leading
term $[B]$ in degree $2c$.  More precisely, choose a rational
cohomology class $\alpha\in H^2(A,\mathbb Q)$ restricting to
$c_1(L)$ on $Z$.  Such a lift exists because the restriction on
$H^1$ is surjective for an abelian subvariety, hence also on its
second exterior power.  Grothendieck--Riemann--Roch gives
\[
 \operatorname{ch}(i_*L)=e^\alpha[B].
\]

Suppose two nonzero terms have the asserted sum.  If their minimum
support codimension exceeds $n$, there is no degree-$2n$ term.
If it equals $n$, that term is a sum of at most two rational
decomposable $2n$-vectors, contrary to
Lemma~\ref{lem:complementary-secants}.

If the minimum codimension $c$ is smaller than $n$, cancellation
of the degree-$2c$ term requires that both codimensions equal $c$
and that their fundamental classes be proportional.  A nonzero
decomposable fundamental class determines the kernel of
$H^1(A,\mathbb Q)\to H^1(B,\mathbb Q)$, and hence the connected
abelian subvariety $B$.  The two supports thus have the same
direction $B$, and their fundamental classes agree.

If $B\ne A$, choose a nonzero vector
$v\in\ker(H^1(A,\mathbb Q)\to H^1(B,\mathbb Q))$.
Since $v\wedge[B]=0$, it annihilates both entire characters and
therefore their degree-$2n$ sum.  This is impossible for a nonzero
Weil class: multiplication by such a class is injective on
$H^1(A,\mathbb C)$, by the first paragraph of the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:complementary-secants}.

Finally, if $B=A$, the two characters are exponentials of first
Chern classes.  Their rank terms must cancel.  Their degree-two
terms must also cancel because $n\geq2$, forcing the two first
Chern classes to be equal.  The entire character then cancels,
again a contradiction.  The one-term case is included, or follows
directly by inspecting its leading term.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The direct-sum obstruction with arbitrary line bundles]
\label{thm:all-line-subtorus-sums}
Let $A$ be a balanced Weil abelian variety of dimension $g=2n\geq4$,
and let
\[
 E=\bigoplus_i(i_i)_*L_i[k_i]
\]
with arbitrary line bundles on translated connected abelian
subvarieties of arbitrary dimensions.  Suppose the components
of $\operatorname{ch}(E)$ below degree $2n$ vanish and
$\operatorname{ch}_n(E)\in W_K\setminus\{0\}$.
Then every coherent finite translation--tensor action and
equivariant structure satisfy
\[
 \dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{Ext}^2(E,E)^G
       \geq3\binom{2n}{2}.
\]
In particular, if a rational algebraic $B$-field satisfies
$e^B\operatorname{ch}(E)\in W_K\setminus\{0\}$, the object
$E$ is not strongly $G$-semiregular.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation and algebraically trivial tensor products preserve
the entire rational Chern character.  Each orbit of shifted
sheaf types consequently contributes an integer multiple of one
of the characters appearing in Lemma~\ref{lem:two-subtorus-characters}.
If there were at most two orbits, that lemma would contradict the
hypothesis on the leading term.  Hence there are at least three
orbits, and Proposition~\ref{prop:arbitrary-subtorus-orbit-ext}
proves the bound.

Multiplication by $e^B$ preserves the lowest nonzero cohomological
degree and its component.  The normalized-pure hypothesis thus
implies the hypothesis on the leading term.  The exact criterion
of Theorem~\compref{thm:pure-weil-ext} then excludes strong
semiregularity.
\end{proof}

The argument allows arbitrary cancellation of higher Chern terms;
it does not assume that the individual characters are pure or
that their supports have middle dimension.  It still uses the
direct-sum presentation in the invariant self-Ext argument.

\subsection{A three-component extension that reduces second self-Ext}
\label{subsec:three-support-extension}

The direct-sum hypothesis cannot be removed merely by counting
support directions.  The following calculation exhibits the
change caused by actual gluing, while also showing why this
particular construction does not produce a Weil class.

Let $T$ be an elliptic curve, choose $0\ne p\in T$, put
$S=T\times T$, and take
\[
 C_1=T\times\{0\},\qquad
 C_2=\{0\}\times T,\qquad
 C_3=\Delta_T+(p,0),
 \qquad D=C_1+C_2+C_3.
\]
The three intersection points are $(0,0)$, $(p,0)$ and $(0,-p)$,
which are distinct.  Each $C_i^2=0$ and each pair has intersection number one, so
$D^2=6$.  The reduced curve $D$ is connected and has arithmetic
genus $1+D^2/2=4$.

Choose a line bundle $\mathcal L$ on $D$ with restrictions
\[
 \mathcal L|_{C_1}\simeq\mathcal O_{C_1}(C_2+C_3),\quad
 \mathcal L|_{C_2}\simeq\mathcal O_{C_2}(C_3),\quad
 \mathcal L|_{C_3}\simeq\mathcal O_{C_3}.
\]
Such a line bundle exists by choosing an identification between
the two one-dimensional fibres over each of the three nodes.
Locally a node has ring
$\mathbb C[[x]]\times_{\mathbb C}\mathbb C[[y]]$;
identifying the fibres of two free rank-one modules gives a free
rank-one module over this ring.  These local gluings and the
given line bundles away from the nodes construct $\mathcal L$.

Let $j:D\hookrightarrow S$ and $F=j_*\mathcal L$.
The standard sequences for the union of Cartier divisors give
\[
 0\longrightarrow\mathcal O_{C_1}\longrightarrow F
 \longrightarrow j_{23*}(\mathcal L|_{C_2+C_3})
 \longrightarrow0,
\]
\[
 0\longrightarrow\mathcal O_{C_2}
 \longrightarrow j_{23*}(\mathcal L|_{C_2+C_3})
 \longrightarrow\mathcal O_{C_3}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Thus $F$ is an actual iterated extension of the three structure
sheaves.  Both extensions are nonsplit: a line bundle on either
connected reduced union has only scalar endomorphisms, whereas
the indicated direct sum would have nontrivial projection
endomorphisms.  Since the $C_i$ are elliptic curves on an abelian
surface, their entire characters are $[C_i]$, and hence
\[
 \operatorname{ch}(F)=[D].
\]
In particular there is no degree-four term.

The sheaf $F$ is simple because
$\operatorname{End}(F)=H^0(D,\mathcal O_D)=\mathbb C$.
Serre duality on $S$ gives $\dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(F,F)=1$,
while Riemann--Roch gives
\[
 \chi(F,F)=-D^2=-6.
\]
It follows that
\[
 \bigl(\dim\operatorname{Ext}^0(F,F),
        \dim\operatorname{Ext}^1(F,F),
        \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(F,F)\bigr)=(1,8,1).
\]

Now let $B$ and $C$ be abelian threefolds and set
\[
 A=S\times B\times C,
 \qquad\mathcal E=F\boxtimes\mathcal O_B\boxtimes k(0)_C.
\]
This is a simple coherent sheaf on an abelian eightfold, hence is
gluable, supported on three abelian-fourfold components
$C_i\times B\times\{0\}$ that meet in divisors.
Its entire character is the pure degree-eight class
\[
 \operatorname{ch}(\mathcal E)=[D]\boxtimes1\boxtimes[0]_C.
\]
K\"unneth identifies its graded self-Ext with
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^*_S(F,F)\otimes
 \bigwedge^*\bigl(H^1(B,\mathcal O_B)\oplus T_0C\bigr).
\]
The exterior factor has six generators.  Therefore
\[
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E,\mathcal E)
       =1+8\cdot6+\binom62=64.
\]

For comparison, replace $F$ by
$\mathcal O_{C_1}\oplus\mathcal O_{C_2}\oplus\mathcal O_{C_3}$.
On $S$ each diagonal self-Ext algebra has two exterior generators,
and for $i\ne j$ the only nonzero cross-Ext is a one-dimensional
$\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal O_{C_i},\mathcal O_{C_j})$,
because the two curves meet transversely in one point.
After taking the same product with $B\times C$, the diagonal
contribution to second self-Ext is $3\binom82=84$, and the six
ordered cross terms each contribute six.  The direct sum thus
has second self-Ext dimension $120$.

The decrease from $120$ to $64$ is a real effect of the extension,
not a choice of presentation of the direct sum.  It does not
reach a Weil detector: every nonzero vector from
$H^1(C,\mathbb Q)$ annihilates the displayed character by wedge
multiplication, whereas a nonzero pure Weil class on an eightfold
has no such vector.  This also explains why the example creates
no conflict with the pure-Weil dimension criterion.  An extension
construction for the main problem must remove this common
annihilator as well as control its invariant self-Ext.
\section{A principal Gaussian family of Postnikov extensions}
\label{sec:principal-graph-postnikov}

The direct-sum obstruction does not control arbitrary extension
classes.  We now construct an explicit family of gluable perfect
complexes with a nonzero pure Weil character on the principal
Gaussian hyperbolic eightfold.  Their obstruction spaces are too
large: every member fails the ordinary dimension test.  We first
prove a stronger numerical bound for indecomposable members with
finite equivariance, and then exclude the required invariant
dimension for every member, including decomposable ones.

\begin{lemma}[An exact-triangle obstruction bound]
\label{lem:postnikov-ext-bound}
Let $P,N$ be coherent sheaves on a smooth projective variety, with
$\operatorname{Hom}(P,N)=\operatorname{Hom}(N,P)=0$.
For $e\in\operatorname{Ext}^2(P,N)$, let $\mathcal E_e$ be defined by
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:postnikov-triangle}
 N[1]\xrightarrow{b}\mathcal E_e\xrightarrow{a}P
                  \xrightarrow{e}N[2].
\end{equation}
Then $\mathcal E_e$ is gluable, with cohomology $N$ in degree $-1$
and $P$ in degree zero, and
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:postnikov-ext-lower}
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_e,\mathcal E_e)
 \geq\dim\operatorname{Ext}^3(P,N)
      -\dim\operatorname{Ext}^1(P,P)
      -\dim\operatorname{Ext}^1(N,N).
\end{equation}
If a finite group acts coherently by exact functors preserving the
triangle, the same inequality holds with invariants on all four
Ext spaces.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The cohomology assertion follows from the triangle.  The standard
$t$-structure gives
$\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E_e,\mathcal E_e[k])=0$ for $k\leq-2$;
a map in degree $-1$ factors through a map $P\to N$, so it also
vanishes.  Thus the object is gluable.

Consider the map
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^3(P,N)\longrightarrow
 \operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_e,\mathcal E_e),
 \qquad f\longmapsto b[2]fa.
\]
Factor it through $\operatorname{Hom}(P,\mathcal E_e[2])$.
The exact sequence obtained by applying $\operatorname{Hom}(P,-)$
to the shifted triangle shows that the first kernel is the image
of $\operatorname{Ext}^1(P,P)$.  Applying
$\operatorname{Hom}(-,\mathcal E_e[2])$ shows that the second kernel
is the image of
$\operatorname{Hom}(N[2],\mathcal E_e[2])
=\operatorname{Hom}(N,\mathcal E_e)$.
The original triangle and $\operatorname{Hom}(N,P)=0$ identify
this last space with $\operatorname{Ext}^1(N,N)$.
Bounding the two kernel dimensions proves
\eqref{eq:postnikov-ext-lower}.  All maps are equivariant when the
triangle is, and invariants of finite groups are exact in
characteristic zero.  This proves the final assertion.
No formality or spectral-sequence degeneration is being assumed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The principal hyperbolic model and its character}

Let $T=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+i\mathbb Z)$ and $S=T_x\times T_y$,
with its product principal polarization.  Give $S$ the Gaussian
action for which
\[
 I(x,y)=(-y,x)
\]
is multiplication by the abstract Gaussian integer $i$.
This action has signature $(1,1)$.
It is important here to use $I$, rather than the diagonal action
$(x,y)\mapsto(ix,-iy)$.

Here is the integral lattice check.  On
$\Lambda=\mathbb Z[i]^2$ the product alternating form is
\[
 E_0(v,w)=\operatorname{Im}(\bar v_1w_1+\bar v_2w_2).
\]
The associated Hermitian form, with $i$ now denoting the Gaussian
action, is
\[
 h(v,w)=E_0(Iv,w)+iE_0(v,w).
\]
The vectors $e=(1,0)$ and $f=(0,i)$ form a basis over
$\mathbb Z[I]$, and their Gram matrix is
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
Thus the Hermitian lattice is $U$.  Taking four copies gives
\[
 A=S^4,
 \qquad (\Lambda_A,h)=U^4,
\]
with the product principal polarization and balanced signature
$(4,4)$.

Index four elliptic curves by $\alpha\in\mu_4$ as follows:
\[
 C_1=\{x=0\},\qquad C_{-1}=\{y=0\},\qquad
 C_i=\{y=x\},\qquad C_{-i}=\{y=-x\}.
\]
Assign multiplicities
\[
 s_1=s_{-1}=2,\qquad s_i=s_{-i}=1.
\]
Put $\theta_x=(i/2)dx\wedge d\bar x$,
$\theta_y=(i/2)dy\wedge d\bar y$, and
\[
 H=\theta_x+\theta_y,\qquad
 b=\frac i2(dx\wedge d\bar y+dy\wedge d\bar x),\qquad
 a=\theta_x-\theta_y+ib.
\]
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-gaussian-a}
 a=\frac i2(dx+i\,dy)\wedge(d\bar x+i\,d\bar y).
\end{equation}
Both factors in \eqref{eq:principal-gaussian-a} belong to the
$i$-eigenspace of $I^*$; the conjugate expression belongs to the
$-i$-eigenspace.  Directly from the four divisor equations,
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-four-divisors}
 s_\alpha[C_\alpha]
   =H+\tfrac12(\alpha a+\bar\alpha\bar a),
 \quad
 \sum_{\alpha\in\mu_4}\bar\alpha s_\alpha[C_\alpha]=2a,
 \quad
 \sum_{\alpha\in\mu_4}\alpha s_\alpha[C_\alpha]=2\bar a.
\end{equation}
The latter identities also define $a$ over $\mathbb Q(i)$ from
rational divisor classes, independently of the differential
normalization.

For $\boldsymbol\alpha=(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_4)$, set
\[
 B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=\prod_{j=1}^4C_{\alpha_j},\qquad
 \mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=\prod_{j=1}^4s_{\alpha_j},\qquad
 c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=\operatorname{Re}\prod_{j=1}^4\alpha_j.
\]
There are $64$ indices with $c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=1$ and $64$
with $c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=-1$; all other coefficients are zero.
Define
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-PN}
 P=\bigoplus_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=1}
       \mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}^{\oplus\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}},
 \qquad
 N=\bigoplus_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=-1}
       \mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}^{\oplus\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[An integral pure Weil character]
\label{prop:principal-graph-character}
The two sums in \eqref{eq:principal-PN} each have total
multiplicity $328$, and their entire character difference is
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-graph-weil}
 \operatorname{ch}(P)-\operatorname{ch}(N)
 =8\left(\prod_{j=1}^4a_j+\prod_{j=1}^4\bar a_j\right)=:w\ne0.
\end{equation}
This is a rational pure Weil class for the principal Gaussian
$U^4$ action.  Every $e\in\operatorname{Ext}^2(P,N)$ gives a
gluable perfect object \eqref{eq:postnikov-triangle} with
$\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal E_e)=w$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Each support is an abelian subvariety with trivial normal bundle,
so its structure sheaf has entire character $[B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}]$.
Using $2c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=
\prod_j\alpha_j+\prod_j\bar\alpha_j$ and
\eqref{eq:principal-four-divisors}, the signed sum factors as
\[
 \frac12\left(
  \prod_{j=1}^4\sum_{\alpha_j}\alpha_js_{\alpha_j}[C_{\alpha_j}]
  +\prod_{j=1}^4\sum_{\alpha_j}\bar\alpha_js_{\alpha_j}[C_{\alpha_j}]
 \right)
 =8\left(\prod_j\bar a_j+\prod_ja_j\right).
\]
The two nonzero decomposable products span the two complex Weil
determinant lines; their sum is rational by its integral cycle
expression.  It is nonzero because the determinant lines are
distinct.

An active index has an even number of diagonal-curve factors.
There are $16$ active supports with no diagonal factors, $96$ with
two, and $16$ with four, of multiplicities $16,4,1$, respectively.
Each list has equally many positive and negative coefficients.
Thus each sign has multiplicity
$8\cdot16+48\cdot4+8=328$.
Distinct supports of the same dimension admit no nonzero maps
between their structure sheaves.  Lemma~\ref{lem:postnikov-ext-bound}
therefore gives gluability.  Perfection follows from smoothness of
$A$, and additivity in the triangle gives the character assertion.
\end{proof}

The support intersections are divisorial whenever exactly one
curve factor changes.  Thus the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-support} does not hold for this network.
For the product principal polarization, the two effective cycles
in \eqref{eq:principal-graph-weil} each have degree $24576$:
an individual weighted support has degree
$\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}\,4!\,2^{\#\{j:\alpha_j=\pm i\}}
=384$, and there are $64$ per sign.
This is a cycle on the fixed split CM seed; no bounded-degree
transport to varying relative-Weyl CM points is asserted.

\begin{remark}[A degree-$24$ detector on the fixed seed]
\label{rem:principal-seed-small-detector}
The degree $24576$ per sign is the cost of the displayed pure
integral representative, not a minimal detector degree.
The single support
$B_{(1,1,1,1)}=\{x_1=\cdots=x_4=0\}\simeq T_y^4$ has degree
$4!=24$.  Since
\[
 \theta_x=\frac H2+\frac{a+\bar a}{4}
\]
and $H$ has one factor in each Gaussian eigenspace, the Weil
projection of its class is
\[
 \operatorname{pr}_{W_K}[B_{(1,1,1,1)}]
 =\frac{\prod_ja_j+\prod_j\bar a_j}{256}
 =\frac{w}{2048}\ne0.
\]
Thus $\delta_W(A,L)\leq24$ on this particular principal seed;
no exact minimum is asserted.  The seed is decomposed and is not
a full relative-Weyl CM point.  This calculation supplies no
bounded detector on a varying family or on a high-discriminant
relative-Weyl point.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The complete Ext calculation}

On $S$, two distinct curves from the list meet transversely.
Their intersection number $d(\alpha,\beta)$ is one, except that
\[
 d(i,-i)=d(-i,i)=4.
\]
The Cartier divisor resolution
$0\to\mathcal O_S(-C_\alpha)\to\mathcal O_S
\to\mathcal O_{C_\alpha}\to0$, after applying
$\mathcal Hom(-,\mathcal O_{C_\beta})$, is a two-term complex
whose first map is injective and whose cokernel is a line bundle
on the finite transverse intersection.  Therefore the only
nonzero cross-Ext group is in degree one, of dimension
$d(\alpha,\beta)$.  For equal curves the self-Ext dimensions are
$1,2,1$, with one generator from $H^1(\mathcal O_C)$ and one
from the trivial normal bundle.

If two indices $\boldsymbol\alpha,\boldsymbol\beta$ differ in
$d$ coordinates, K\"unneth consequently gives
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-cross-ext}
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^k
  (\mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}},
   \mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\beta}})
 =\left(\prod_{j:\alpha_j\ne\beta_j}d(\alpha_j,\beta_j)\right)
    \binom{2(4-d)}{k-d},
\end{equation}
where the binomial coefficient is zero outside its ordinary
range.  Multiplicities in \eqref{eq:principal-PN} multiply this
number by $\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}\mu_{\boldsymbol\beta}$.

Here is a compact exact certificate for all the needed sums.
Work in $\mathbb Z[X,Y]/(X^4-1,Y^4-1)$ and put
\[
 \begin{split}
 S(X)&=2+X+2X^2+X^3,\\
 H(X,Y)&=4+XY+4X^2Y^2+X^3Y^3,\\
 T(X,Y)&=S(X)S(Y)-H(X,Y)+3(XY^3+X^3Y).
 \end{split}
\]
The coefficient of $X^0Y^2Z^d$ in $(H+ZT)^4$ is the sum of
the multiplicity-weighted intersection factors in
\eqref{eq:principal-cross-ext}, over pairs from $P$ to $N$
differing in $d$ coordinates.  Direct expansion gives
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-ext-count-polynomial}
 [X^0Y^2](H+ZT)^4
 =16000Z+19200Z^2+102400Z^3+57344Z^4.
\end{equation}
The four terms respectively contribute the following numbers:
\[
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r}
 d&\text{weighted intersection sum}
   &\operatorname{Ext}^2(P,N)&\operatorname{Ext}^3(P,N)\\ \hline
 1&16000&96000&240000\\
 2&19200&19200&76800\\
 3&102400&0&102400\\
 4&57344&0&0\\ \hline
 \text{total}&&115200&419200
\end{array}
\]
This follows either by substituting
$Z^d(1+Z)^{2(4-d)}$ for each $Z^d$ in
\eqref{eq:principal-ext-count-polynomial}, or directly from
\eqref{eq:principal-cross-ext}.

Two distinct supports of the same coefficient sign differ in
at least two coordinates.  Their first cross-Ext vanishes, so
\[
 \begin{split}
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^1(P,P)
 +\dim\operatorname{Ext}^1(N,N)
 &=8\sum_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}\ne0}\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}^2\\
 &=8\cdot\frac{10^4+6^4}{2}=45184.
 \end{split}
\]
We obtain a bound for every extension class, not just for a
generic choice.

\begin{theorem}[The ordinary obstruction for every Postnikov class]
\label{thm:principal-postnikov-ordinary}
For $P,N$ in \eqref{eq:principal-PN} and every integer $r\geq1$,
put $P_r=P^{\oplus r}$ and $N_r=N^{\oplus r}$.
The space $\operatorname{Ext}^2(P_r,N_r)$ has dimension $115200r^2$.
For every $e$ in this space, the Postnikov triangle
$N_r[1]\to\mathcal E_{r,e}\to P_r\xrightarrow eN_r[2]$
defines a gluable perfect object with character $rw$, and
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-postnikov-bound}
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_{r,e},\mathcal E_{r,e})
 \geq(419200-45184)r^2=374016r^2>56.
\end{equation}
In particular no member is an ordinary strongly semiregular
pure-Weil representative.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
All cross-Hom vanishings persist under common multiplicity.
The preceding exact Ext counts scale by $r^2$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:postnikov-ext-bound} therefore proves gluability and
the bound; smoothness gives perfection, and the triangle gives
the character $rw$.  Apply the pure-Weil dimension
criterion in \cite[Theorem~\compref{thm:pure-weil-ext}]{CompanionA}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Explicit indecomposable members and finite equivariance}

\begin{proposition}[A nonempty open indecomposable locus]
\label{prop:principal-postnikov-indecomposable}
There is an explicit choice of $e$ for which the endomorphisms of
$\mathcal E_e$ induce only common scalar endomorphisms on $P$ and
$N$.  Such choices form a nonempty Zariski open subset of
$\operatorname{Ext}^2(P,N)$.  Every corresponding
$\mathcal E_e$ is indecomposable.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Partition the $128$ active indices according to which coordinates
use a diagonal curve, rather than an axis.  There are eight
patterns, since the number of diagonal coordinates is even.
Within each pattern the sixteen indices form a four-dimensional
sign cube, whose edges change $\alpha_j$ to $-\alpha_j$.
Every edge joins a positive vertex to a negative vertex, and all
vertices in the pattern have the same multiplicity $\mu$.

Choose a spanning tree in each sign cube.  For every tree edge,
choose a nonzero underlying cross-Ext class of degree two and
make its coefficient in $e$ the identity matrix on
$\mathbb C^\mu$.  On one tree edge choose two more independent
underlying classes and give them the matrix coefficients
\[
 D_\mu=\operatorname{diag}(1,\ldots,\mu),\qquad
 Q_\mu(e_j)=e_{j+1\pmod\mu}.
\]
There are at least six underlying degree-two classes on every
such edge, by \eqref{eq:principal-cross-ext}, so these choices
are possible.  For $\mu=1$ the identity coefficient alone
suffices.  Compatibility with the identity coefficients forces
all cohomology endomorphism matrices in one sign cube to agree.
The commutant of $D_\mu$ and $Q_\mu$ consists of scalars, so they
are common scalars on that cube.

The graph of the eight patterns, joining two patterns when two
coordinates change between axis and diagonal, is connected.
Choose a spanning tree in this graph.  For each of its edges,
one can choose a positive index in the first pattern and a
negative index in the second that differ in precisely those two
coordinates: increase both exponents in $\alpha_j=i^{a_j}$ by
one.  Their underlying cross-Ext of degree two is nonzero.
Give this entry of $e$ any nonzero rectangular matrix between
the two multiplicity spaces.  Compatibility now forces the
scalars of the two patterns to agree.  The resulting $e$ has
only common scalar cohomology endomorphisms.

More explicitly, the cohomology endomorphism pairs lie in
\[
 \{(u_P,u_N):e\,u_P=u_N[2]e\}.
\]
The preceding construction makes this space one-dimensional.
Its dimension is the kernel dimension of a matrix depending
linearly on $e$, so its minimum value one holds on a nonempty
Zariski open set.

The kernel of the map from
$\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E_e)$ to its cohomology
endomorphisms is $\operatorname{Ext}^1(P,N)$: its elements have
the form $bfa$ with $f:P\to N[1]$.  This also follows directly
by applying the two long exact sequences of
\eqref{eq:postnikov-triangle}; the negative Hom groups and
$\operatorname{Hom}(P,N)$ vanish.  Its square is zero because
$ab=0$.  The quotient is the scalar field, so
$\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E_e)$ is a local algebra.
There is no nontrivial idempotent, proving indecomposability.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Allowed finite actions do not rescue an indecomposable member]
\label{thm:principal-postnikov-equivariant}
If $\mathcal E_e$ is indecomposable, then for every coherent finite
group action through functors $t_x^*(-)\otimes L$, with
$L\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(A)$, the conjugation action on
self-Ext is trivial.  In particular,
\[
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_e,\mathcal E_e)^G
 =\dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_e,\mathcal E_e)
 \geq374016>56.
\]
Thus none of the indecomposable members in
Proposition~\ref{prop:principal-postnikov-indecomposable} is an
equivariant strongly semiregular pure-Weil representative.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation and tensoring by a line bundle are $t$-exact.
An isomorphism $t_x^*\mathcal E_e\otimes L\simeq\mathcal E_e$
therefore preserves the support directions in each cohomology
sheaf.  Translations preserve a direction and the $128$ directions
are distinct, so every $B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}$ is preserved
individually.  Hence
\[
 x\in\bigcap_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}\ne0}B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}.
\]
The sixteen products consisting entirely of axes already have
zero intersection.  Thus $x=0$.

For every active support we also have
$L|_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}^{\oplus\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}
\simeq\mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}^{\oplus\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}$.
A nontrivial degree-zero line bundle on an abelian variety has no
nonzero global section, so each restriction is trivial.
Restriction to all the axis products detects every factor of
$\operatorname{Pic}^0(T^8)$, and consequently $L$ is trivial.

Every allowed functor in the action is therefore isomorphic to
the identity.  After choosing these isomorphisms, its coherent
linearizations form a possibly projective finite action on
$\mathcal E_e$; the composition constants are scalars.
The bounded derived category of a smooth projective variety is
Hom-finite and idempotent complete.  The endomorphism algebra of
an indecomposable object is consequently a finite-dimensional
local $\mathbb C$-algebra, with residue field $\mathbb C$.
If a unit in such an algebra has a positive power equal to a
scalar, the unit itself is scalar: after removing its scalar
part it is unipotent, and a nontrivial unipotent element has no
finite order in characteristic zero.  Every group element in the
coherent action has a positive power that is scalar.  Thus all
linearizations are scalar and conjugation on every self-Ext
group is trivial.  Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-ordinary}.
\end{proof}

The preceding numerical equality uses indecomposability.  A
decomposable object can have a nontrivial finite representation
on its multiplicities, so its invariant Ext space need not be
its entire Ext space.  Nevertheless the equality required for
strong semiregularity is impossible for this entire network.

\begin{lemma}[The simultaneous mixed-quadratic annihilator]
\label{lem:mixed-quadratic-joint-annihilator}
Let $H=H_1\oplus H_2$ with $\dim H_1=\dim H_2=g$.  Then
\[
 \{\eta\in\textstyle\bigwedge^g H:
       (u\wedge v)\wedge\eta=0
       \text{ for all }u\in H_1,\ v\in H_2\}
 =\bigwedge^gH_1\oplus\bigwedge^gH_2.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Decompose $\eta$ into bidegrees $(p,g-p)$ for $H_1,H_2$.
Multiplication by a mixed quadratic increases each bidegree by
one, so the conditions hold separately on every component.
For a vector space $V$ of dimension $g$ and $p<g$, the map
\[
 \bigwedge^p V\longrightarrow
 \operatorname{Hom}(V,\bigwedge^{p+1}V),
 \quad z\longmapsto(v\longmapsto v\wedge z)
\]
is injective.  For example, a nonzero basis coefficient of $z$
can be detected by wedging with a basis vector missing from
that coefficient.  For $0<p<g$, the tensor product of the two
injective maps, in degrees $p$ and $g-p$, is injective.
Therefore the corresponding component of $\eta$ vanishes.
The two endpoint components are killed by every mixed quadratic,
which proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Every finite action fails on the Postnikov network]
\label{thm:principal-postnikov-all-actions}
For every $r\geq1$, every $e\in\operatorname{Ext}^2(P_r,N_r)$ and every coherent
finite translation--Picard action on $\mathcal E_{r,e}$,
\[
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_{r,e},\mathcal E_{r,e})^G>56.
\]
This includes finite abstract actions with ineffective kernel.
In particular no member of this Postnikov family is strongly
$G$-semiregular for any allowed action.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The support and restriction argument in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-equivariant} did not use
indecomposability or the common multiplicity: every translation--Picard functor stabilizing
$\mathcal E_{r,e}$ is isomorphic to the identity.  After choosing
such isomorphisms, a coherent finite action is a projective
finite action on the object.  The corresponding twisted group
algebra over $\mathbb C$ is semisimple.  Since the category of
perfect complexes is idempotent complete, its matrix idempotents
give an isotypical decomposition
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-isotypical}
 \mathcal E_{r,e}\simeq\bigoplus_\rho V_\rho\otimes F_\rho,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_{r,e},\mathcal E_{r,e})^G
 \simeq\bigoplus_\rho\operatorname{Ext}^2(F_\rho,F_\rho),
\end{equation}
where the $V_\rho$ are the pairwise inequivalent irreducible
projective representations and the $F_\rho$ are nonzero
multiplicity objects.  The second formula is Schur's lemma,
applied to conjugation on each matrix of Ext groups.

Each $F_\rho$ is a direct summand of $\mathcal E_{r,e}$, so it has
cohomology only in degrees $-1,0$.  Distinct supports in $P$
or $N$ have no nonzero degree-zero maps, and the endomorphism
algebra of $\mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}$ is $\mathbb C$.
Its two cohomology sheaves are therefore
\[
 H^0(F_\rho)=\bigoplus_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=1}
       \mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}^{\oplus p_{\rho\alpha}},
 \qquad
 H^{-1}(F_\rho)=\bigoplus_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=-1}
       \mathcal O_{B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}^{\oplus n_{\rho\alpha}}
\]
for nonnegative integers $p_{\rho\alpha},n_{\rho\alpha}$.
At least one is positive.  Put
$W=\prod_ja_j+\prod_j\bar a_j$, so $w=8W$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:principal-four-divisors} gives the exact formula
\[
 \operatorname{pr}_{W_K}[B_{\boldsymbol\alpha}]
       =\frac{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}{16\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}W.
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-isotypical-positive}
 \operatorname{pr}_{W_K}\operatorname{ch}(F_\rho)
 =\frac1{16}\left(
    \sum_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=1}
                \frac{p_{\rho\alpha}}{\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}
   +\sum_{c_{\boldsymbol\alpha}=-1}
                \frac{n_{\rho\alpha}}{\mu_{\boldsymbol\alpha}}
                  \right)W\ne0.
\end{equation}
In particular no nonzero isotypical multiplicity object has
zero Chern character.

Suppose for contradiction that the invariant second self-Ext
dimension is $56$.  Use the Hochschild action, the trace
identity, and the holomorphic-volume identification from the
proof of \cite[Theorem~\compref{thm:pure-weil-ext}]{CompanionA}.
In this identification $HH_0(A)$ is $\bigwedge^8 H$, where
$H=H_1\oplus H_2$ and $\dim H_i=8$, and the character $rw$ becomes a sum of
nonzero volume forms on $H_1,H_2$.
The composite
\[
 HH^2(A)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{\mathcal E_{r,e}}}
 \operatorname{Ext}^2(\mathcal E_{r,e},\mathcal E_{r,e})^G
 \xrightarrow{\sigma_{\mathcal E_{r,e}}}HH_{-2}(A)
\]
is multiplication by this character and has rank $56$.
Thus $\sigma_{\mathcal E_{r,e}}$ is injective on the invariant
space.  Every mixed quadratic $x\in H_1\wedge H_2$ annihilates
$w$, so the trace identity and this injectivity imply
$\operatorname{ev}_{\mathcal E_{r,e}}(x)=0$.
Naturality of Hochschild evaluation under direct summands
in \eqref{eq:principal-isotypical} gives
$\operatorname{ev}_{F_\rho}(x)=0$ for every $\rho$.
Applying the trace identity to each $F_\rho$ now gives
$x\cdot\operatorname{ch}(F_\rho)=0$ for every such $x$.

Under the same holomorphic-volume identification every Chern
character lies in $\bigwedge^8H$; this is the Hochschild
identification, not an identification of ordinary cohomological
degrees.  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixed-quadratic-joint-annihilator}
therefore places $\operatorname{ch}(F_\rho)$ in the two Weil
determinant lines.  It is rational and nonzero by
\eqref{eq:principal-isotypical-positive}, hence is a nonzero
pure Weil character.  The rank part of the same trace identity
gives
\[
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2(F_\rho,F_\rho)\geq56
 \quad\text{for every }\rho.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:principal-isotypical}, the assumed
dimension $56$ permits only one isotype.  Thus
$\mathcal E_{r,e}\simeq V\otimes F$.
For every support its cohomological multiplicity is divisible
by $d=\dim V$.  An all-diagonal support has multiplicity $r$,
so $d$ divides $r$.  Set $s=r/d\geq1$.  Comparing every support
multiplicity gives $H^0(F)=P_s$ and $H^{-1}(F)=N_s$.
There is no other cohomology, so the canonical truncation triangle
identifies $F$ with a Postnikov object in the same family at scale
$s$.  Its ordinary second self-Ext dimension is at least
$374016s^2>56$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-ordinary}.
This contradicts the isotypical invariant formula.

The trace-rank argument always gives invariant dimension at
least $56$, and equality has been excluded.  This proves the
strict inequality claimed.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the strengthened exclusion]
The argument does not claim the invariant lower bound $374016$
for decomposable members.  Nor does it give an unconditional
bound $112$: purity of the individual $F_\rho$ was deduced only
under the contradictory equality assumption.  The conclusion
excludes the precise dimension $56$ for this explicit family
of Postnikov extensions.  It does not exclude arbitrary
perfect objects.  Linear Gaussian automorphisms that permute
support directions are also outside the translation--Picard
criterion used here.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Translations and compatible isogeny descent}

\begin{corollary}[Global translations and degree-zero twists]
\label{cor:principal-postnikov-translated}
The same exclusion holds for
$t_a^*\mathcal E_{r,e}\otimes M$, for every $a\in A$ and
$M\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(A)$, in particular for every global
torsion translate of the network.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Translation and tensoring by $M$ preserve the Chern character
and are exact autoequivalences.  Conjugating a coherent finite
translation--Picard action by this autoequivalence is again an
action of that type; the canonical composition isomorphisms
retain its coherence.  This conjugation identifies the
invariant self-Ext spaces.  Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-all-actions}.
\end{proof}

The pullback assertion below specifies its descent hypothesis.
An arbitrary additional action on a cover is not being declared
compatible with a chosen descent datum.

\begin{proposition}[Isogeny pullbacks with compatible descent]
\label{prop:principal-postnikov-isogeny-descent}
Let $f:A'\to A$ be a Gaussian-linear isogeny of complex abelian
eightfolds and let $K=\ker f$.  Let $E_0$ be an object of
Theorem~\ref{thm:principal-postnikov-all-actions} or
Corollary~\ref{cor:principal-postnikov-translated}, and give
$E'=f^*E_0$ its canonical coherent deck action of $K$.
Suppose a finite group $\widetilde G$ acts coherently on $E'$
through translation--Picard functors, with the following
properties:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item $K$ is a normal subgroup of $\widetilde G$ and the
       restricted action is the specified deck action;
 \item under finite \'etale descent along $f$, the quotient
       $H=\widetilde G/K$ induces a coherent
       translation--Picard action on the descended object $E_0$.
\end{enumerate}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:principal-isogeny-invariant-ext}
 \operatorname{Ext}^2_{A'}(E',E')^{\widetilde G}
    \simeq\operatorname{Ext}^2_A(E_0,E_0)^H,
 \qquad
 \dim\operatorname{Ext}^2_{A'}(E',E')^{\widetilde G}>56.
\end{equation}
The same strict lower conclusion holds for every subgroup of
such a $\widetilde G$.  With only the canonical deck action,
the invariant dimension is at least $374016r^2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In characteristic zero an isogeny is finite \'etale, and $f$ is
a torsor for the finite translation group $K$.
For a perfect object the projection formula gives
\[
 R\operatorname{Hom}_{A'}(f^*E_0,f^*E_0)
 \simeq R\Gamma\bigl(A,E_0^\vee\otimes^{\mathbf L}E_0
                         \otimes f_*\mathcal O_{A'}\bigr).
\]
The canonical deck action satisfies
$(f_*\mathcal O_{A'})^K=\mathcal O_A$.  Since averaging makes
finite-group invariants exact over $\mathbb C$, taking
invariants and cohomology gives
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^j_{A'}(f^*E_0,f^*E_0)^K
       \simeq\operatorname{Ext}^j_A(E_0,E_0)
\]
for every $j$.  The compatibility hypothesis identifies the
remaining quotient action with the prescribed $H$-action on
the right.  Taking its invariants proves
\eqref{eq:principal-isogeny-invariant-ext}, and the scaled
theorem and its translate/twist corollary give the strict
inequality.  Invariants under a subgroup contain those under
$\widetilde G$, so they satisfy the same strict lower bound.
For $\widetilde G=K$ the displayed descent isomorphism identifies
the invariant space with ordinary self-Ext on $A$, yielding
the numerical bound $374016r^2$.

Gaussian linearity identifies the rational Weil planes under
$f^*$, so $\operatorname{ch}(E')=r f^*w$ is again a nonzero
pure Weil character.  The strong dimension criterion therefore
has the same value $56$ on the cover.
\end{proof}

For clarity, compatible cover translations and twists are
included in the proposition.  If $\tau\in A'$ is torsion
(or indeed arbitrary) and $M\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(A)$, then
\[
 t_\tau^*f^*\mathcal E_{r,e}\otimes f^*M
   \simeq f^*\bigl(t_{f(\tau)}^*\mathcal E_{r,e}\otimes M\bigr).
\]
With the indicated descent datum and compatible finite action,
apply the proposition to the object inside parentheses.
Likewise a given finite action on a pullback is excluded if it
extends, together with the canonical deck action, to a finite
group satisfying the two hypotheses above.

These assertions concern common integral scalings of the entire
fixed network and global translations, twists and compatible
isogeny pullbacks.  They do not address unequal multiplicities,
independent translations of individual supports, arbitrary
actions incompatible with the chosen descent, or other perfect
objects.  An isogeny of this split seed also preserves its
endomorphism algebra up to isogeny; it does not make the seed a
full relative-Weyl CM point.


\section{What remains open}
\label{sec:open}
Theorem~\compref{thm:global} is a criterion for the Hodge conjecture. It proves neither the conjecture nor a reduction to a finite computation. The outstanding condition is a degree bound uniform in the CM fibre.

For Weil classes the principal unresolved question is a uniform bound for detectors in every connected split family, in every dimension. Theorem~\compref{thm:principal-quotient} reduces the integral lattice and polarization choices to the single principal hyperbolic family $U^n$ for each imaginary quadratic field $K$ and dimension $2n$, with an explicit loss in degree. Stabilization would then give algebraicity of Weil classes for every imaginary quadratic field and every determinant. It would not establish the Hodge conjecture for all abelian varieties. The first target beyond the known constructions is a split Weil eightfold family over a field other than the Eisenstein field. Theorems asserting only the existence of cycles at each CM point do not provide the required bound.
